```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0001
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 227a-227b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 227a-227b
  start_marker: 227a
  end_marker: 227b
  start_char: 8
  end_char: 729
  text_sha256: d04078312584f80a864009f500dab71db728268e0120b7793146589edf667aab
greek_terms:
  - ποῖ
  - πόθεν
  - ὦ φίλε Φαῖδρε, ποῖ δὴ καὶ πόθεν;
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The dialogue opens with Socrates asking Phaedrus two questions: where he is going and where he is coming from. These twin questions immediately establish the dramatic situation: Phaedrus's recent location and his present destination, which will turn out to frame the entire conversation."
textual_basis: "At 227a, Socrates's first line is a direct interrogative pairing using the terms for whither and whence. Phaedrus answers both in order: he is coming from Lysias son of Cephalus and going for a walk outside the city wall."
limits: This observation notes the structural function of the opening exchange; it does not claim that the dual-direction question formula is unique to the Phaedrus or carries hidden meaning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "The opening exchange clusters proper names around Lysias: Lysias son of Cephalus, Acumenus as a mutual associate whose walking advice Phaedrus follows, Epicrates as host, and the Morychian house near the Olympieum."
textual_basis: At 227a the passage names Lysias, Cephalus, and Acumenus; at 227b it adds Epicrates and the Morychian house.
limits: Morychia is recorded as a house designation, not as a directly introduced person.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0003
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 227a-227b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 227a-227b
  start_marker: 227a
  end_marker: 227b
  start_char: 8
  end_char: 729
  text_sha256: d04078312584f80a864009f500dab71db728268e0120b7793146589edf667aab
greek_terms:
  - διατριβή
  - δῆλον ὅτι
  - τῶν λόγων
  - εἱστία
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asks Phaedrus what their pastime was at Lysias's house, then immediately supplies the answer himself with a hedging phrase meaning 'or is it not clear that,' proposing that Lysias feasted them on speeches. The question is not genuinely open; Socrates already infers that speeches were the substance of the gathering.
textual_basis: At 227b Socrates asks what the pastime was, then adds 'or is it not clear that Lysias feasted you on speeches?' The 'or is it not clear that' phrase marks the answer as obvious, and the verb for 'feasted' casts the speech-making in convivial terms.
limits: This observation identifies a pattern of feigned or staged questioning, not a claim about Socrates's hidden knowledge. The feasting metaphor could belong to the craft_analogy family but is treated here as part of the questioning dynamic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0004
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 227c-227d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 227c-227d
  start_marker: 227c
  end_marker: 227d
  start_char: 729
  end_char: 1446
  text_sha256: c77bb2b96412d60fef35fcf93c99b83e6933fc679219c235dc776dff9b0dd0aa
greek_terms:
  - ἐρωτικός
  - ἐραστοῦ
  - ἐρῶντι
  - μὴ ἐρῶντι
english_gloss: erotic discourse, lover, one who loves, non-lover
observation: |
  Before Socrates hears the speech itself, Phaedrus supplies a compressed summary of Lysias's written logos: it is an erotic discourse in which someone attempts to persuade a beautiful youth, but the conceit is that the speaker is a non-lover rather than a lover, arguing that favors should be granted to the non-lover rather than to the lover.
textual_basis: |
  Phaedrus describes the logos as erotic (erotikos), identifies the speaker as a would-be persuader of a beautiful youth, and states the central paradox: the argument is that one should grant favors to the non-lover (me eronti) rather than to the lover (eronti). The summary occupies 227c and establishes the rhetorical set-up before the speech is read aloud.
limits: |
  This is Phaedrus's summary, not the speech itself. The observation does not assess whether the summary is accurate or complete, nor does it evaluate the argument's content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0005
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 227c-227d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 227c-227d
  start_marker: 227c
  end_marker: 227d
  start_char: 729
  end_char: 1446
  text_sha256: c77bb2b96412d60fef35fcf93c99b83e6933fc679219c235dc776dff9b0dd0aa
greek_terms:
  - κεκόμψευται
english_gloss: attempting, has been embellished
observation: |
  Socrates responds to Phaedrus's summary with an exclamation of mock-praise ("O noble one!") and a wish that Lysias had also written that one should grant favors to a poor man rather than a rich one, and to an older man rather than a younger one — traits Socrates attributes to himself — calling such logoi urbane and beneficial to the people.
textual_basis: |
  At 227c, Socrates exclaims "O generous/noble one!" and follows with a counterfactual wish that Lysias had written on behalf of the poor and old rather than the non-lover. He adds that such speeches would be "urbane and demotic-beneficial" (asteioi kai demopheleis), in a tone that applies praise to an argument Socrates has not yet heard. The irony is reinforced when he claims intense desire to hear the speech.
limits: |
  Irony is a pragmatic feature not provable from the text alone; the observation notes the incongruity between Socrates's extravagant praise and his not yet having heard the logos. It does not assert what Socrates secretly believes.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0006
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 227c-227d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 227c-227d
  start_marker: 227c
  end_marker: 227d
  start_char: 729
  end_char: 1446
  text_sha256: c77bb2b96412d60fef35fcf93c99b83e6933fc679219c235dc776dff9b0dd0aa
greek_terms:
  - βαδίζων
  - περίπατον
  - Μέγαράδε
  - ἀπολειφθῶ
english_gloss: walking, walk, to Megara, I would not be left behind
observation: |
  Socrates declares his eagerness to hear the speech by claiming that he would not be left behind even if Phaedrus were to walk to Megara and back, touching the wall according to Herodicus's method and turning back. This embeds their dramatic location — walking outside the walls of Athens — into Socrates's avowal of intense desire to hear the logos.
textual_basis: |
  At 227c-227d, Socrates says he has so desired to hear the speech that even if Phaedrus were to walk to Megara and back, touching the wall as Herodicus recommends, Socrates would not be left behind (ouk me sou apoleiphtho). The humorous hyperbole locates the two speakers as walkers and establishes the dialogue's outdoor, peripatetic setting.
limits: |
  The observation does not resolve the identity of Herodicus or the precise method described. It notes only that the dramatic setting is established through Socrates's speech rather than through narrative description.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates conditionally disclaims knowledge of Phaedrus, then immediately claims confident knowledge and reconstructs Phaedrus's speech-book routine.
textual_basis: At 228a-228b the exchange moves from a conditional self-forgetting formula to a confident reconstruction of repeated hearing, taking the book, and rehearsing the speech.
limits: This records the formal irony marker only, not a hidden-motive claim.

```

```yaml
textual_basis: The segment 228b describes Phaedrus going outside the wall to rehearse, encountering Socrates who is sick with desire to hear speeches, and rejoicing because he would have a fellow Corybantic reveler. This establishes the dramatic occasion for the dialogue.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 228b Socrates says Phaedrus repeatedly had Lysias speak, then took the book and reviewed what he most desired; Socrates adds that Phaedrus was going outside the wall to practice, thinking he knew the speech unless it was very long.
limits: This observation is limited to the book-and-memory frame in this span. The later physical reveal of the speech under Phaedrus' cloak is recorded separately.
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates characterizes Phaedrus, not Lysias, as playing coy when asked to speak, though he would have spoken by force if no willing listener appeared.
textual_basis: At 228c, after the speech-lover asks him to speak, the subject plays coy and is then described as ready to speak by force.
limits: This is Socrates's teasing reconstruction of Phaedrus's behavior, not direct evidence of Phaedrus's intention.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - τὴν μέντοι διάνοιαν σχεδὸν ἁπάντων
  - ῥήματα οὐκ ἐξέμαθον
english_gloss: the thought of nearly all, I did not learn the words by heart
observation: Phaedrus admits he has not memorized the exact wording of Lysias's speech, only the general thought. He will recount the argument in summary, point by point from the beginning.
textual_basis: At 228d Phaedrus says he has not learned the wording by heart but only the thought of nearly all of it, and proposes to go through the main points in order. This is an explicit reliability hedge on the embedded speech.
limits: This hedge is stated but not tested within the segment. The observation does not assess whether Phaedrus's summary is accurate or whether the hedge is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - δείξας γε πρῶτον
  - τί ἄρα ἐν τῇ ἀριστερᾷ ἔχεις ὑπὸ τῷ ἱματίῳ
  - τοπάζω γάρ σε ἔχειν τὸν λόγον αὐτόν
english_gloss: first show what you have in your left hand under the cloak; I guess you have the speech itself
observation: Just as Phaedrus begins his oral summary, Socrates interrupts him to ask what he is holding under his cloak with his left hand, guessing it is the actual written speech.
textual_basis: At 228d Socrates tells Phaedrus to first show what he has in his left hand under his cloak, guessing that Phaedrus has the speech itself. This reveals that a physical written text is present, undercutting the need for Phaedrus's memory-hedged summary.
limits: The observation registers the interruption and the physical-text reveal but does not interpret whether Socrates's guess is a genuine surmise or whether Phaedrus was dissembling about needing to summarize from memory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0013
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 229d-229e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 229d-229e
  start_marker: 229d
  end_marker: 229e
  start_char: 4503
  end_char: 5234
  text_sha256: c116096d5dbc325a9d6866e3f6a2ceee1af229addeffcb55cfdce177704b09af
greek_terms:
  - τὸ Δελφικὸν γράμμα
  - γνῶναι ἐμαυτόν
english_gloss: the Delphic inscription, know myself
observation: Socrates says he has no leisure for rationalizing myths because he cannot yet know himself according to the Delphic inscription, and it appears laughable to him to examine other things while still ignorant of himself.
textual_basis: At 229e, Socrates says he lacks leisure for myth-rationalization, giving as the cause (to de aition) his inability to fulfill the Delphic command to know himself. This self-knowledge failure is presented as the explicit reason for refusing myth-interpretation, making it a knowledge-disavowal that serves as an argumentative move.
limits: This observation records the disavowal as a textual move, not a claim about Socrates' actual epistemic state or hidden knowledge. The passage does not establish whether the self-ignorance claim is sincere or ironic.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0014
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 229d-229e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 229d-229e
  start_marker: 229d
  end_marker: 229e
  start_char: 4503
  end_char: 5234
  text_sha256: c116096d5dbc325a9d6866e3f6a2ceee1af229addeffcb55cfdce177704b09af
greek_terms:
  - τῶν Ἱπποκενταύρων εἶδος
  - τῆς Χιμαίρας
  - Γοργόνων
  - Πηγάσων
english_gloss: Hippocentaurs, Chimera, Gorgons, Pegasus
observation: "Socrates enumerates a list of mythical creatures whose forms a rationalizer would have to correct: Hippocentaurs, Chimera, Gorgons, Pegasus, and other impossible monstrous natures. He frames this as an onerous labor requiring abundant leisure."
textual_basis: From 229d to 229e, Socrates lists a series of mythical beings (Hippocentaurs, Chimera, a crowd of Gorgons and Pegasus, and other impossible monstrosities) that someone who rationalizes myths must work through. The catalog effect marks myth as a bounded domain distinct from rational inquiry.
limits: This observation notes the catalog as a myth-demarcation technique. It does not claim that Socrates rejects myth outright; the passage only rejects allegorical rationalization of myth, not myth itself.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0015
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 229d-229e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 229d-229e
  start_marker: 229d
  end_marker: 229e
  start_char: 4503
  end_char: 5234
  text_sha256: c116096d5dbc325a9d6866e3f6a2ceee1af229addeffcb55cfdce177704b09af
greek_terms:
  - χαρίεντα
  - δεινοῦ
  - ἐπιπόνου
  - εὐτυχοῦς
  - ἀγροίκῳ τινὶ σοφίᾳ
english_gloss: clever, clever-clever, laborious, not very fortunate, rustic wisdom
observation: Socrates characterizes myth-rationalizers as clever but also clever-clever (deinou), laborious (epiponou), and not very fortunate, and describes their method as a kind of rustic wisdom (agroikoi tini sophiai). This pejorative characterization sets the terms for what kind of intellectual labor is worth undertaking.
textual_basis: At 229d-e, Socrates says myth-rationalization is charming (charienta) but belongs to a man who is clever-clever, laborious, and unlucky. He later calls the rationalizing approach 'rustic wisdom.' The accumulation of pejorative terms functions as a dismissal that redirects the conversational agenda.
limits: This observation records the characterization of a rival intellectual method, not a claim that Socrates' own method is superior. The passage does not specify what Socrates' alternative method would be beyond the self-knowledge turn at 229e.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 229c Phaedrus asks Socrates, using the term that the text renders as myth-narrative, whether he is persuaded that this story is true. The question explicitly frames the myth as a narrative whose truth-status is at issue.
limits: This observation records only that the question is posed. It does not track Socrates' answer, which begins later in 229c and continues beyond this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0017
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 229b-229c

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  start_marker: 229b

  text_sha256: 0372d7a55b566deb7fd19068b05f9ce15ca7528711043b5eb8805b69cb3fbb84
greek_terms:
  - ὥσπερ οἱ σοφοί
  - σοφιζόμενος
  - πνεῦμα Βορέου
  - Φαρμακείᾳ
english_gloss: like the wise men; rationalizing; breath/gust of Boreas; Pharmaceia
observation: "Socrates describes a rationalizing alternative to the Boreas myth: the girl was pushed by a gust of wind from nearby rocks and this event was later mythologized as an abduction by Boreas."
textual_basis: At 229c Socrates says that if he disbelieved the myth, he would, like the wise men, rationalize it. He describes a gust of wind pushing Oreithyia while she was playing with Pharmaceia on the nearby rocks, and that after dying this way she was said to have been snatched by Boreas. The passage sets up rationalizing explanation as a recognized alternative to myth-acceptance.
limits: Socrates presents this as a hypothetical he does not endorse; the observation records only that the rationalizing move is described, not that Socrates adopts it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The myth of Boreas abducting Oreithyia from the Ilissus river is invoked by name, and a local altar of Boreas is mentioned as a physical landmark.
textual_basis: At 229b Phaedrus asks whether this is the spot from which Boreas is said to have snatched Oreithyia from the Ilissus. At 229c Socrates corrects the location and notes there is an altar of Boreas further downstream. The named figures are drawn from Greek myth and cult.
limits: The observation notes the naming of mythic figures and a cult site; it does not interpret the myth's philosophical function or Socrates' later treatment of it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0019
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 230a-230b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 230a-230b
  start_marker: 230a
  end_marker: 230b
  start_char: 5234
  end_char: 6043
  text_sha256: 0f95e75bfdbb1d2aa873a713b6b1363c9b8730d6d0744cf979e0555fe780a600
greek_terms:
  - Τυφῶνος πολυπλοκώτερον
  - ἡμερώτερόν τε καὶ ἁπλούστερον ζῷον
english_gloss: null
observation: "Socrates frames his self-examination as a test against two mythic types: whether he is a more complex and inflamed beast than Typhon, or a gentler and simpler creature sharing by nature in a divine and modest portion. The contrast sets a mythic ceiling and floor for his self-knowledge."
textual_basis: "Socrates says he has abandoned studying external things to examine himself, then poses the disjunctive in 230a: whether he turns out to be some beast more convoluted than Typhon and more flamed up, or a tamer and simpler creature, partaking by nature of some divine and un-puffed-up portion. The self-comparison to Typhon is a mythic reference used as a boundary for a possible real character."
limits: Does not assert that Socrates is either type; only records the self-questioning framework. Does not interpret Typhon as a cult figure or expand into the mythology.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0020
temporary_id: true
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 231b-231c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 231b-231c
  start_marker: 231b
  end_marker: 231c
  start_char: 7663
  end_char: 8463
  text_sha256: 981a1a89e5be0d7323a2eb3f5ef09e02d658e1fdf658082a16996c6c7f03f1f2
greek_terms:
  - ἡγοῦνται πάλαι τὴν ἀξίαν ἀποδεδωκέναι χάριν
  - τοῖς δὲ μὴ ἐρῶσιν
english_gloss: They consider they have long ago repaid the favor
observation: |
  The speaker attributes to non-lovers (tois de me erosin) a set of rhetorical advantages over lovers: the non-lover cannot excuse neglect of his own household by citing the beloved, cannot tally past toils as debts owed, and cannot blame quarrels with kin on the beloved. The argument frames these standard lover's complaints as so many evils (kakon) whose removal leaves the non-lover with nothing but eager service.
textual_basis: |
  At 231b-231c the speaker contrasts the lover, who claims to have already discharged any debt of gratitude (apodedokenai charin) to the beloved, with the non-lover, who is stripped of the excuses and grievances the lover routinely deploys. The enumeration of defects removed (perihairemenon tosouton kakon) constitutes a rhetorical inversion of the expected valuation of erotic devotion.
limits: |
  The observation records a rhetorical move within a set speech attributed to Lysias; it does not assert that this represents Plato's own view or that the argument is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0021
temporary_id: true
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 231b-231c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 231b-231c
  start_marker: 231b
  end_marker: 231c
  start_char: 7663
  end_char: 8463
  text_sha256: 981a1a89e5be0d7323a2eb3f5ef09e02d658e1fdf658082a16996c6c7f03f1f2
greek_terms:
  - ὅτι ὅσων ἂν ὕστερον ἐρασθῶσιν, ἐκείνους αὐτῶν περὶ πλείονος ποιήσονται
  - καὶ τούτους κακῶς ποιήσουσιν
english_gloss: Whoever they fall in love with later, those they will value more, and they will treat these badly
observation: |
  The speaker argues that if lovers claim to cherish their beloved above all and are willing to make enemies for their sake, then it is easy to see (rhaidion gnonai) that when they later fall in love with someone else they will value that new beloved more and, if the new beloved wishes, will mistreat the former one. The conclusion is presented as a forced inference from the lover's own stated commitments.
textual_basis: |
  At 231c the speaker introduces a conditional (ei ... axios) based on the lover's own claim of devotion, then draws the consequence (hoston an hysteron erasthosin, ekeinous auton peri pleionos poiesontai) that the lover will prefer the later beloved, and that the lover will even harm (kakos poiesousin) the earlier beloved if the new one approves. The argument is structured as a reductio of the lover's own position.
limits: |
  This observation notes the argumentative structure, not its validity. It does not claim that the speech expresses Plato's own view on erotic fidelity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates says 'you choose in whatever posture you think you will read most easily, and read' (230e). Phaedrus responds 'listen then' and begins the written speech, which opens in first-person (231a). The speech is embedded within the dramatic frame.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0023

  - τυγχάνω

observation: The embedded Lysian speech at 231a opens with a non-lover (ou erastes) addressing a beloved, arguing that the beloved should grant favors to a non-lover rather than to a lover. This sets up an erotic rivalry frame between lover and non-lover that will structure the subsequent speeches.
textual_basis: "The speech begins at 231a with the speaker declaring he is not a lover: 'hoti ouk erastes on sou tynchano' — because I happen not to be a lover of yours. The speech then immediately contrasts the behavior of lovers with non-lovers."
limits: This is the opening of the embedded Lysian speech; it does not yet establish Socrates' own position on the lover/non-lover question or the full argument of the speech. The rivalry frame is set up within the written text, not the dramatic frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0024
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 230e-231a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 7663
  text_sha256: 97f39377db1923f11de1e16d24c7de51f34e2681553521bc1101dc92971f66cf
greek_terms:
  - μεταμέλει
  - ἐπιθυμίας
  - παύσωνται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 231a the Lysian speech opens with a contrast: lovers later regret (metamelei) their good deeds once desire (epithymia) ceases, while non-lovers have no occasion for regret because they act voluntarily and deliberately rather than under compulsion. The non-lover is presented as the more reliable benefactor, inverting the expected ethical priority of the passionate lover."
textual_basis: "The speech states at 231a: 'lovers regret what good they have done once they cease from desire; but for non-lovers there is no time at which it is fitting to change their mind. For not under compulsion but willingly, deliberating as best they can about their own affairs, they do good according to their capacity.'"
limits: This observation records the maxim as stated within the Lysian speech. It does not evaluate the argument's validity or anticipate how Socrates will later treat it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0028
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - νοσεῖν
  - σωφρονεῖν
  - κακῶς φρονοῦσιν
  - οὐ δύνασθαι αὑτῶν κρατεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "In Lysias's speech, the lover is characterized in pathological terms: lovers admit they are sick rather than being of sound mind, know they think poorly, but cannot control themselves. This medicalized characterization is then used to undermine the reliability of the lover's judgment about what is good."
textual_basis: At 231d, the speaker uses the verb for 'to be sick' and contrasts it with 'to be of sound mind,' framing the lover's condition as a pathology. The lover is said to acknowledge this condition yet lack self-mastery. The rhetorical question that follows uses this pathological framing to cast doubt on the lover's capacity for sound judgment.
limits: This observation records the pathological characterization of the lover within Lysias's speech. It does not claim this is Plato's own view of eros, nor does it address how Socrates will later characterize eros in his palinode.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0029
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 233d-233e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 233d-233e
  start_marker: 233d
  end_marker: 233e
  start_char: 12263
  end_char: 13090
  text_sha256: 3e5b2c7140e18bf66a8651e8388931a3e137d07b3ff3bd4757a7c54f950a6671
greek_terms:
  - τοῖς δεομένοις
  - τοὺς ἀπορωτάτους
  - τοὺς προσαιτοῦντας
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The speaker argues that if one ought to gratify those who are most in need, then the same principle would absurdly extend to non-erotic social domains: parents would not value their children, we would not have loyal friends, and we should invite beggars rather than friends to private feasts.
textual_basis: |
  At 233d the speaker introduces the condition with ei chre tois deomenois malista charizesthai and draws three sequential counterfactuals: undervaluing children and parents, losing loyal friends (who are said to arise from other practices, not from this kind of desire), and redirecting private expenditure from friends to beggars (233d-233e). The reductio is completed before Phaedrus responds at 233e.
limits: |
  The observation is local to 233d-233e and records the structure of the reductio, not whether the premise is correctly attributed nor whether the implied conclusion (that the lover who needs gratification should not be favoured over the non-lover with self-control) is valid.
review_status: accepted
```


```yaml

observation: Phaedrus delivers a sustained set-speech contrasting the lover and non-lover across 232e-233a. The passage continues a longer comparative argument already underway before 232e, so this segment is not the beginning of the set-speech but a continuation of an extended antistrophic structure.
textual_basis: The segment opens in the middle of a clause at 232e and continues through a respondent turn marked by Phaedrus's speech prefix (PHAI.) with no interruption from Socrates. The argument proceeds through parallel contrasts between lovers and non-lovers across multiple criteria.
limits: This observation notes only that the passage is a sustained respondent speech, not a claim about the quality of the argument or whether Phaedrus is reciting Lysias's words verbatim.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0036
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 232e-233a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  start_marker: 232e

  end_char: 11474
  text_sha256: d29fcb63294a0b8b433ba90312313daf182d1b37f71f56256e82b3253e859767
greek_terms:
  - ἐραστῇ
  - βελτίονί
english_gloss: lover, better
observation: The speaker asserts that it is more advantageous to gratify a non-lover than a lover, on the grounds that lovers praise what is said and done even contrary to what is best out of fear of giving offence, while non-lovers have no such distorting motive.
textual_basis: "At 233a the speaker uses the comparative form for 'better' and contrasts the lover's fear-driven praise (expressed by a participle meaning 'fearing' plus a term for 'they might be hated') with the non-lover's freedom from such distortion. The comparative structure is explicit: gratifying the speaker (a non-lover) is presented as superior."
limits: This records only that the text makes a comparative claim about advantage as part of a rhetorical set-speech. It does not assess whether the argument is sound or whether Socrates would endorse it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0037
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 232e-233a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 232e-233a
  start_marker: 232e
  end_marker: 233a
  start_char: 10788
  end_char: 11474
  text_sha256: d29fcb63294a0b8b433ba90312313daf182d1b37f71f56256e82b3253e859767
greek_terms:
  - μνημεῖα
  - καταλειφθῆναι
english_gloss: memorials, be left behind
observation: "The speaker distinguishes the lover from the non-lover on temporal grounds: the lover begins with bodily desire before knowing character at 232e-233a, making future friendship uncertain once desire ceases. By contrast, the non-lover's prior friendship means that sexual favours become lasting memorials of future relations rather than a risk to friendship."
textual_basis: The temporal contrast is structured around the lover's sequence (desiring the body before knowing the character) versus the non-lover's prior friendship (being friends before doing these things). The memorial metaphor at 233a frames the favours as monuments pointing forward to what will be, not backward-looking desire.
limits: This notes only the temporal framing structure in the rhetoric, not whether the contrast is logically valid or whether Plato endorses this mode of argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0038
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 232a-232b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 232a-232b
  start_marker: 232a
  end_marker: 232b
  start_char: 9102
  end_char: 9951
  text_sha256: a02e12fe87f75d29dda765c429c2e5f9107e50b79b4c44f822312db89ce146c1
greek_terms:
  - ἐρῶντας
  - μὴ ἐρῶντας
  - ζηλοῦσθαι
  - φιλοτιμουμένους
  - ἐπιδείκνυσθαι
  - κρείττους αὑτῶν
  - τὸ βέλτιστον
  - δόξης
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The speech characterizes the lover (erastes) and non-lover as contrasting ethical types with opposed motivational structures. The lover type is driven by reputation-seeking: belief in being envied, elation in speech, and love of honor-motivated display toward all. The non-lover type is defined by self-mastery and choice of the best over human reputation."
textual_basis: At 232a the speaker describes lovers as thinking they are envied (zelousthai), being elated in speaking (eparthenai toi legein), and acting from love of honor (philotimoumenous) to display (epideiknysthai) to all that their labor was not in vain. Non-lovers, being stronger than themselves (kreittous hauton), choose the best (to beltiston) instead of reputation (doxes) from men.
limits: The characterization occurs within a rhetorical speech attributed to Lysias, not in Socratic examination. It is a persuasive typology, not a dialectical definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0039
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 232a-232b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 232a-232b
  start_marker: 232a
  end_marker: 232b
  start_char: 9102
  end_char: 9951
  text_sha256: a02e12fe87f75d29dda765c429c2e5f9107e50b79b4c44f822312db89ce146c1
greek_terms:
  - τοὺς μὲν ἐρῶντας
  - τοὺς δὲ μὴ ἐρῶντας
  - ἀνάγκη
  - φιλίαν
  - ἐπιθυμίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speech deploys a systematic sequence of parallel antitheses contrasting lovers and non-lovers across multiple criteria. Each claim about lovers (tous men erontas) is immediately countered with a parallel claim about non-lovers (tous de me erontas), covering domains of motivation, social visibility, and public blame.
textual_basis: At 232a the pattern tous men erontas... tous de me erontas structures a contrast between reputation-seeking display and self-controlled choice of the best. At 232a-b the same pattern contrasts lovers who are necessarily seen following beloveds and suspected of desire with non-lovers whose associations are attributed to friendship or other pleasure and who escape blame.
limits: This observation notes the formal rhetorical structure, not the truth of the claims. The antithesis is a persuasive device within the Lysian speaker's argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  start_char: 11474
  end_char: 12263
  text_sha256: 83962275acd45f3564ba74d2bdb1aeff2219de87a4e8bfb9684dd7d951861ff9
greek_terms:
  - ὁ ἔρως
  - ἀνιαρὰ
  - ἐπαίνου
  - τὴν μέλλουσαν ὠφελίαν
  - ἐμαυτοῦ κρατῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The Lysian speaker presents eros as distorting pain and praise, then contrasts his own self-mastery and concern for future benefit with the lover's present pleasure.
textual_basis: At 233b eros makes some things painful and compels praise; at 233c the speaker contrasts present pleasure with future benefit and being overcome by eros with self-control.
limits: This records the contrast as stated by the Lysian speaker and does not make the broader claim that friendship exists only without eros.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0043

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker characterizes the erotic lover (erastes) as suffering from a pathology in which the lover perceives everything as happening to their own detriment, and on that basis seeks to isolate the beloved from all rival associations.
textual_basis: At 232c the speaker states that lovers are subject to many griefs and regard everything as happening to their own harm. The passage then enumerates how this drives the lover to prevent the beloved's associations with the wealthy (232c), the educated (232c-d), and those possessing any other good (232d).
limits: The observation records only the non-lover speaker's characterization of erotic lovers; it does not assess whether this characterization is endorsed, ironic, or refuted elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0044
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 232c-232d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  text_sha256: b6db32fdd70f173d5f291fe7956c1b302f1aa3f9b91780ddd0416bb0bc129dd8
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The speaker deploys an inversion counterargument: the non-lover, motivated by virtue rather than passion, does not envy the beloved's associates but instead hates only those who refuse association, reasoning that rejection shows contempt while willing association brings benefit."
textual_basis: "At 232d the speaker contrasts the lover's jealous isolation of the beloved with the non-lover's attitude: those who are not in love but acted through virtue do not envy associates but hate only those unwilling to associate, believing themselves benefited by companions and slighted by refusers."
limits: This is a point within the non-lover's encomium argument, not an independent Socratic elenchus. The observation records the argument structure, not its validity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0045

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The passage constructs two contrasting ethical types through social behavior: the erotic lover who isolates the beloved out of fear of rivals ranked by wealth, education, and other goods (232c-d), and the non-lover who welcomes the beloved's associations and is motivated by virtue."
textual_basis: "At 232c-d the speaker catalogs three classes of rivals the lover fears: those possessing wealth, those possessing education, and those possessing any other good, and contrasts this with the non-lover's opposite social disposition at 232d."
limits: These are character types constructed within a rhetorical speech (Lysias's argument as reported by Phaedrus), not independently narrated dramatic personae. The observation records the text's construction of types, not a biographical claim about any historical individual.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0046
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234a-234b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 13964
  text_sha256: 119e5e8e00e53da6345f0035b0950aeda88278f1a79b7730224177dd0a1a609f
greek_terms:
  - οὐδὲ
  - ἀλλὰ
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker deploys a tightly parallel series of antithetical pairings using an oude/alla (not X, but Y) structure, accumulating six paired contrasts in rapid succession.
textual_basis: From 234a to 234b, six clauses open with oude and are countered by alla, each pairing rejecting the lover's type in favor of the non-lover's type. The series includes contrasts of worth, enjoyment, benefaction, public display, duration, and motive for enmity.
limits: This observation identifies a rhetorical figure of antithetical accumulation. It does not assess the logical validity of the contrasts or claim that the series is exhaustive.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0047
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234a-234b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234a-234b
  start_marker: 234a
  end_marker: 234b
  start_char: 13090
  end_char: 13964
  text_sha256: 119e5e8e00e53da6345f0035b0950aeda88278f1a79b7730224177dd0a1a609f
greek_terms:
  - τοὺς μὲν ἐρῶντας
  - τοῖς δὲ μὴ ἐρῶσιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker invokes social authority (friends and household members, hoi philoi and ton oikeion) who admonish lovers but never fault non-lovers, framing the lover/non-lover distinction as a matter of recognized social judgment rather than private preference.
textual_basis: At 234b, the speaker states that friends admonish lovers (tous men erontas hoi philoi nouthetousin) on the grounds that the practice is bad, whereas no one of the household (ton oikeion) has ever blamed non-lovers for taking bad counsel on this account.
limits: This observation notes the appeal to third-party social judgment as a rhetorical device. It does not evaluate whether the claim about social consensus is accurate or whether the speaker is the lover or non-lover.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0048
source_work: Phaedrus

source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 13964
  text_sha256: 119e5e8e00e53da6345f0035b0950aeda88278f1a79b7730224177dd0a1a609f
greek_terms:
  - παυσαμένου τῆς ὥρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: The speaker defers the non-lover's demonstration of virtue to a future moment after the beloved's youthful bloom (hora) has ceased, making the cessation of physical attractiveness a condition for proving genuine character.
textual_basis: At the 234a-234b boundary, the antithesis contrasts those who seek a pretext for enmity when desire ceases with those who will display their own virtue when the beloved's bloom has ceased (pausamenou tes horas tote ten hauton areten epideixontai).
limits: This observation identifies a temporal structure in the speaker's argument where virtue-display is postponed. It does not claim this is Plato's own view or that the postponement is logically necessary.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0049
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234c-234d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234c-234d
  start_marker: 234c
  end_marker: 234d
  start_char: 13964
  end_char: 14737
  text_sha256: ac9b4ed53e563d252c8e8b1a940073c84e359e662c05172e6a35f88446d82429
greek_terms:
  - δαιμονίως
  - ἐκπλαγῆναι
  - συνεβάκχευσα
  - τῆς θείας κεφαλῆς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates responds to Phaedrus's question about the speech with extravagant, religiously-tinged praise — calling the speech daimonically inspired (daimonios), saying he was struck out of his senses (ekplagenai), that he joined in Bacchic frenzy (sunebakcheusa) following Phaedrus, and refers to Phaedrus as a divine head (theias kephales).
textual_basis: At 234d, Socrates says the speech was spoken daimonios, that he was ekplagenai, and that he sunebakcheusa following Phaedrus, of the divine head. The language stacks divine-possession and mystery-cult vocabulary on a rhetorical judgment, which is disproportionate to the content of Lysias's speech.
limits: This observation does not determine whether Socrates is sincere or ironic. It registers only that the praise vocabulary is drawn from divine-mania and Bacchic registers, which is a recurring Socratic pattern.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0050
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234c-234d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234c-234d
  start_marker: 234c
  end_marker: 234d
  start_char: 13964
  end_char: 14737
  text_sha256: ac9b4ed53e563d252c8e8b1a940073c84e359e662c05172e6a35f88446d82429
greek_terms:
  - ὑπερφυῶς τά τε ἄλλα καὶ τοῖς ὀνόμασιν εἰρῆσθαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus asks Socrates for his judgment of the speech (ti soi phainetai, o Sokrates, ho logos;) and Socrates deflects, saying he followed Phaedrus because he considered Phaedrus more knowledgeable (epaein) about such matters. Socrates subordinates his own judgment to Phaedrus's reaction.
textual_basis: Phaedrus asks at the end of 234c whether the speech is not extraordinarily well-composed in its diction (huperphuos ... tois onomasin eiresthai). At 234d Socrates responds by saying he looked to Phaedrus while Phaedrus was reading, judged him more knowledgeable (se mallon e eme epaein), and followed him.
limits: This records the deferral structure in the exchange. It does not establish whether the deferral is ironic, sincere, or a procedural tactic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0051
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234c-234d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234c-234d
  start_marker: 234c
  end_marker: 234d
  start_char: 13964
  end_char: 14737
  text_sha256: ac9b4ed53e563d252c8e8b1a940073c84e359e662c05172e6a35f88446d82429
greek_terms:
  - παίζειν
  - ἐσπουδακέναι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus asks Socrates whether he seems to be playing (paizein), and Socrates counters by asking whether Phaedrus thinks he is playing rather than being serious (espoudakenai). The pair explicitly raises the question of whether the preceding exchange is play or seriousness.
textual_basis: At 234d, after Socrates's extravagant praise, Phaedrus says eien; houto de dokei paizein; and Socrates replies dokô gar soi paizein kai ouchi espoudakenai; The play/serious contrast (paizein/espoudakenai) is introduced explicitly by the characters.
limits: This observation registers the presence of the play/seriousness contrast as an explicit dramatic marker. It does not resolve whether Socrates is playing or serious, nor does it assert that this marker codes hidden meaning.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - νωθείας
  - ἐπιλέλησμαι
english_gloss: streams, like a vessel, sluggishness, I have forgotten
observation: Socrates claims he has been filled like a vessel through hearing but has forgotten how and from whom he heard, attributing the forgetting to sluggishness.
textual_basis: At 235d Socrates speaks of being filled with streams through hearing and then immediately disclaims memory of the source, citing sluggishness. This is a staged claim of ignorance about the provenance of his own inspiration.
limits: This observation identifies a textual gesture of knowledge-disavowal. It does not determine whether the disavowal is sincere or ironic, nor whether Socrates actually forgot or is feigning.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
  - ἐννέα ἄρχοντες
  - Δελφοὺς
english_gloss: golden statue of equal measure, nine archons, Delphi
observation: Phaedrus commissions Socrates to produce a better speech than Lysias's, promising as reward a golden statue of equal measure dedicated at Delphi, like the nine archons would do.
textual_basis: At 235d-235e, Phaedrus says Socrates has promised to speak better and no lesser alternatives than those in the book, and pledges a golden statue at Delphi in the manner of the nine archons. This escalates a rhetorical comparison into a formal contest with a hyperbolic public reward.
limits: This observation identifies the contest-commission structure and its stakes. It does not determine whether Phaedrus is being playful or serious, nor whether Socrates accepts the contest frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: golden, golden statue
observation: Socrates calls Phaedrus truly golden at 235e, echoing Phaedrus's own promise of a golden statue at 235d-235e.
textual_basis: Phaedrus promises a golden statue at 235d-235e. Socrates immediately responds at 235e by calling him dearest and golden, repeating the golden root. This is a wordplay that redirects Phaedrus's hyperbolic promise back onto Phaedrus himself.
limits: This observation identifies lexical repetition and wordplay. It does not determine whether the wordplay carries ironic, affectionate, or deflating force.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 236b Socrates says Phaedrus has become serious because Socrates seized on his beloved while teasing him, and because Phaedrus thinks Socrates will truly attempt a more elaborate speech against Lysias' wisdom.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0057
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 236a-236b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 236a-236b
  start_marker: 236a
  end_marker: 236b
  start_char: 17283
  end_char: 18069
  text_sha256: df363cfd658e59dc730b7ff1ae1aff7ee889d92779d8cb9ce55b16d745b6ce41
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: arrangement vs. invention
observation: Socrates draws a technical distinction between invention (heuresis) and arrangement (diathesis) in speechmaking, claiming that for necessary topics one praises only the arrangement, while for non-necessary and difficult topics one praises both arrangement and invention.
textual_basis: "At 236a Socrates says: 'of such things [necessary topics] one must praise not the invention but the arrangement, while of the non-necessary and difficult things to discover, one must praise both the arrangement and the invention.' This explicitly maps rhetorical production onto a two-part technical schema."
limits: The observation records only the explicit distinction drawn here; it does not claim this is Plato's definitive rhetoric theory or that the distinction is used consistently throughout the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0058
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 235b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 235b
  start_marker: 235b
  end_marker: 235b
  start_char: 15604
  end_char: 16027
  text_sha256: 6117bf26fec6fb6d4d0cf01e1320b541aa8d3c2153efbcba46c733a36995774f
greek_terms:
  - παλαιοὶ
  - σοφοὶ
  - ἄνδρες
  - γυναῖκες
  - ἐξελέγξουσί
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates declines to agree that Lysias's speech has left nothing worth saying on the topic, claiming that ancient wise men and women who have spoken and written on these matters would refute him if he conceded to Phaedrus merely to please him.
textual_basis: |
  At 235b, Phaedrus insists Lysias's speech is exhaustive, but Socrates refuses to be persuaded ("I will no longer be able to be persuaded by you") and attributes better knowledge on the subject to "ancient and wise men and women" who have spoken and written before. This gesture subordinates Socrates' own judgment to unnamed authorities and is a characteristic knowledge-disavowal structure.
limits: |
  The observation does not assess whether Socrates genuinely believes that prior authorities have treated the topic better; it only marks the structural move of deferring to unnamed wise predecessors rather than claiming independent knowledge.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0059

greek_terms:
  - Σαπφοῦς
  - Ἀνακρέοντος
  - συγγραφέων
  - ἀμαθίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  When pressed by Phaedrus to identify the sources of his superior knowledge, Socrates names Sappho and Anacreon as possible authorities, along with unnamed prose writers, but admits he cannot specify exactly from whom he has heard better arguments.
textual_basis: |
  At 235c, Phaedrus asks who these authorities are, and Socrates responds that he cannot say at the moment but suggests "Sappho the beautiful" or "Anacreon the wise" or certain prose writers. The naming of specific historical poets as potential sources of better discourse on eros constitutes a prosopographic moment.
limits: |
  This observation does not assert that Sappho or Anacreon actually furnished arguments Socrates will deploy; it records only the naming of historical figures as cited possible sources within the dramatic frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0060
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 235c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 235c
  start_marker: 235c
  end_marker: 235c
  start_char: 16027
  end_char: 16463
  text_sha256: c3da48e9672990d9f4a8f6e6ab286d9f685d4f68012950e6e72cd37394156d39
greek_terms:
  - ἀμαθίαν
  - πλῆρές
  - στῆθος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates claims he has a full chest of things to say that are not inferior to Lysias's speech, yet simultaneously insists that he knows none of these things come from his own thought because he is conscious of his own ignorance. He attributes the source to others whose identity he cannot specify.
textual_basis: |
  At 235c, Socrates says he senses his chest is somehow full and he could speak other things not worse than Lysias's, but he knows well that none of this comes from himself, being conscious of his own ignorance ("knowing my own ignorance"). He concludes the source must therefore be from elsewhere, from others. This is a dual move: claiming capacity while disavowing the knowledge as one's own.
limits: |
  This observation does not comment on whether Socrates believes in divine inspiration or daimonic sources; it records only the textual structure of simultaneous ability-claim and knowledge-disavowal within the quoted span.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus swears an oath by the plane tree to compel Socrates to deliver a speech, threatening to withhold all future speeches if Socrates refuses.
textual_basis: At 236e, Phaedrus swears by the plane tree that he will never display (epideixein) or report (exangelein) any speech to Socrates unless Socrates delivers his speech in front of that very tree. Socrates calls this a compulsion (ananke) tailored to a lover of speeches (philologos) and says he cannot hold back from such a feast (thoine).
limits: This observation records the oath as a dramatic device that compels speech; it does not assess whether the oath is binding in Athenian practice or whether Phaedrus would have carried out the threat.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - μύθου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates prefaces his speech with a formal invocation to the Muses, naming the speech a mythos and framing it as divinely assisted utterance.
textual_basis: At 237a, Socrates calls on the Muses (Mousai) with 'Come then, Muses,' speculates on the etymology of their epithet, and asks them to take hold of the myth (mythos) with him. He explicitly names the speech a mythos and attributes its delivery to compulsion by Phaedrus.
limits: This observation records the Muse invocation as a myth-demarcating frame; it does not assess whether the Muses are invoked sincerely, parodically, or conventionally, nor does it evaluate the content of the mythos that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0064
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 237b-237c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 237b-237c
  start_marker: 237b
  end_marker: 237c
  start_char: 19795
  end_char: 20634
  text_sha256: 7c4be9b6f20f57c54bf4d3cc9f9fcc0791d842691247960922cffe673494931f
greek_terms:
  - μία ἀρχὴ
  - οὐσίαν ἑκάστου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The non-lover speaker opens his speech by stating a methodological principle: for anyone who intends to deliberate well, there is a single starting-point (mia arche) — one must know what the deliberation is about, or miss the mark entirely. This principle is immediately applied to eros: since the question at hand is whether to grant favor to a lover or a non-lover, the speaker announces that eros must first be defined — what it is (oion t' esti) and what power it has (hen echei dunamin)."
textual_basis: "At 237b-c the speaker states that the many are unaware that they do not know the substance (ousia) of each thing and therefore fail to agree even with themselves. He then applies the principle: epi soi kai emoi ho logos prokeitai... peri erotos hoion t' esti kai hen echei dunamin — the deliberation requires a definition of eros before proceeding to the comparative question of lover vs. non-lover."
limits: This observation records only the explicit methodological demand for a preliminary definition. It does not evaluate whether the subsequent definition of eros in the speech satisfies this demand or whether the speaker is ironic. It also does not assess whether the distinction between what eros is and what power it has maps onto later Platonic definitional schemas.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0065
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 237b-237c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 237b-237c
  start_marker: 237b
  end_marker: 237c
  start_char: 19795
  end_char: 20634
  text_sha256: 7c4be9b6f20f57c54bf4d3cc9f9fcc0791d842691247960922cffe673494931f
greek_terms:
  - λέληθεν
  - οὐκ ἴσασι
  - οὐσίαν ἑκάστου
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The speaker diagnoses a widespread cognitive failure: the many are unaware (lelethen) that they do not know the substance (ousian) of each thing. Believing they know, they fail to reach agreement at the outset of inquiry, and as they proceed they render only what is plausible (to eikos). The diagnostic claim is that ignorance of essence causes argumentative failure both within oneself and with others."
textual_basis: "At 237c the speaker says: tous de pollous lelethen hoti ouk isasi ten ousian hekastou. Hos oun eidotes ou diomologountai en archei tes skepseos, proelthontes de to eikos apodidoasin; oute gar heautois oute allelois homologousin. The passage distinguishes between knowing essence and operating with mere plausibility, and attributes failure of agreement to the absence of essence-knowledge."
limits: This observation records the diagnostic claim as a textual feature of the speaker's methodological preamble. It does not evaluate whether this diagnosis is Plato's own view, whether the speaker's own subsequent speech evades the same critique, or whether the speaker's concealed identity (as a lover pretending to be a non-lover) undercuts the diagnosis.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0066
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 237b-237c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 237b-237c
  start_marker: 237b
  end_marker: 237c
  start_char: 19795
  end_char: 20634
  text_sha256: 7c4be9b6f20f57c54bf4d3cc9f9fcc0791d842691247960922cffe673494931f
greek_terms:
  - αἱμύλος
  - ἐπεπείκει
  - ὡς οὐκ ἐρῴη
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The speech is framed by a dramatic scenario in which a wily (haimylos) lover has persuaded a boy that he is not in love, and now argues that the boy should grant favors to a non-lover rather than to a lover (hos me eronti pro tou erontos deoi charizesthai). The speaker's identity as a concealed lover is disclosed to the reader before the speech begins, creating an embedded irony: the methodological appeal to definitions proceeds from a speaker whose own stated position is a pretense."
textual_basis: "At 237b the narrator explains: heis de tis auton haimylos en, hos oudenos hetton eron epepeikei ton paida hos ouk eroie. Kai pote auton aiton epeithen tout' auto, hos me eronti pro tou erontos deoi charizesthai. The lover-vs-non-lover opposition is thus established as the speech's rhetorical occasion before the definitional method is introduced."
limits: This observation records the dramatic setup of the lover/non-lover opposition and the speaker's concealed identity. It does not claim that this ironic frame invalidates the definitional argument that follows, nor does it assess the relationship between this opening and the later palinode.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0067
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238a-238b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238a-238b
  start_marker: 238a
  end_marker: 238b
  start_char: 21284
  end_char: 22104
  text_sha256: b139a05dd6ad688af6019de2232d691f7a668e2e6212d87ebdbcd76fa23af3e9
greek_terms:
  - ὕβρις
  - πολυώνυμον
  - πολυμελὲς
  - πολυμερές
  - γαστριμαργία
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 238a, hubris is introduced as a general term for appetite ruling without reason, then immediately broken into named sub-species (poluonumon, polumeles, polumeres). Each particular appetite, when it dominates, gives its name to the person possessed by it, as with gastrimargia at 238a-238b.
textual_basis: "The passage defines hubris as a genus (appetite drawing irrationally toward pleasures and ruling), then states it is poluonumon because it is polumeles kai polumeres (many-limbed, many-parted). A specific kind is named: the appetite that rules concerning food yields the name gastrimargia for its possessor. Other sibling appetites (adelphai) are mentioned analogously."
limits: This observation records the definition-by-subdivision structure but does not assess whether the definition of hubris is Socrates' own view or part of Lysias' imagined speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0068
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238a-238b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238a-238b
  start_marker: 238a
  end_marker: 238b
  start_char: 21284
  end_char: 22104
  text_sha256: b139a05dd6ad688af6019de2232d691f7a668e2e6212d87ebdbcd76fa23af3e9
greek_terms:
  - τυραννεύσασα
  - δυναστευούσης
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 238a-238b, each particular appetite is described in political terms of ruling (arxasa, turanneusasa, dunasteuouses). The dominating appetite acts as a tyrant over the person, and the person is named after whichever appetite holds power.
textual_basis: Appetite is said to rule (arxasa, 238a); concerning drink it acts tyrannically (turanneusasa, 238b); the names of desires belong to the one that is always in power (tes aei dunasteuouses, 238b). The sustained personification of appetite as a ruling power maps the political domain of tyranny onto the psychological domain of desire.
limits: This records the political metaphor for psychic domination. It does not claim this is Socrates' own psychology or that it prefigures the tripartite soul of the Republic; it only notes the metaphor within the speech Socrates attributes to an unnamed speaker.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0069
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238a-238b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238a-238b
  start_marker: 238a
  end_marker: 238b
  start_char: 21284
  end_char: 22104
  text_sha256: b139a05dd6ad688af6019de2232d691f7a668e2e6212d87ebdbcd76fa23af3e9
greek_terms:
  - καλὴν
  - ἐπαξίαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 238a, after stating that each dominating appetite confers its name on the possessor, the speaker adds that none of these names is fine or worthy to possess. The definitional subdivision of hubris thus carries an explicit negative evaluation built into the classification itself.
textual_basis: "The clause at 238a stating that the names are neither fine nor worthy to possess follows directly from the claim that the person is named after the outstanding form of hubris. The classification is not presented as value-neutral: sub-species of hubris are inherently shameful names to bear."
limits: This notes the evaluative character of the definitional procedure. It does not assess whether this evaluation is Socratic, Lysianic, or part of a rhetorical strategy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0070
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 236c-236d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 236c-236d
  start_marker: 236c
  end_marker: 236d
  start_char: 18069
  end_char: 18993
  text_sha256: a24e28eb35294bffc8ed6709eba4e5f461e9003ec89f6650c875ee25726e2e4f
greek_terms:
  - φορτικὸν πρᾶγμα
  - ἀνταποδιδόντες
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus coerces Socrates into speaking by threatening comic reciprocal mockery. He frames his threat through the language of comic convention, saying they would be forced to do the coarse thing of the comic poets, exchanging tit-for-tat, and warns Socrates not to make that necessary.
textual_basis: At 236c Phaedrus says he must speak lest they be compelled to do the coarse thing of the comic poets (to ton komoidon phortikon pragma) in exchanging back and forth (antapodidontes). He immediately follows with a threat to quote a line exposing Socrates' desire to speak, and adds a physical coercion threat at 236d ('I am stronger and younger'), all as leverage to secure Socrates' agreement to deliver the speech.
limits: The observation does not claim that Socrates actually agrees to speak under duress in this segment; the procedural pressure is exerted by Phaedrus alone in these lines.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0071
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 236c-236d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  text_sha256: a24e28eb35294bffc8ed6709eba4e5f461e9003ec89f6650c875ee25726e2e4f
greek_terms:
  - μόνω ἐν ἐρημίᾳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus presses Socrates to speak by noting that they are alone in a deserted spot (mono en eremiai) and that he, Phaedrus, is stronger and younger, so Socrates should understand what he means and speak willingly rather than under force.
textual_basis: At 236c-d Phaedrus declares 'we are alone in a deserted place, and I am stronger and younger; from all this, understand what I'm telling you, and by no means wish to speak under force rather than willingly.' This explicitly invokes the physical setting and the participants' physical attributes to frame the speech compulsion.
limits: The observation records the dramatic staging of the physical and social setting as leverage; it does not assess whether Phaedrus would actually use force.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0072

  end_char: 18993
  text_sha256: a24e28eb35294bffc8ed6709eba4e5f461e9003ec89f6650c875ee25726e2e4f
greek_terms:
  - γελοῖος ἔσομαι
  - ἀγαθὸν ποιητὴν
  - ἰδιώτης αὐτοσχεδιάζων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates protests that he will be ridiculous (geloios esomai), an amateur improvising (idiotes autoschediazon) on the same themes alongside a good poet. This is a self-deprecating hedge before delivering the speech Phaedrus demands.
textual_basis: "At 236d Socrates says to Phaedrus: 'But, blessed Phaedrus, I will be ridiculous — an amateur improvising on the same subjects beside a good poet.' This self-characterization as a laughable amateur (geloios, idiotes) contrasted with a good poet (agathon poieten) is structurally ironic: Socrates is about to deliver a rhetorically elaborate speech."
limits: The observation notes the self-deprecating posture; it does not evaluate whether Socrates' subsequent speech actually fulfills the amateur claim or surpasses Lysias.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0073
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238c-238d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238c-238d

  end_char: 22920
  text_sha256: 5bd414dcb51bdd9b150f9482fc5e10c174c1f1e85249203b1a4b9f0b0ad2b8ab
greek_terms:
  - ἔρως
  - ῥώμη
  - ἐπωνυμία
english_gloss: erotic desire is named from strength (rhome), having been led and strengthened toward bodily beauty
observation: "Socrates provides an etymological account of eros (eros) at 238c: desire led toward the pleasure of beauty, strengthened by its kindred appetites toward bodily beauty, prevailing in its leading, took its name from its own strength (rhome) and was called eros."
textual_basis: "The text explicitly derives the name eros from rhome (strength): the desire is said to have taken its name from this very strength (apo autes tes rhomes eponymian labousa). This is a definition-by-etymology move within the broader definitional project of the speech."
limits: This observation records only the etymological device at 238c. It does not assess whether the etymology is linguistically sound, whether Socrates endorses it, or whether it belongs to the non-lover persona adopted in the speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0074
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238c-238d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238c-238d
  start_marker: 238c
  end_marker: 238d
  start_char: 22104
  end_char: 22920
  text_sha256: 5bd414dcb51bdd9b150f9482fc5e10c174c1f1e85249203b1a4b9f0b0ad2b8ab
greek_terms:
  - νυμφόληπτος
  - θεῖος
  - θεῖον πάθος
  - διθυράμβων
english_gloss: Socrates claims the place is divine, fears becoming nympholept, says his speech approaches dithyrambs
observation: Socrates interrupts his own speech to ask Phaedrus whether he seems to have undergone a divine experience (theion pathos, 238c). Phaedrus confirms an unusual fluency has taken hold of him. Socrates then warns that the place seems divine (theios, 238d) and that if he becomes nympholeptos as the speech proceeds, Phaedrus should not be surprised, because his present utterances are no longer far from dithyrambs. He then attributes the cause to Phaedrus.
textual_basis: "Socrates explicitly frames his heightened rhetorical state as a divine or inspired condition: theion pathos (238c), theios ho topos (238d), nympholeptos (238d), and dithyrambon (238d). He also blames Phaedrus for causing this state. The combination of divine-possession language with a self-conscious attribution to the interlocutor carries an ironic marker: Socrates distances himself from the speech even as he delivers it."
limits: This observation records the ironic framing of Socrates' speech as divinely inspired. It does not assess whether the inspiration is genuine, or whether the passage functions as a critique of rhetoric. It notes only the textual phenomena at 238c-238d.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0075
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238c-238d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238c-238d
  start_marker: 238c
  end_marker: 238d
  start_char: 22104
  end_char: 22920
  text_sha256: 5bd414dcb51bdd9b150f9482fc5e10c174c1f1e85249203b1a4b9f0b0ad2b8ab
greek_terms:
  - σιγῇ τοίνυν μου ἄκουε
  - τὰ λοιπὰ ἄκουε
english_gloss: Phaedrus notes Socrates' unusual fluency; Socrates twice commands Phaedrus to listen in silence
observation: At 238c Phaedrus remarks that an unusual fluency (euroia) has taken hold of Socrates. Socrates immediately responds with a command to listen in silence (sige toinyn mou akoue, 238c-d). He repeats the command a few lines later (alla ta loipa akoue, 238d). Socrates constrains Phaedrus to a silent listener role for the remainder of the speech, citing his own unusual inspired state as grounds.
textual_basis: "The text marks an explicit shift in turn geometry: Phaedrus comments on Socrates' atypical fluency (para to eiothos euroia), Socrates responds by constraining Phaedrus to silence twice in rapid succession, and then resumes the speech. This is a procedural move within the dialogue that converts a conversational exchange into a sustained monologue under the sign of a claimed divine inspiration."
limits: This observation records the turn-geometry shift from dialogue to monologue at 238c-238d. It does not evaluate whether the fluency is genuinely inspired or whether the constraint on Phaedrus is ironic. It is restricted to the procedural move in this segment.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0076
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 237d-237e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - ἰδέα
  - ἔμφυτος
  - ἐπίκτητος δόξα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The speech introduces two ruling and leading forms within each person: one is innate desire for pleasures, the other is acquired opinion aiming at the best."
textual_basis: At 237d-237e the speaker says that in each of us there are two forms that rule and lead, and distinguishes the first as innate desire for pleasures from the second as acquired opinion striving for the best.
limits: This is a psychological division of internal ruling principles in a lover/non-lover speech, not a metaphysical claim about transcendent forms; the use of the term idea here is psychological rather than ontological.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Eros is defined as a kind of desire, with the immediate qualification that non-lovers also desire beautiful things.
textual_basis: At 237d the speaker states that eros is some kind of desire, then immediately notes that non-lovers too desire beautiful things, which introduces the need for a criterion to distinguish the lover from the non-lover.
limits: This is a provisional definition within the speech's methodological framework; it does not claim this is a final or exhaustive account of eros.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 237d-237e the two forms are said sometimes to be in concord and sometimes in factional strife, and at one time one prevails, at another the other.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0079
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 239b-239c
source_ref:

greek_terms:
  - θεία φιλοσοφία
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 239b the non-lover speech identifies divine philosophy (theia philosophia) as the greatest practical good from which the lover must keep the beloved away, on pain of being despised.
textual_basis: |
  The text asserts that the lover causes the greatest harm by excluding the beloved from whatever would make him most thoughtful (phronimōtatos), and then names this greatest excluded good as divine philosophy at 239b. The passage falls within the non-lover's set speech, so the identification is rhetorically framed.
limits: |
  Does not assert that philosophy is excluded in all erotic relationships. The claim appears inside a partisan non-lover speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0080
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - μαλθακόν
  - στερεὸν
  - ἁπαλῆς καὶ ἀνάνδρου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 239c the non-lover speech constructs a body-type profile of the beloved that the lover seeks to cultivate: soft rather than solid, reared in shade rather than pure sun, inexperienced in manly toils and dry sweats, but experienced in soft and unmanly nurture.
textual_basis: |
  The passage at 239c explicitly contrasts a soft, sheltered, unmanly bodily disposition with a hardy, sun-exposed, manly one, attributing the preference for the former to the lover's pursuit of pleasure over the good.
limits: |
  The body-type profile is part of the non-lover's polemical characterization and does not necessarily represent Socrates' or the dialogue's final view. The observation registers the construction of an ethical-bodily type, not an endorsement of it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0081

greek_terms:
  - ἡδὺ πρὸ ἀγαθοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 239c the non-lover speech characterizes the erotic lover as compelled to pursue the pleasurable before the good.
textual_basis: |
  The clause that states the lover is compelled to pursue the pleasurable before the good at 239c embeds an explicit evaluative ranking of pleasure over good, presented as a compulsion.
limits: |
  The claim about pleasure-before-good motivation is part of the non-lover's indictment of eros and so is a dramatic speech-act, not a philosophical thesis advanced by Socrates. It does not address whether all eros or only the lover type under description is so compelled.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0082
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238e-239a

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  text_sha256: aa6a23fbaa8d8cb2092688b5808653197c0bc2924cf16448e8b483cf51328564
greek_terms:
  - ἐπιθυμίας
  - δουλεύοντί
  - ἡδονῇ
  - νοσοῦντι
english_gloss: desire, enslaving, pleasure, sick
observation: |
  Socrates frames the lover as someone ruled by desire and enslaved to pleasure,
  then introduces a medical analogy: just as a sick person finds everything
  pleasant that does not resist, so the lover wants the beloved to be as weak
  and non-resistant as possible (238e). The analogy maps the source domain of
  disease/health onto the target domain of erotic pursuit.
textual_basis: |
  At 238e the speech explicitly states the lover is ruled by desire and
  enslaved to pleasure. The medical analogy is introduced when the speech says
  that to a sick person everything pleasant is what does not resist. The
  beloved is cast as the object the lover will shape to be weaker so as to
  avoid resistance.
limits: |
  The analogy is not developed with the precision of Socratic craft analogies
  elsewhere; it functions as a rhetorical flourish within a set speech. The
  observation covers only the disease analogy at 238e, not the subsequent
  enumerations of harms at 239a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0083
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 238e-239a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 238e-239a
  start_marker: 238e
  end_marker: 239a
  start_char: 22920
  end_char: 23581
  text_sha256: aa6a23fbaa8d8cb2092688b5808653197c0bc2924cf16448e8b483cf51328564
greek_terms:
  - ἥττω
  - ὑποδεέστερον
  - κρείττω
  - ἰσούμενον
english_gloss: weaker, inferior, stronger, equal
observation: |
  The speech sets up a zero-sum structure in which the lover necessarily makes
  the beloved weaker across multiple domains of comparison: the lover will not
  tolerate a beloved who is stronger or equal, but will always render him
  weaker and inferior (239a). This is presented as a necessary consequence
  (signaled by the necessity marker) of the lover's condition, not as a
  contingent outcome.
textual_basis: |
  At 239a the text states that the lover will not willingly tolerate a beloved
  who is stronger or equal, but always renders him weaker and inferior. The
  necessity marker recurs in the same passage, framing the harms as
  unavoidable.
limits: |
  This is a claim made within the non-Socratic speech delivered by Socrates
  under the guise of the non-lover. The zero-sum logic is a feature of that
  rhetorical performance, not necessarily a Socratic thesis. The observation
  does not assess whether later passages undermine this frame.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - πατρὸς
  - μητρὸς
  - συγγενῶν
  - φίλων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates, speaking as the non-lover, claims that the lover would wish the beloved to be deprived of his nearest and dearest possessions — father, mother, relatives, and friends — so that the beloved becomes an orphan with respect to those most favorable and divine belongings (239e).
textual_basis: "Socrates states this explicitly at 239e: the lover would pray before all else that the beloved be orphaned of the most beloved and well-disposed and divine possessions, specifying father, mother, relatives, and friends as what the lover would accept the beloved being deprived of. The Greek terms for father, mother, relatives, and friends appear in sequence at 239e."
limits: This observation records the claim within the non-lover's speech at 239e. It does not assess whether this claim is later refuted, modified, or whether Socrates endorses it outside the speech's dramatic frame. The claim is presented as the lover's hidden wish, not as a general psychology of love.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ἓν κεφάλαιον ὁρισαμένους
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 239d, the speaker explicitly marks the end of one topic and signals a transition to the next: after enumerating consequences of the lover's influence on the beloved's body, he says it is not worth going further and proposes to define a summary point before moving on to another subject. The new topic at 239e is the benefit or harm the beloved receives from the lover's company and guardianship."
textual_basis: "At 239d the speaker states it is not worth proceeding further and that he will define one summary point before going on to another topic. At 239e the announced new topic appears: what benefit or what harm the company and guardianship of the lover will provide concerning the possession."
limits: This observation records the explicit procedural move from the speaker within the non-lover's speech. It does not assess the rhetorical function of this move or whether the transition is effective. The signal is internal to Lysias's (or the non-lover's) discourse, not Socrates's own framing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 239d the speaker says that this kind of body, in war and other great needs, makes enemies take courage and friends and the lovers themselves fear.
limits: This observation records the claim made within the non-lover's speech that the lover's influence produces a body unfit for war. english_gloss: ""
```
```yaml
observation: |
  At 240e-241a, the lover's psychological change after eros ends is described using political governance vocabulary: he replaces eros and mania with nous and sophrosyne as his internal ruler (archon) and protector (prostates).
textual_basis: |
  The text describes the former lover as having changed to another ruler and protector within himself, nous and sophrosyne in place of eros and mania (240e-241a).
limits: |
  This observation notes the use of political vocabulary for internal psychic states within Socrates' first speech. It does not claim a full soul-polis analogy or that this is Socrates' own view.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - αἰσχύνης
```
