```yaml
observation: |
  At 240e-241a, the former lover is described as untrustworthy after eros ends: he had made many promises with oaths and supplications during his erotic period, but when it is time to repay he has changed internally and is ashamed to admit it, leaving his promises unfulfilled.
textual_basis: |
  The text states the lover becomes untrustworthy after eros ends (240e-241a), and describes many oaths and promises made earlier (240e-241a). The lover is then described as being too ashamed to admit his internal change (241a).
limits: |
  This records the oath-breaking pattern as part of the critique of the lover in Socrates' first speech. It does not claim this as a universal claim about all erotic relationships.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0089
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234e-235a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234e-235a
  start_marker: 234e
  end_marker: 235a
  start_char: 14737
  end_char: 15604
  text_sha256: 011dc7e277db81933a896b4e158df093e2aa23bf7822065bef7d5908a8c72792
greek_terms:
  - σαφῆ
  - στρογγύλα
  - ἀκριβῶς ἕκαστα τῶν ὀνομάτων ἀποτετόρνευται
english_gloss: clear, rounded, precisely turned each of the words
observation: "Socrates distinguishes two axes for praising a speech: whether the speech says what it ought (ta deonta), and whether its verbal craftsmanship is clear, compact, and precisely turned. He reports that he attended only to Lysias' rhetorical style and missed the content altogether because of his own dullness."
textual_basis: "At 234e, Phaedrus asks whether any Greek could speak greater and more things on the same subject. Socrates responds at 234e-235a by proposing two standards: one about ta deonta, the other about clarity (saphe), compactness (stroggyla), and precision of word-crafting (akribos hekasta ton onomaton apotetorneutai). He then confesses he paid attention only to the rhetorical dimension."
limits: This observation notes the explicit two-axis framework Socrates offers and his self-reported attention to style over content. It does not assess whether Socrates is being ironic or whether he actually believes Lysias' speech fails on the content axis.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0090
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234e-235a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234e-235a
  start_marker: 234e

  start_char: 14737
  end_char: 15604
  text_sha256: 011dc7e277db81933a896b4e158df093e2aa23bf7822065bef7d5908a8c72792
greek_terms:
  - δὶς καὶ τρὶς τὰ αὐτὰ εἰρηκέναι
english_gloss: said the same things two and three times
observation: Socrates observes that Lysias' speech appears to have said the same things two or three times, and he infers that this repetition indicates either a lack of resourcefulness in speaking at length on the same subject or indifference to the matter.
textual_basis: "Socrates states at 235a: 'he seemed to me to have said the same things twice and thrice' (dis kai tris ta auta eirekenai), and offers two explanations: the speaker was not very well-supplied (ou panu euporon) for saying many things on the same subject, or perhaps he did not care about such a thing (ouden auto melon tou toioutou)."
limits: This observation records the explicit repetition critique and its two stated explanations. It does not claim that the repetition is objectively present or that Lysias actually lacked skill.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0091
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 234e-235a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 234e-235a
  start_marker: 234e
  end_marker: 235a
  start_char: 14737
  end_char: 15604
  text_sha256: 011dc7e277db81933a896b4e158df093e2aa23bf7822065bef7d5908a8c72792
greek_terms:
  - νεανιεύεσθαι
  - ἐπιδεικνύμενος
  - ταὐτὰ ἑτέρως τε καὶ ἑτέρως λέγων ἀμφοτέρως εἰπεῖν ἄριστα
english_gloss: showing off like a youngster, displaying that he could say the same things in different ways and speak excellently both ways
observation: "Socrates frames his critique of Lysias' speech through self-deprecation, claiming his own dullness (oudeneia) prevented him from attending to content. He then recharacterizes Lysias' repetition not as a flaw but as a deliberate epideictic display of virtuosity: saying the same things in different ways and doing both excellently."
textual_basis: At 234e-235a, Socrates attributes his inattention to content to his own oudeneia (dullness/nothingness). At 235a, he says Lysias seemed to him to be 'showing off like a youngster' (neanieuesthai), displaying (epideiknumenos) that he could say the same things differently (tauta heteros te kai heteros legon) and speak excellently both ways (amphoteros eipein arista).
limits: "This observation records the irony structure: self-deprecation paired with a reframing that turns an apparent defect into a display of skill. It does not determine whether Socrates means this praise sincerely or sarcastically."
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0092
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240c-240d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 240c-240d
  start_marker: 240c
  end_marker: 240d
  start_char: 26017
  end_char: 26811
  text_sha256: 49b6d9db122db47a7ea0f4d3de68c438625253e4dce9b1e28b8844b96df4c5bd
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: age-equal pleases age-equal
observation: |
  The speaker cites a traditional saying that like pleases like ("the old saying the age-equal to the age-equal") and then immediately undermines it by asserting that even the company of equals produces satiety (koron). This subordinate clause (alla homos) concedes the proverb's force but blocks its application as a defense of the lover-beloved relationship.
textual_basis: |
  At 240c the speaker introduces the pa laios logos (old saying) that the equal in age pleases the equal in age, providing the rationale of shared time producing friendship through similarity. The speaker then pivots with alla homos (but nevertheless) to claim that the association of age-equals also brings satiety (koron).
limits: |
  The observation tracks a rhetorical move of citing then subverting common opinion; it does not evaluate the truth of the proverb or whether the speaker endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0093
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240c-240d

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  start_marker: 240c
  end_marker: 240d
  start_char: 26017
  end_char: 26811
  text_sha256: 49b6d9db122db47a7ea0f4d3de68c438625253e4dce9b1e28b8844b96df4c5bd
greek_terms:
  - κόρον
english_gloss: satiety, the compulsory, driven by compulsion and stinging goad
observation: |
  The lover is described as compelled by necessity and a goad (oistros) to remain constantly with the beloved day and night, unwillingly (hekon) separating. The erotic attachment is framed as an involuntary, pathological drive rather than a chosen relationship.
textual_basis: |
  At 240c-d the speaker claims that the lover, being older, never willingly leaves the younger beloved day or night but is driven by necessity and a goad (hyp' anankes te kai oistrou elaunetai). The language of compulsion (ananke) and the goad (oistros, a term associated with animal frenzy) casts the lover's behavior as pathological rather than voluntary.
limits: |
  This record notes the pathological framing of the lover; it does not assess whether the speech as a whole endorses this characterization or whether it represents Socrates' view.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0094
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240c-240d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 240c-240d
  start_marker: 240c
  end_marker: 240d
  start_char: 26017
  end_char: 26811
  text_sha256: 49b6d9db122db47a7ea0f4d3de68c438625253e4dce9b1e28b8844b96df4c5bd
greek_terms:
  - παραμύθιον
  - ἀηδίας
english_gloss: consolation, disgust
observation: |
  The speaker constructs a stark sensory asymmetry between lover and beloved: the lover enjoys seeing, hearing, touching, and perceiving the beloved through every sense; the beloved, by contrast, sees an aging face "not in its season" and receives no compensatory pleasures, reaching the extreme of disgust (aedias). The passage juxtaposes the lover's multi-sensory pleasure catalogue against the beloved's deprivation.
textual_basis: |
  At 240d the lover is described as receiving pleasures through sight, hearing, touch, and every sense (horonti, akouonti, haptomeno, kai pasan aisthesin aisthanomeno), bound tightly in pleasurable service. The beloved then is asked what consolation or pleasure (paramythion) he receives, and the answer is none: he sees an aging, unseasonal face and reaches extreme disgust (ep' eschaton elthein aedias).
limits: |
  This record notes the structural antithesis between lover and beloved sensory experience; it does not claim this represents psychological truth or evaluate the rhetorical effectiveness.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0095
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 241b-241c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  - βλαβερωτάτῳ πρὸς τὴν τῆς ψυχῆς παίδευσιν
english_gloss: Most harmful to the education of the soul.
observation: At 241c the speaker ranks the harms caused by the lover in a threefold ascending scale — harm to property, harm to bodily condition, and most harmful of all to the soul's education (paideusis). The soul's paideusis is declared the most valuable thing for both humans and gods in truth.
textual_basis: The text catalogs three harms (harm to property, harm to bodily condition, and most harmful to the soul's paideusis) and adds that nothing is more honored in truth by human beings or gods.
limits: This observation records the threefold harm scale and the superlative valuation of psychic education within the non-lover's rhetorical speech. It does not assert that Socrates endorses this ranking, nor that the claim is true.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: Socrates invokes the archaic purification ritual for those who err in mythologizing, citing Stesichorus as the authority who recognized the cause of his blindness and immediately composed a palinode, and then quotes the opening of Stesichorus's recantation.
textual_basis: At 242e-243a Socrates says there is an ancient purification for those who err concerning mythologizing, which Homer did not perceive but Stesichorus did; he then quotes Stesichorus's line beginning 'That story is not true.'
limits: The observation records the appeal to poetic authority for ritual purification, not a claim about the truth of the palinode model or its application.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 242e-243a Socrates states it is necessary for him to purify himself and describes the ancient purification for those erring in mythologizing, then begins quoting Stesichorus's palinode as the model.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0099
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - ποτίμῳ λόγῳ
  - ἁλμυρὰν ἀκοὴν
english_gloss: with a drinkable/potable speech ... salty hearing
observation: "Socrates frames his imminent recantation speech as a ritual purification: he characterizes his first speech as polluted and desires to wash away the salty hearing with a drinkable speech."
textual_basis: At 243d Socrates says he feels shame before Eros and desires to wash away (apoklysasthai) the salty hearing (halmyran akoen) with a drinkable speech (potimoi logoi). The metaphor of salt water vs. fresh drinking water casts the previous speech as impure and the coming palinode as cleansing.
limits: The observation records the purification metaphor but does not claim that Socrates genuinely believes he has committed impiety rather than performing ironic self-correction for rhetorical effect.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0100
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 243d-243e

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 33424
  text_sha256: 8826a7819339219431593c632f518d6b69303141080fd5758dd087de214133b2
greek_terms:
  - πᾶσα ἀνάγκη
  - ἀναγκασθῆναι
english_gloss: every necessity ... to be compelled
observation: Phaedrus declares that once Socrates delivers his praise of the lover, Lysias will be under total necessity to write a competing speech in response, framing the upcoming palinode as a speech that compels a rival composition.
textual_basis: At 243d-e, Phaedrus says it is every necessity (pasa ananke) that Lysias will be compelled (anankasthenai) by him to write again on the same subject after Socrates speaks. The doubled necessity language (noun + verb from the same root) emphasizes the coercive force Socrates' speech will exert on Lysias.
limits: The observation records the compulsion framing but does not assess whether Phaedrus genuinely has the power to compel Lysias or whether this is playful exaggeration among friends.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0101
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - ποῦ δή μοι ὁ παῖς
  - παρά σοι μάλα πλησίον
english_gloss: Where then is the boy for me ... right beside you, very near
observation: Socrates asks where the boy to whom he was speaking is, and Phaedrus confirms the boy is always right beside Socrates whenever he wishes, indicating that a third person — the imagined beloved addressed in the speech — is dramatically present as a silent listener.
textual_basis: At 243e Socrates asks 'Where then is the boy to whom I was speaking?' (pou de moi ho pais pros hon elegon) so that the boy may also hear the recantation. Phaedrus replies that the boy is always right beside Socrates, very near (para soi mala plesion), whenever Socrates wishes. This establishes the boy as a dramatically present but silent third party.
limits: The observation records the dramatic presence of an unnamed addressee but does not identify the boy with any specific historical person or claim that the boy represents an allegorical figure.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0102
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 243b-243c
source_ref:

  start_char: 31838
  end_char: 32676
  text_sha256: be8d1fb7f2f1fa2c29b04041e8bc65abefb0eae939ba458866203ca436d6e874
greek_terms:
  - παλινῳδίαν
  - ἀνέβλεψεν
  - ἐγκεκαλυμμένος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates completes his recantation framework by quoting Stesichorus and narrating the Palinode legend as a procedural model for his own speech.
textual_basis: At 243b Socrates quotes a line attributed to Stesichorus, then recounts that after composing the full Palinode Stesichorus immediately recovered his sight. Socrates declares he will be wiser by offering his own palinode before suffering harm, and marks the shift from the earlier covered-head posture to one with bare head.
limits: This observation records the use of a poetic-mythic exemplum as an argumentative template. It does not assess whether the Stesichorus story is historically accurate or whether Socrates' invocation is sincere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 243b Socrates says he will now speak with bare head, contrasting it with the earlier posture of being covered from shame.
```

```yaml

  - ἐλεύθερον ἔρωτα

observation: Socrates preemptively characterizes the imagined critical listener of his first speech as a freeborn man of gentle character who would mistake the speakers for sailors who have never seen free love.
textual_basis: At 243c Socrates says that if a noble and gentle listener overheard the two speeches, he would think he was hearing men raised among sailors who have never seen free love. This frames the upcoming palinode as a correction against a degraded picture of eros.
limits: This observation records the rhetorical strategy of imagining a respectable auditor to motivate a self-correction. It does not claim that Socrates actually believes the characterization of his first speech as sailor-talk.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0105

  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports that as he was about to cross the river after his first speech on eros, his customary daimonion sign appeared and stopped him, preventing his departure.
textual_basis: At 242a-b Socrates had declared he would cross the river and leave, but at 242b he reports that when he was about to cross, the daimonion and the customary sign came to him. The intervention is explicitly marked as the familiar recurring sign.
limits: The text does not yet reveal what the sign commands; it only reports the occurrence. The observation records the dramatic interruption, not the content of the divine message.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0106

  end_char: 30215
  text_sha256: 8b73521f610dd6e9f6c594256b827b4fb84bf4475bd59bd3e5460a187b82b807
greek_terms:
  - θεῖός
  - περὶ τοὺς λόγους
  - ἀτεχνῶς θαυμάσιος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates calls Phaedrus divine about speeches and simply amazing, then claims Phaedrus has caused more speeches to be produced than anyone of his generation except Simmias the Theban.
textual_basis: At 242a-b Socrates says Phaedrus is divine about speeches and simply amazing, then asserts that of those born in Phaedrus's lifetime, no one has produced more speeches than him — whether speaking himself or compelling others — excepting Simmias, with Phaedrus surpassing the rest by far. The hyperbolic escalation and the exclusion of only one person signal the ironic register.
limits: This does not prove Socrates' praise is insincere; it records that the hyperbolic formulation fits the recurring pattern of Socratic mock praise.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0107
source_work: Phaedrus

  end_char: 35786

greek_terms:
  - κατοκωχή
  - μανία
  - Μουσῶν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 245a Socrates introduces the third form of divine madness: possession and madness from the Muses (as the third item in a numbered taxonomy). This expands a numbered taxonomy of madness types that began earlier in the palinode.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates marks the third madness as from the Muses at 245a, enumerating it as a distinct type. The passage between 244e and 245a shows the transition from the second type (telestic/ritual madness) to the third (poetic madness).
limits: |
  This observation notes the classification structure, not whether the Muses' madness is endorsed or ironized. It does not assess the earlier or later items in the taxonomy beyond the transition visible at 244e-245a.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0108
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245a
  start_marker: 245a
  end_marker: 245a
  start_char: 35349
  end_char: 35786
  text_sha256: 520943c93cdb7bc285c6f06be887f91fe6e010b7e25b4a5b038e0d59661306a1
greek_terms:
  - ἄνευ μανίας Μουσῶν
  - ποιητικὰς θύρας
  - σωφρονοῦντος
  - μαινομένων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 245a Socrates claims that whoever approaches the doors of poetry without the Muses' madness, trusting that craft alone will make them an adequate poet, remains incomplete, and the sane person's poetry is eclipsed by that of the mad.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates contrasts the would-be poet who relies on craft with those who compose through Muse-inspired madness. The passage states that the sane poet's work vanishes beside that of the inspired mad poets. The craft-versus-inspiration antithesis is explicit in the pairing of craft against Muse-madness at 245a.
limits: |
  This observation records the craft-vs.-inspiration contrast as a textual feature. It does not determine whether Socrates endorses, ironizes, or merely reports this view for the purposes of the palinode. It does not claim this is Plato's own poetics.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0109

greek_terms:
  - ἔπη
  - διθυράμβους
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 241d-e Socrates interrupts his own speech, announces that he has already shifted from dithyrambs into epic verse (epe), and breaks off rather than continuing to praise the non-lover.
textual_basis: Socrates says he is already uttering epe rather than dithyrambs (241e), marking a register shift within the speech, and declares the speech finished (241d) despite Phaedrus expecting it to continue.
limits: Does not establish that the speech actually contains verse; the claim is a self-description by the speaker, and the break-off prevents confirmation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0110

  - Νυμφῶν
  - ἐνθουσιάσω
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 241d-e Socrates attributes his own speech's rhetorical momentum to the nymphs of the place and claims he will clearly be inspired (enthusiaso) by them if he continues, disclaiming personal control over the speech.
textual_basis: Socrates tells Phaedrus at 241e that if he begins praising the non-lover he will clearly be possessed by the nymphs into whom Phaedrus threw him deliberately (ek pronoias).
limits: This is a self-attribution within the dialogue; it does not establish that the nymphs actually cause the speech, only that Socrates frames his rhetorical performance as externally driven.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: Socrates declares at 241d that the speech has its end, Phaedrus protests that he thought it was only halfway done and should continue with praise of the non-lover (241d-e), and Socrates responds with a one-logos summary instead of a full account (241e).
limits: Does not establish why Socrates stops; the nymph inspiration claim is a separate dramatic feature.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0112
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245b-245c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245b-245c
  start_marker: 245b
  end_marker: 245c
  start_char: 35786
  end_char: 36693
  text_sha256: 282c382b7947f3cb3e115e6cedc2ff8e46728272498856a9611cb9f84aa58cf5
greek_terms:
  - μανίας
  - μανία
  - ψυχὴ πᾶσα ἀθάνατος
  - ἀεικίνητον
english_gloss: madness from gods, all soul is immortal, ever-moving
observation: Socrates pivots from enumerating divine madness benefits to announcing a proof that erotic madness is a god-sent blessing, then declares the proof's starting principle that all soul is immortal, framing the argument as a demonstration that will be credible to the wise but incredible to the clever.
textual_basis: "At 245b Socrates says let no argument frighten us away from preferring the inspired lover; instead the opponent must show that eros is not sent by gods for benefit. Socrates counters that he must demonstrate the opposite, that this madness is given by gods for the greatest good fortune (245c). He then marks this as an apodeixis that will be persuasive to the wise but not to the clever, and states the first principle: all soul is immortal, because the ever-moving is immortal (245c)."
limits: This observation records the structure of a divine-hedged argument launch, not the truth or validity of the immortality argument that follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0113
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245b-245c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245b-245c
  start_marker: 245b
  end_marker: 245c
  start_char: 35786
  end_char: 36693
  text_sha256: 282c382b7947f3cb3e115e6cedc2ff8e46728272498856a9611cb9f84aa58cf5
greek_terms:
  - ψυχῆς φύσεως πέρι θείας τε καὶ ἀνθρωπίνης
  - πάθη τε καὶ ἔργα
  - ἀρχὴ δὲ ἀποδείξεως
english_gloss: nature of soul divine and human, affections and works, beginning of proof
observation: Socrates announces that to grasp the truth about the nature of soul one must first understand its divine and human nature, its affections and works, and then posits as the starting point of the demonstration that all soul is immortal.
textual_basis: "At 245c Socrates states that one must first grasp the nature of soul, both divine and human, seeing its affections (pathe) and works (erga), and the beginning of the proof is this: all soul is immortal. He then defines the ever-moving as immortal and argues that only the self-moving, since it never abandons itself, never ceases moving, and is the source and beginning of motion for all other moved things (245c)."
limits: The observation notes the explicit statement of required conditions and a starting hypothesis, not whether the argument succeeds or whether this definition of soul is complete.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0114
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245b-245c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245b-245c
  start_marker: 245b
  end_marker: 245c
  start_char: 35786
  end_char: 36693
  text_sha256: 282c382b7947f3cb3e115e6cedc2ff8e46728272498856a9611cb9f84aa58cf5
greek_terms:
  - ψυχὴ πᾶσα ἀθάνατος
  - τὸ αὑτὸ κινοῦν
  - πηγὴ καὶ ἀρχὴ κινήσεως
english_gloss: all soul is immortal, the self-moving, source and beginning of motion
observation: Socrates deduces soul's immortality from the premise that the self-moving, as the source and beginning of motion, can never cease moving, and identifies this as the starting principle of the proof that erotic madness is a divine blessing.
textual_basis: "At 245c Socrates states that all soul is immortal, then reasons: the ever-moving is immortal; what is moved by another ceases motion and ceases life; only the self-moving, since it never abandons itself, never ceases moving, and is the source (pege) and beginning (arche) of motion for all other moved things (245c)."
limits: This observation records a form-trajectory claim about soul's immortality as a first principle. It does not evaluate whether the inference is valid or whether it constitutes a form-based argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0115
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 246a-246b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 246a-246b
  start_marker: 246a
  end_marker: 246b
  start_char: 37529
  end_char: 38343
  text_sha256: 6edf1eea813f07d10609bbfe713866411089683d185160f302c76c17c78e91a1
greek_terms:
  - συμφύτῳ δυνάμει ὑποπτέρου ζεύγους τε καὶ ἡνιόχου
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 246a, Socrates marks a shift from a proof about the soul's immortality to an account of the soul's form, explicitly distinguishing between what the soul is (hoion men esti), which would require a long divine narration, and what it is like (hoi de eoiken), which can be told in a shorter human account. He then introduces the composite image of a winged chariot-team with a charioteer as what the soul resembles.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates states that describing what the soul is would be a matter of a divine and long narration (theias kai makras diegeseos), while what it resembles admits a human and shorter one (anthropines te kai elattonos). On this basis he proposes the likeness of a winged team and charioteer (246a). The image is introduced as an explicit likeness (eoiketo), not as a doctrinal assertion about the soul's essence.
limits: |
  This observation records the demarcation between argument and mythic image at the pivot point 246a. It does not claim that the entire subsequent passage is myth, nor does it assess the truth-value of the image. The observation also does not analyze the prior immortality argument in detail.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0116
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 246a-246b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 246a-246b
  start_marker: 246a
  end_marker: 246b
  start_char: 37529
  end_char: 38343
  text_sha256: 6edf1eea813f07d10609bbfe713866411089683d185160f302c76c17c78e91a1
greek_terms:
  - θεῶν μὲν οὖν ἵπποι τε καὶ ἡνίοχοι πάντες αὐτοί τε ἀγαθοὶ καὶ ἐξ ἀγαθῶν
  - τὸ δὲ τῶν ἄλλων μέμεικται
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Within the chariot-team image introduced at 246a, Socrates draws an immediate asymmetry between divine and non-divine souls. The horses and charioteers of the gods are all good and from good stock. By contrast, in the case of the others (to de ton allon), the team is mixed: one horse is noble and good, the other is of opposite stock and disposition (246a-246b).
textual_basis: |
  At 246a, the gods' teams are described as all good and from good stock. At 246b, the mixed condition of other souls is introduced, and the human charioteer's pair is specified: one horse kalos te kai agathos kai ek toiouton, the other ex enantion te kai enantios. The charioteering is therefore necessarily difficult and troublesome (chalepē dē kai dyskolos ex anankēs hē peri hēmas hēniochesiōs).
limits: |
  This observation records the structural asymmetry built into the chariot image, not a theological claim about the actual nature of gods. It does not address the later cosmological myth or the fate of souls.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0117
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 246a-246b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 246a-246b
  start_marker: 246a
  end_marker: 246b
  start_char: 37529
  end_char: 38343
  text_sha256: 6edf1eea813f07d10609bbfe713866411089683d185160f302c76c17c78e91a1
greek_terms:
  - ψυχὴ πᾶσα παντὸς ἐπιμελεῖται τοῦ ἀψύχου
  - πάντα δὲ οὐρανὸν περιπολεῖ
  - ἄλλοτ’ ἐν ἄλλοις εἴδεσι γιγνομένη
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 246b, after the chariot image is introduced, Socrates states that all soul cares for all that is soulless, traverses the whole heaven, and comes to be at different times in different forms. This claim about the soul's cosmic range and its manifestation in multiple forms sits immediately adjacent to the mythic image, blending cosmological assertion with the likeness-based account.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 246b says that every soul cares for all soulless things (psychē pāsa pantos epimeleitai tou apsychou), traverses all heaven (panta de ouranon peripolei), and comes to be at different times in different forms (allot' en allois eidesi gignomenē). The use of eidesi (forms/shapes) connects the soul's manifestation to form-language without yet introducing the separated Forms.
limits: |
  This observation notes the proximity of form-language to the mythic image. It does not claim that the passage asserts a theory of Forms, nor does it treat eidē here as equivalent to the separated Forms of other dialogues. The observation is limited to the text at 246b.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0120
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 246c-246d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 246c-246d
  start_marker: 246c
  end_marker: 246d
  start_char: 38343
  end_char: 39168
  text_sha256: bb16dfb5b24cdc05a5c5be8234332da9ea62009086072fd20e5fd63ab887562e
greek_terms:
  - πτεροῦ δύναμις
  - ἐμβριθὲς ἄγειν ἄνω
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 246c-246d, within the soul-chariot myth, the power of the wing (pterou dynamis) is described as having the nature to lead what is heavy upward (to embrithes agein ano), elevating it toward where the race of gods dwells. The wing's function is given a quasi-physical explanatory role within the mythic framework.
textual_basis: "The text states: \"the power of the wing is by nature to lead the heavy upward, elevating it to where the race of gods dwells\" (pephuken he pterou dynamis to embrithes agein ano meteorizousa hei to ton theon genos oikei). This provides a mechanism within the myth for the soul's ascent."
limits: This is a description of a mythic mechanism, not a dialectical argument. The claim that wings lift the heavy upward is part of the imaginative apparatus of the chariot myth, not a literal physical account.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0121
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 244a-244b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 244a-244b
  start_marker: 244a
  end_marker: 244b
  start_char: 33424
  end_char: 34280
  text_sha256: cdf9d3032f957485764c6277907231181bc48cab457b525cd52f80ae56be268a
greek_terms:
  - μανίαν
  - μανίας
  - μανεῖσαι
english_gloss: madness, madness, divine gift, having been mad
observation: |
  At 244a-b, Socrates introduces a palinode speech that reclassifies madness (mania) from an unqualified evil to a source of the greatest goods when given by divine gift. He names the Delphic prophetess and the priestesses at Dodona as institutional examples who accomplished many fine things for Greece while mad, but little or nothing when in their right mind.
textual_basis: |
  The passage opens by framing the forthcoming speech as a recantation attributed to Stesichorus (244a). It then states the argumentative pivot: "if it were simply that madness is an evil, that would be well said; but now the greatest goods come to us through madness, that is, when given by divine gift" (244a). Two specific oracular institutions —the prophetess at Delphi and the priestesses at Dodona— are cited as evidence that madness in a possessed state produced great public and private benefits while sobriety produced little or nothing (244a-b).
limits: |
  The observation records the classification move and the examples invoked, not an argumentative assessment of whether the evidence supports the reclassification. The passage names a divine-madness taxonomy but does not yet define the kinds of madness, which occurs later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0122
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 246e-247a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - μέγας ἡγεμὼν ἐν οὐρανῷ Ζεύς
  - στρατιὰ θεῶν τε καὶ δαιμόνων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a bounded mythic image of the supra-heavenly realm: Zeus as great leader in heaven driving a winged chariot, followed by an army of gods and daimons arranged in eleven divisions.
textual_basis: |
  At 246e-247a, Socrates marks the transition with an explicit naming of Zeus as the commander, followed by a structured military-religious order of gods and daimons moving through the heavens. The description is coherent, pictorial, and framed as a discrete narrative segment.
limits: |
  The observation does not interpret the allegorical meaning of the chariot or the division of gods; it only records that a bounded mythic image is introduced here.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0125

  text_sha256: c0d4ee9288f64678c38a6616763aed222d92232160fe6f0a09b541597af420ff
greek_terms:
  - ἀφοσιώσωμαι
english_gloss: Socrates reports his daimonion sign as an inner voice that restrains him from departing until he has purified himself of an offense against the divine.
observation: Socrates describes his customary divine sign (daimonion) intervening to prevent him from leaving until he has performed a ritual purification for an offense against the divine, occasioned by having just delivered his first speech against eros.
textual_basis: At 242c Socrates states that his sign always restrains him from something he is about to do, then reports hearing a voice that forbids him to depart before he purifies himself for having erred against the divine.
limits: The passage describes a reported inner experience, not a visible divine epiphany. It does not specify whether the sign endorses or rejects any particular philosophical thesis about eros beyond prompting ritual purification.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0126

  end_char: 31087
  text_sha256: c0d4ee9288f64678c38a6616763aed222d92232160fe6f0a09b541597af420ff
greek_terms:
  - δεινόν
  - εὐήθη
  - ἀσεβῆ
  - Ἀφροδίτης
english_gloss: Socrates characterizes his first speech and Lysias's speech as impious offenses against the god Eros, who is a divine being or son of Aphrodite.
observation: Socrates characterizes both Lysias's speech and his own first speech as terrible, simple-minded, and impious, framing his forthcoming palinode as a correction of a religious offense against Eros, whom he identifies as a god or son of Aphrodite.
textual_basis: At 242c-d Socrates calls the speech terrible and impious, then presses Phaedrus to affirm that Eros is a child of Aphrodite and a god, which Phaedrus concedes in the exchange at 242d. This frames the palinode as a purification for impiety toward Eros.
limits: The observation notes the procedural framing of the palinode as a religious correction. It does not assess whether the second speech is genuinely pious or whether the characterization of the first speech as impious is consistent with its content.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - Ἴβυκον
  - ἐδυσωπούμην
  - ἔθραξε
english_gloss: Socrates quotes Ibycus to express the fear that he may have erred against the gods and thereby lose honor among mortals, describing himself as already feeling shame while delivering the earlier speech.
observation: Socrates reports that while he was delivering his first speech he already felt inner disturbance and shame, and he quotes the lyric poet Ibycus to articulate his fear of offending the gods and thereby losing honor among human beings.
textual_basis: At 242c-d Socrates says that something troubled him while speaking and he felt shame in the manner of Ibycus, then quotes the Ibycus line about fearing lost honor before men for having stumbled before the gods.
limits: The observation records the use of a poetic quotation as an authority for a religious-moral scruple. It does not assess whether the Ibycus fragment is contextually apt or whether Socrates's self-diagnosis is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0128
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245d-245e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245d-245e
  start_marker: 245d
  end_marker: 245e
  start_char: 36693
  end_char: 37529
  text_sha256: 64545d158e6a84b677945b02f8ee5d52c54f59662ad454a3fe6cf04e577de04c
greek_terms:
  - ἀρχὴ
  - ἀγένητον
  - ἀδιάφθορον
  - τὸ αὐτὸ αὑτὸ κινοῦν
  - ἀθανάτου
  - ψυχῆς οὐσίαν
  - λόγον
english_gloss: first principle, ungenerated, indestructible, the self-moving, immortal, essence of soul, account
observation: Socrates argues in a continuous speech that the soul is immortal by identifying it with the self-moving principle. He establishes that a first principle (archê) must be ungenerated (agênêton) and indestructible (adiaphthoron), that the self-moving is the archê of motion, and that the self-moving is therefore immortal — then declares that this self-moving is the essence (ousia) and account (logos) of soul.
textual_basis: At 245d Socrates states that the first principle must be ungenerated and, because ungenerated, indestructible; he identifies the self-moving as the source (archê) of motion at 245d-e and concludes at 245e that since the self-moving has been shown immortal, anyone who says this is the essence and account of soul will not be ashamed.
limits: The observation records only that the text presents a proof that soul is immortal from the self-motion premise. It does not assess whether the proof is valid, whether it is consistent with other Platonic arguments, or whether Socrates intends it as a definitive or provisional claim.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0129
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 245d-245e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 245d-245e
  start_marker: 245d
  end_marker: 245e
  start_char: 36693
  end_char: 37529
  text_sha256: 64545d158e6a84b677945b02f8ee5d52c54f59662ad454a3fe6cf04e577de04c
greek_terms:
  - ἄψυχον
  - ἔμψυχον
  - ἔξωθεν
  - ἔνδοθεν
  - ψυχῆς
english_gloss: soulless, ensouled, from outside, from within, of soul
observation: "Socrates defines soul through the criterion of self-motion: a body moved from outside is soulless (apsychon), while a body moved from within by itself is ensouled (empsychon). This definition is presented as the nature (physis) of soul and is used to ground the preceding immortality argument."
textual_basis: "At 245e Socrates states: 'every body which is moved from outside is soulless, but that which is moved from within from itself is ensouled, since this is the nature of soul.' The empsychon/apsychon contrast turns the self-motion principle into a definitional criterion for soul."
limits: The observation records only that a definition of soul by internal versus external motion is stated. It does not evaluate whether this definition is successfully proved, whether it accounts for all Greek notions of psuchê, or whether subsequent arguments in the dialogue modify it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0130

greek_terms:
  - θεσμός τε Ἀδραστείας
  - πτερορρυήσῃ
  - νόμος
  - μὴ φυτεῦσαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces the ordinance of Adrasteia governing the soul's safety, wing-loss, fall to earth, and first-generation placement after failing to follow the divine circuit.
textual_basis: At 248c the speech names the ordinance of Adrasteia, states that a soul that sees something true remains unharmed, and then describes the failing soul as filled with forgetfulness and badness, grown heavy, shedding wings, and falling to earth. At 248d the law begins by excluding first-generation planting into an animal nature.
limits: This observation records the mythic legal frame for the soul's fall and first placement; the full ranked catalogue of human lives is recorded separately.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0131
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 248d-248e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 248d-248e
  start_marker: 248d
  end_marker: 248e
  start_char: 42764
  end_char: 43522
  text_sha256: 62393e6b242c250b1aa84204f9db1911c1efd2fe0e5d9bfc775dffc3b2170b28
greek_terms:
  - πλεῖστα ἰδοῦσαν
  - δευτέραν
  - τρίτην
  - τετάρτην
  - πέμπτην
  - ἕκτῃ
  - ἑβδόμῃ
  - ὀγδόῃ
  - ἐνάτῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The passage constructs a nine-tier ordered ladder of births in descending rank, each tier named by a life-type: philosopher, lawful king, statesman, craftsman/athlete, seer, poet, manual worker, sophist, tyrant. The ordering is explicitly by how much intelligible truth the soul saw before falling."
textual_basis: "At 248d-248e the catalogue lists the lives in descending order: the soul that saw the most enters a philosophic or beauty-loving life, followed by lawful king, statesman or manager, trainer or healer, seer or initiator, poet or imitator, craftsman or farmer, sophist or demagogue, and tyrant. The ordinal sequence marks the catalogue as ranked."
limits: This does not confirm that the nine-step ladder is a definition of the soul or a metaphysical ordering; it records only that the catalogue is structured as a downward ordinal ranking. The passage does not claim that the lower tiers are excluded from salvation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0132
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 247d-247e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 247d-247e
  start_marker: 247d
  end_marker: 247e
  start_char: 40767
  end_char: 41478
  text_sha256: 97f197fce332241bfd24ab0291aadcf32981967e337002244130922ac907d31e
greek_terms:
  - αὐτὴν δικαιοσύνην
  - σωφροσύνην
  - ἐπιστήμην
  - οὐχ ᾗ γένεσις πρόσεστιν
  - τὴν ἐν τῷ ὅ ἐστιν ὂν ὄντως ἐπιστήμην οὖσαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: In the account of the hyperouranian region (247d-247e), the soul that follows a god beholds justice itself, temperance itself, and knowledge itself — specifically knowledge that is not involved with coming-to-be and is not relative to particular beings, but knowledge that is in that which really is being. The vision is presented as a cyclical periodic feast in which the soul gazes upon true beings and is nourished.
textual_basis: The passage at 247d-e enumerates three objects of vision (justice, temperance, knowledge), explicitly distinguishes the knowledge seen there from knowledge tied to genesis and from knowledge that is in a different thing (247d-247e), and locates it in 'that which really is' (to ho estin on ontos, 247e). This pattern of beholding a small set of forms in a transcendent place matches the forms_trajectory family.
limits: The passage does not use the term eidos or idea at this point. It does not enumerate a full set of forms, only three. It does not discuss participation or separation vocabulary beyond the ontological contrast it draws.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0133
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 247d-247e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 247d-247e
  start_marker: 247d
  end_marker: 247e
  start_char: 40767
  end_char: 41478
  text_sha256: 97f197fce332241bfd24ab0291aadcf32981967e337002244130922ac907d31e
greek_terms:
  - ἀμβροσίαν
  - νέκταρ
  - ἑστιαθεῖσα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The supercelestial vision sequence closes with a mythical nourishment image: after the soul has feasted upon true beings (hestiatheisa, 247e), the charioteer returns home, stables the horses, and feeds them ambrosia with nectar poured upon it (247e). The vocabulary of divine food items (ambrosia, nectar) and feasting marks this as a bounded mythical element capping the ontological vision."
textual_basis: At 247e the soul's return from the hyperouranian vision is immediately followed by the domestic image of the charioteer stabling the horses and dispensing ambrosia and nectar. The verb hestiatheisa (feasted) links the intellectual vision of forms to a feeding metaphor, and the mythological foodstuffs ambrosia and nectar are standard markers of divine / mythical discourse in the Greek tradition.
limits: This observation notes the mythical closure device, not an argument that the forms content is itself mythical. The passage is not making an explicit myth/logos hedge here; the myth boundary is implicit in the vocabulary register shift.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0134
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 247d-247e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 247d-247e
  start_marker: 247d
  end_marker: 247e
  start_char: 40767
  end_char: 41478
  text_sha256: 97f197fce332241bfd24ab0291aadcf32981967e337002244130922ac907d31e
greek_terms:
  - δικαιοσύνην
  - σωφροσύνην
  - ἐπιστήμην
english_gloss: ""
observation: "The vision of forms at 247d-e uses a tripartite anaphoric structure: 'it sees justice itself, it sees temperance, it sees knowledge' (kathorai men... kathorai de... kathorai de..., 247d). The repeated verb with accumulating objects imposes a rhetorical turn pattern within what is otherwise a continuous mythological exposition, marking the enumeration as a formal highlight."
textual_basis: The threefold kathorai at 247d, each introducing a new object of vision, creates a structured rhetorical sequence before the qualifying clause about knowledge not being tied to genesis. This anaphoric turn geometry stands out against the surrounding narrative flow of the palinode.
limits: This is a formal observation about rhetorical structure within a mythical passage, not about the dialogic turn-taking between characters. It does not claim that the enumeration proves any doctrinal point.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0135
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 244c-244d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 244c-244d
  start_marker: 244c
  end_marker: 244d
  start_char: 34280
  end_char: 35108
  text_sha256: bedd1907ea1b10ae1cee682f50f423d6fb230833ad8ba54fa9777046fa0f92aa
greek_terms:
  - τὸ ταῦ
  - ἀπειροκάλως
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates argues that the ancients connected the name of the prophetic art directly to madness (mania), calling it manike, and that contemporary people tastelessly inserted a tau to produce mantike, obscuring the original link between divine madness and prophecy.
textual_basis: At 244c Socrates states that the ancients would not have embroidered the name of the finest art (tei kallistei technei) with the very word manike if they thought madness was shameful; instead they named it as something beautiful when it comes by divine lot. He then says that present-day people tastelessly (apeirokalos) insert the tau and call it mantike.
limits: This observation records the etymological argument as a textual move; it does not assess whether the etymology is historically accurate or whether Socrates endorses it beyond the rhetorical needs of the palinode.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0136
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 244c-244d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 244c-244d
  start_marker: 244c
  end_marker: 244d
  start_char: 34280
  end_char: 35108
  text_sha256: bedd1907ea1b10ae1cee682f50f423d6fb230833ad8ba54fa9777046fa0f92aa
greek_terms:
  - ὅσῳ
  - τόσῳ
  - τελεώτερον
  - ἐντιμότερον
  - κάλλιον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates deploys a proportional comparative formula (hoso…toso) to rank divine madness above human sanity, using the relative standing of prophetic arts as an analogue: just as mantike is more complete and more honored than oionistike, so by the same measure divine madness surpasses human self-control."
textual_basis: "At 244c-244d, the clause introduced by hoso de oun (to the degree that) and answered by toso (by that same degree) establishes a direct proportionality: the superiority of mantike over oionistike in name and deed maps onto the superiority of god-given madness over merely human sophrosyne."
limits: This observation identifies the formal proportional structure; it does not claim that the argument is logically valid or that the analogy between prophetic arts and mental states is developed elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0137
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 244c-244d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 244c-244d
  start_marker: 244c
  end_marker: 244d
  start_char: 34280
  end_char: 35108
  text_sha256: bedd1907ea1b10ae1cee682f50f423d6fb230833ad8ba54fa9777046fa0f92aa
greek_terms:
  - μαρτυροῦσιν
  - σωφροσύνης
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates casts the ancients (hoi palaioi) in the role of witnesses (marturousin) who testify on behalf of divine madness, treating their naming practices as evidence that god-given mania is more beautiful than human sophrosyne.
textual_basis: At 244c-244d the verb marturousin (they bear witness) has hoi palaioi as its subject, and what they witness to is that mania from a god is kallion (more beautiful) than sophrosyne from human sources. The argument rests on the authority of ancient naming conventions rather than on deductive proof.
limits: This observation notes the appeal to ancient authority as a textual strategy; it does not evaluate whether Socrates is being ironic or whether the appeal is consistent with his treatment of tradition elsewhere.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The supercelestial being (ousia) is described through three negations of sensory qualities: it is without color (achromatos), without shape (aschematistos), and intangible (anaphes).
textual_basis: |
  At 247c the text characterizes the hyperouranios topos as "colorless and shapeless and intangible being, really being" and states it is visible only to intellect, the pilot of the soul (psyches kybernetei mono theate no).
limits: |
  This passage does not specify whether these negations apply to all forms or only to the being contemplated in the supercelestial place; it is a localized description within the chariot myth.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  The speaker pauses before describing the supercelestial place to insert a hedging remark: "one must dare to speak the truth, especially when speaking about truth."
textual_basis: |
  At 247c the text inserts the parenthetical remark "tolmeteon gar oun to ge alethes eipein, allos te kai peri aletheias legonta" before launching into the description of colorless, shapeless, intangible being.
limits: |
  This hedge only marks self-consciousness about the adequacy of the account; it does not indicate whether the ensuing description is meant as myth, dialectic, or some hybrid mode.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0141
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 248e-249a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - πτεροῦται
  - φιλοσοφήσαντος ἀδόλως
  - παιδεραστήσαντος μετὰ φιλοσοφίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates states that souls do not regrow their wings before ten thousand years, with a single exception: the soul of one who has practiced philosophy without guile or has loved boys with philosophy. These exceptional souls can be re-winged after three thousand years if they choose this life three times in succession."
textual_basis: At 248e-249a Socrates narrows the general rule (no return before ten thousand years) to a privileged class defined by philosophy or philosophic pederasty, then further restricts that class to those who choose the philosophical life three consecutive times. The vocabulary of wing-growth links the return-time to a soul-form condition.
limits: This observation records the soul-return timetable exception, not a doctrine of reincarnation mechanics. It does not claim that the exception constitutes a full theory of forms, only that a special soul condition (philosophic life) alters the cosmic cycle.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - κρίσεως ἔτυχον
  - δικαιωτήρια
  - Δίκης
  - ἀνθρώπου εἴδει
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates describes a post-mortem judgment in which some souls go to places of punishment under the earth while others are lifted by Dike to a heavenly place and live worthily of the life they lived in human form.
textual_basis: At 249a the passage pivots from the exceptional philosophic soul to the other souls. These undergo judgment and are assigned to either underground places of justice or a heavenly region by Dike. The language is judicial and topographic.
limits: This record notes the post-mortem judgment bifurcation. It does not claim that the topography is systematic or that the judgment criteria are fully specified here; the passage sketches the alternatives without detailing each.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0143
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 248a-248b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 248a-248b
  start_marker: 248a
  end_marker: 248b
  start_char: 41478
  end_char: 42324
  text_sha256: a934f9c5d4852e20b6b8f800e48d353072d26c626feea4f77cb952eb1cd3f9e6
greek_terms:
  - ὑπερῆρεν
  - ἔξω τόπον
  - συμπεριηνέχθη
  - περιφορά
  - καθορῶσα τὰ ὄντα
  - τροφῇ δοξαστῇ
  - εἰκασμένη
  - μόγις
  - ὑποβρύχιαι
  - ἀτελεῖς τῆς τοῦ ὄντος θέας
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The passage at 248a-248b presents a graded hierarchy of souls in relation to the vision of being: some souls, following the god closely, raise the charioteer's head into the region beyond the heavens and are carried around in the revolution, seeing beings with difficulty; others rise and sink, seeing some beings and not others; still others, striving upward but unable, are swept along below the surface, trampling one another. All depart uninitiated in the vision of being and feed on opinion-nourishment.
textual_basis: |
  At 248a the best soul follows the god and is likened to it, raising the charioteer's head into the "place beyond" and being carried around in the revolution, seeing beings with difficulty. The second tier rises and sinks, seeing some beings and not others. The third tier follows but is unable, being swept under and trampling one another. All depart "uninitiated in the vision of being" and afterward feed on "opinion-nourishment." The passage culminates with reference to the "plain of truth" where the fitting pasture for the best part of the soul is found (248b).
limits: |
  The observation reports the graded soul hierarchy and the language of vision, being, and truth-nourishment at 248a-248b. It does not claim that this passage establishes a formal doctrine of soul-types or interprets the eschatological implications. It does not address the mythic frame within which this passage is embedded.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0144

  text_sha256: a934f9c5d4852e20b6b8f800e48d353072d26c626feea4f77cb952eb1cd3f9e6
greek_terms:
  - θόρυβος
  - ἅμιλλα
  - ἱδρὼς ἔσχατος
  - κακίᾳ ἡνιόχων
  - χωλεύονται
  - πτερὰ θραύονται
  - ἀτελεῖς τῆς τοῦ ὄντος θέας
  - τροφῇ δοξαστῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 248a-248b the chariot-image continues with a vivid account of competitive struggle among souls: uproar, rivalry, and extreme sweat arise, and through the badness of charioteers many souls are lamed and many break their wings. The passage describes the souls' collective failure and their subsequent reliance on opinion-nourishment, framed within the ongoing myth of the soul's cosmic journey.
textual_basis: |
  At 248a-b the text describes the chaos and competition among the souls who cannot rise: uproar and rivalry and extreme sweat. The cause is identified as the badness of charioteers, resulting in many being lamed and many breaking many wings. The souls all depart uninitiated in the vision of being and feed on opinion-nourishment. The extended chariot and wing imagery operates within the mythic narrative introduced earlier in the palinode.
limits: |
  This observation notes the vivid competitive-struggle imagery and the charioteer-defect cause within the mythic frame. It does not claim the passage as an esoteric doctrine, does not interpret the specific number of soul-tiers, and does not address the philosophical argument for which the myth serves as vehicle.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - μνήμῃ κεχαρίσθω
  - πόθῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly transitions from the mythic account of the blessed pre-incarnate vision to the subsequent discussion of beauty, marking the preceding passage as a favor granted to memory and explaining that longing for those things made the speech longer.
textual_basis: At 250c-250d, Socrates says 'let these things be a favor to memory' and explains that 'because of longing for those things, it has now been spoken at greater length.' This self-conscious marking separates the mythic recollection from the analytical treatment of beauty that follows.
limits: This observation records only the explicit boundary marker; it does not assess whether the mythic content is fully separable from what follows or whether the transition is rhetorically complete.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 250c, Socrates describes souls as pure and unmarked by the body, adding that they are 'bound fast in the manner of an oyster.' The image of the oyster shell functions as a binding or imprisoning container for the soul.
limits: This observation records the oyster-shell binding image as a specific instance of body-imprisonment imagery; it does not adjudicate whether this image aligns with or differs from other body-as-prison images elsewhere in the corpus.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0147
source_work: Phaedrus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 47189
  text_sha256: dc016317482bdfa52adeae4b147566f92b3103b9542871a871a27ed6aca3495b
greek_terms:
  - φρόνησις οὐχ ὁρᾶται
  - κάλλος μόνον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Beauty is described as having a unique privilege among intelligible realities: it shone among the forms and is now grasped through the sharpest sense (sight), while wisdom is not visible and would inspire terrible loves if it produced a clear image for sight."
textual_basis: "At 250d, Socrates states that beauty shone among the forms and is grasped through the clearest sense. He contrasts: wisdom is not seen, for it would produce terrible loves if it provided such a clear image coming into sight. Beauty alone has this privilege of being most manifest to sight."
limits: This observation records the specific contrast between beauty's sensible accessibility and wisdom's invisibility within the broader forms trajectory; it does not claim that this contrast is unique to the Phaedrus or that it represents Plato's settled view.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0148
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 249b-249c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 249b-249c
  start_marker: 249b
  end_marker: 249c
  start_char: 43976
  end_char: 44814
  text_sha256: 047ece94ee8ae5f5dbd4d617f0732cc676704f474de803a4b67b84723dd02658
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: to understand what is said according to form, gathering together into one through reasoning what comes from many perceptions
observation: |
  At 249b-c Socrates defines the human capacity for recollection as comprehending what is spoken according to form, gathering together into one through reasoning what comes from many perceptions. This is the same passage that declares only the philosopher's thought grows wings. The definition makes the cognitive act of unifying perceptibles through logos a necessary condition for incarnation in human shape.
textual_basis: |
  The Greek at 249b-c reads: dei gar anthropon synienai kat' eidos legomenon, ek pollon ion aistheseon eis hen logismoi synairoumenon (for a human must understand what is said according to form, going from many perceptions and being gathered together into one by reasoning). This is then equated with anamnesis.
limits: |
  The passage describes the cognitive mechanism but does not catalogue which forms are accessible through this procedure. It ties the capacity to human incarnation as such, not only to philosophical achievement.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἀρτιτελής
english_gloss: newly initiated, corrupted, just-initiated
observation: Within the palinode myth, Socrates divides souls into two types — the uninitiated or corrupted and the newly initiated — and assigns each a distinct erotic response to beauty.
textual_basis: The passage at 250e-251a structures the entire description around a men...de contrast between the two soul types, with the corrupted soul pursuing pleasure in a bestial manner and the initiated soul experiencing shudder, fear, and reverence.
limits: This observation does not claim that the binary is exhaustive or that it maps onto other Platonic soul taxonomies. It only notes the textual structure within this mythic passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "Form-language appears within the palinode myth: beauty itself is distinguished from its earthly namesake, and the beloved's face is described as having beauty well imitated."
textual_basis: At 250e, Socrates says the corrupted soul does not move from here to there toward beauty itself but looks at its earthly namesake. At 251a, the newly initiated sees a godlike face with beauty well imitated, using mimesis vocabulary characteristic of form-particular relations.
limits: This observation does not claim that the palinode presents a systematic theory of forms. It only notes the presence of form-associated vocabulary within the mythic narrative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 251a, Socrates uses explicit religious vocabulary — reveres, would sacrifice, cult statue — to characterize the erotic response of the newly initiated soul to the beloved.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0152

  - ἀγανάκτησις
  - γαργαλίζεται
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 251c, Socrates compares the soul's experience of growing wings to the physical sensations of teething: itching, irritation, and tingling around the gums."
textual_basis: The text explicitly introduces the comparison with 'just as the experience of those teething happens around the teeth when they first grow' (251c), then applies the same verbs — seethes, is irritated, tingles — to the soul growing wings. The analogy maps a familiar bodily process onto an invisible psychic event.
limits: This observation records the analogy's presence and structure. It does not claim that the teething comparison is medically accurate or that it exhausts the description of erotic experience in the passage.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 251c, Socrates offers an etymology of 'himeros' (longing/desire), deriving it from 'parts' (mere) that 'go' (ienai) from the beloved to the lover.
textual_basis: The text states that particles flow from the boy's beauty and 'for this reason it is called himeros' (251c). The etymology breaks himeros into mere (parts) + ienai (going), linking the name to the flowing-particle mechanics of the passage.
limits: This records an in-text etymology, not a claim about actual Greek linguistic history. It does not assess whether the etymology is philologically sound or merely a Socratic wordplay.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation records the mythic assertion within its narrative frame. It does not assert that Socrates or Plato holds this as a doctrinal claim, nor does it evaluate the claim's truth or philosophical status.
```

```yaml
  - ἐκπλήττονται
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates grades souls by the adequacy of their memory of the Forms, claiming that only a few retain sufficient recollection while most have fallen into forgetfulness of the sacred things they once saw.
textual_basis: At 250a, Socrates states that recollection from things here to the things there is not easy for every soul, distinguishing those who saw the Forms only briefly and those who fell here and were turned toward injustice through certain associations so that they have forgetfulness (lethe) of the sacred things. He then says only a few remain for whom memory is sufficiently present.
limits: Records the graded-recollection claim at 250a. Does not address whether the same grading of souls by memory appears elsewhere in the Phaedrus or in other Platonic dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: The soul's prenatal vision of the Forms is cast in the vocabulary of mystery-cult initiation, using the verb for being initiated and the noun for sacred rites.
textual_basis: At 250b, Socrates says the souls saw and were initiated into the mysteries, using the aorist passive of teleo (eteleounto) and the noun telete (ton teleton), which are standard terms for mystery-cult initiation. This frames philosophical vision of the Forms in religious initiation language.
limits: Records the presence of mystery-initiation vocabulary at 250b. Does not claim that the whole palinode is structured as a mystery discourse or that Plato endorses mystery cults as philosophical models.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0157
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 250a-250b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 46362
  text_sha256: dafdd61e8a8b4e793619a6b44164b8f3ec6e093a44ef31745fb5b64e7941a377
greek_terms:
  - κάλλος
  - δικαιοσύνης
  - σωφροσύνης
  - φέγγος
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates distinguishes beauty from justice and moderation by claiming that beauty alone was bright in the prenatal vision and retains perceptibility in earthly likenesses, while the other Forms have no brightness in their images here.
textual_basis: At 250b, Socrates says that justice, moderation, and other things honored by souls have no brightness (phengos) in the likenesses here, and that only a few, through dim organs, behold the kind of the thing imaged. He then contrasts this with beauty, which was bright to see then when souls followed the gods in the blessed chorus.
limits: Records the special status of beauty among the Forms at 250b. Does not claim that Plato consistently privileges beauty in this way across all dialogues or that the claim applies to every Form.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0158
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 251d-251e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 251d-251e
  start_marker: 251d
  end_marker: 251e
  start_char: 48713
  end_char: 49450
  text_sha256: 3ea4f0e7ca28d89a2c67998ed7546f30a023ef77c09befa71b2d83743db4db05
greek_terms:
  - βλάστην τοῦ πτεροῦ
english_gloss: wing, sprout/growth of the wing, passages
observation: |
  The soul's desire for the beautiful is described through a sustained physiological metaphor of wing-growth. When the soul is near the beloved, the passages where the wing strives to grow are irrigated and the wing sprouts; when separated, the mouth of each passage dries up, closes, and shuts out the sprout of the wing.
textual_basis: |
  At 251d, the text describes the soul's oscillation: when present with the beloved, the passages (Greek: diexodoi) are moistened and the wing sprouts; when apart and dry, the mouths of these passages close and shut out the sprout of the wing. The metaphor treats the wing as something organic that grows, withers, and can be blocked, mapping erotic desire onto a quasi-biological process.
limits: |
  Does not establish that the wing is a Form or that the wing participates in a Form; the passage uses wing-growth as an image for the soul's erotic condition, not as explicit form-language about essence or participation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

observation: "At 252c Socrates quotes a hexameter line distinguishing mortal and immortal names for Eros: mortals call him Eros (winged), gods call him Pteros (Winged-one), because of his wing-growing necessity."
textual_basis: The verse is introduced as a quotation and the speaker marks it as optional belief ("one may be persuaded or not"), but then states that the cause and experience of lovers is nonetheless that very thing. The naming distinction mirrors the divine/human naming convention seen elsewhere.
```

```yaml
  - τιμῶν

  - κατακοσμεῖ
english_gloss: honoring, imitating, statue/image, fashions, adorns
observation: At 252c-d Socrates describes each lover living by honoring and imitating the god of whose chorus he was a follower, and fashioning and adorning the beloved "like a statue" (agalma) as if the beloved were that god himself.
textual_basis: 'The sequence in 252d moves from cosmic following (choreutes) to imitation (mimoumenos) of the specific god, to a craft image: the lover selects a beloved "after his own manner" and then "fashions and adorns" him like a cult statue (agalma). This maps a craft domain (tektainesthai, katakosmein — carpentry/fashioning, adorning) onto the erotic-psychological domain.'
limits: Does not assert that this is the only craft analogy in the palinode; only that the statue-making language appears here as a distinct craft mapping.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 252c-d erotic character is differentiated by the god a soul followed in the pre-natal cosmic circuit. Followers of Zeus bear the burden of the "winged-named one" more heavily, while followers of Ares become murderous toward the beloved when they think themselves wronged; each lover lives by imitating his own god.
textual_basis: "252c specifies the differing reactions of Zeus-followers and Ares-followers (embritheteron pherein vs. phonikoi). 252d generalizes the principle: each lover lives honoring and imitating whichever god he followed as a choral dancer (choreutes) in the pre-natal circuit."
limits: This observation records the mythic differentiation of erotic types by divine following; it does not claim that this is the only place in the dialogue where such typology occurs (the charioteer myth earlier in the palinode also organizes souls by divine retinue).
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0167
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 253d-253e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 253d-253e
  start_marker: 253d
  end_marker: 253e
  start_char: 52785
  end_char: 53528
  text_sha256: a0c29437531a1947eac5e69c5d4b3598d240dae85d925ea2aa028534445aa181
greek_terms:
  - μάστιγι μετὰ κέντρων μόγις ὑπείκων
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The passage elaborates the charioteer-horse pair analogy by describing the two horses' opposing natures and their differing responsiveness to guidance. The good horse (253d) obeys command and word alone; the bad horse (253e) barely yields to whip and goad; the charioteer (253e) must manage both.
textual_basis: |
  The excerpt explicitly distinguishes two horses at 253d ("of the horses, the one ... good, the other not") and describes each in terms of how it responds to the charioteer's guidance. The good horse at 253d is guided by command and word alone; the bad horse at 253e barely yields to whip and goad. This maps a chariot-driving source domain (guidance through command versus force) onto an internal governance target.
limits: |
  This observation does not address the broader chariot allegory outside 253d-253e, nor does it interpret what specific soul-parts the horses represent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0168
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 253d-253e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 253d-253e
  start_marker: 253d
  end_marker: 253e
  start_char: 52785
  end_char: 53528
  text_sha256: a0c29437531a1947eac5e69c5d4b3598d240dae85d925ea2aa028534445aa181
greek_terms:
  - ὁ μέν
  - τιμῆς ἐραστὴς μετὰ σωφροσύνης τε καὶ αἰδοῦς
  - ὕβρεως καὶ ἀλαζονείας ἑταῖρος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The passage presents a systematic point-by-point contrast between the good horse and the bad horse, pairing physical attributes (bodily form, neck shape, nose profile, skin color, eye color) with corresponding moral qualities (honor with moderation and shame versus hubris and pretension).
textual_basis: |
  The excerpt structures the contrast explicitly: "the one..." at 253d-253e is followed by "the other..." at 253e. Each attribute of the good horse has a corresponding opposite in the bad horse: straight versus crooked form, white versus dark skin, black versus grey eyes, lover of honor with moderation and shame versus companion of hubris and pretension, obedience to command versus resistance needing the whip.
limits: |
  This observation records the formal structure of the contrast, not the psychological doctrine the contrast encodes.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0169
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 253d-253e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 253d-253e
  start_marker: 253d
  end_marker: 253e

  end_char: 53528
  text_sha256: a0c29437531a1947eac5e69c5d4b3598d240dae85d925ea2aa028534445aa181
greek_terms:
  - ταῦτα μενέτω
  - νῦν δὲ λεκτέον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  The passage contains explicit discourse-management formulas that structure topic boundaries. At the start of 253d, a closing formula ("let these things remain") marks the end of a prior topic, followed by an opening formula ("now it must be said") to introduce the horse description. At the end of 253e, a pivot marker ("when therefore...") shifts from static description to the charioteer's erotic response.
textual_basis: |
  The excerpt opens at 253d with an explicit topic-closing formula ("let these things still remain for us now") followed by a topic-opening formula ("but now it must be said"). At 253e, a pivot marker ("when therefore the charioteer seeing...") shifts from static description to narrative action. These are overt verbal signals of discourse organization, not implicit transitions.
limits: |
  This observation does not assess what topic is being closed or whether the transition is complete; it only records the presence of explicit discourse-management markers.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - κακίζων
  - δειλίᾳ
  - ἀνανδρίᾳ
english_gloss: abused, reviling, cowardice, unmanliness
observation: The hubristic horse (representing the appetitive part of the soul) is given articulate speech, verbally abusing the charioteer and the good horse using moral vocabulary.
textual_basis: At 254c-d, the hubristic horse abuses the charioteer and yoke-mate in anger with articulate moral criticism, accusing them of cowardice (deilia) and unmanliness (anandria). This is direct reported speech within the mythic narrative, attributing articulate moral criticism to a figure that represents a non-rational part of the soul.
limits: This observation notes the personification technique within the myth but does not claim that Plato endorses the horse's speech content or that the appetitive part literally speaks in the soul.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0171
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 254e-255a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 254e-255a
  start_marker: 254e
  end_marker: 255a
  start_char: 55195
  end_char: 56078
  text_sha256: 52f1ebee5e0cfc4d2981338194e7dffbf1a62fc538440cd42ccc2e08e44c8dff
greek_terms:
  - ἡνίοχος
  - ὑβριστοῦ ἵππου
  - ταπεινωθεὶς
  - προνοίᾳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Within the charioteer myth, the hubristic horse is tamed through repeated violent correction by the charioteer: the charioteer pulls the bit back violently, bloodies the horse's tongue and jaws, and forces its legs to the ground, causing pain (254e). After repeated suffering, the bad horse ceases its hubris, is humbled, and follows the charioteer's forethought; when it sees the beautiful one, it is destroyed by fear (254e-255a)."
textual_basis: "The passage at 254e-255a narrates a bounded sequence within the charioteer myth: the violent correction, the horse's humiliation, and its subsequent obedience to the charioteer's forethought, using specific vocabulary of taming and governance."
limits: This observation records the narrative mechanics of the taming episode within the myth. It does not interpret what the horse or charioteer allegorically represent, nor does it assess the ethical status of violence as a method of psychic governance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0172
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 254e-255a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 254e-255a
  start_marker: 254e
  end_marker: 255a
  start_char: 55195
  end_char: 56078
  text_sha256: 52f1ebee5e0cfc4d2981338194e7dffbf1a62fc538440cd42ccc2e08e44c8dff
greek_terms:
  - ἰσόθεος
  - σχηματιζομένου
  - ἀληθῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 255a the beloved is described as receiving complete service 'as if equal to a god.' The text immediately draws an appearance/reality contrast: this service comes not from a lover who is pretending but from one who has truly experienced this."
textual_basis: The phrasing 'as if equal to a god' at 255a attributes a god-like status to the beloved within the erotic relationship. The subsequent pretending versus truly-experiencing contrast at 255a distinguishes genuine erotic experience from merely performed or feigned devotion.
limits: This observation records the godlikeness formulation and the true-versus-pretending distinction at 255a. It does not assess whether this formulation is ironic, nor does it interpret the theological implications of describing a human beloved with god-equal language.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0173
source_work: Phaedrus
```
