```yaml
  text_sha256: 78464d4a1d22edbd516d5aae3b9fa08b05e8ed3e7c40ec2d4c41d26e6ecd0b3e
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: In 256c-256d, the non-philosophical pair of lovers who succumb to physical consummation exit the body "unwinged but having started to grow wings" (apteroi men, hormekotes de pterousthai), receiving a lesser reward than the philosophical pair but still a positive post-mortem outcome from erotic madness.
textual_basis: The passage contrasts "apteroi men, hormekotes de pterousthai" at the moment of death, and states they are not sent to the dark underworld journey because they have already begun the heavenly journey. This is the second tier in a three-tier outcome structure (philosophical, honor-loving, and — earlier — the non-lover's fate).
limits: This observation does not address the full three-tier structure, only the graded pterosis language at 256c-256d. It does not claim the passage uses explicit form-language (e.g., eidos or idea), only that it employs the wing-growth trajectory vocabulary established earlier in the palinode.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: 'At 256c-256d, the text demarcates a specific eschatological law: those who have already begun the "under-the-heaven journey" are not permitted to go into darkness and the subterranean path, but instead live a luminous life in happiness together.'
textual_basis: The text states "eis gar skoton kai ten hypo ges poreian ou nomos estin eti elthein tois katergmenois ede tes hypouraniou poreias" — a law-like formula (nomos estin) that distinguishes post-mortem destinations by the soul's prior engagement with the heavenly realm.
limits: This observation notes the mythic eschatological law at 256c-256d. It does not analyze how this law relates to earlier mythic content in the palinode, nor does it claim the "law" is a philosophical doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - κατόπτρῳ
  - λέληθεν
english_gloss: mirror; escaped notice
observation: The beloved sees himself in the lover as in a mirror (katoptron) but does not recognize what he sees; the verb lelēthen (has escaped his notice) marks a gap between the experience and self-understanding.
textual_basis: At 255d, the text says the beloved, looking at himself in the lover as in a mirror (hōsper de en katoptrōi en tōi erōnti heauton horōn), does not notice (lelēthen). He is unaware of what he has undergone (oud' hoti peponthen oiden) and cannot articulate it.
limits: The mirror image is part of the palinode's mythical psychology, not a claim about all erotic experience. The observation does not assert that Socrates or Plato endorses the irony.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 255e, the text states: kalei de auton kai oietai ouk erōta alla philian einai (he calls it and thinks it is not love but friendship). This occurs immediately after the passage has described the beloved as being filled with erōs (erōtos eneplēsen, 255d) and as possessing anterōs."
```

```yaml
textual_basis: After the charioteer sees beauty on its holy pedestal at 254b, he feared, was struck with reverent awe, and fell backward. This is a bodily recoil from direct form-apprehension, not merely an intellectual or emotional response.
```

```yaml
english_gloss: philosophical life, orderly conduct
observation: |
  Socrates distinguishes the ordered philosophical path, in which the better elements of the soul win, from the more vulgar, unphilosophical and honor-loving path that follows when the pair live less strictly.
textual_basis: |
  At 256a-256b the better elements lead the pair into ordered life and philosophy; at 256c the alternative is introduced as a more vulgar, unphilosophical, honor-loving life.
```

```yaml

  - ἄτεχνος τριβή
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces personified approaching arguments (logoi) that testify rhetoric is not techne (art) but atechnos tribe (artless knack), framing the evaluation of rhetoric within the craft/knack distinction.
textual_basis: At 260e, Socrates says he seems to hear arguments approaching and protesting (diamartyromenon) that rhetoric 'is not art but artless knack' (ouk esti techne all' atechnos tribe).
limits: This observation notes the techne/tribe distinction is applied to rhetoric; it does not evaluate whether the characterization is correct.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 260e, Socrates invokes the Spartan (ho Lakon): a genuine art of speaking without grasping truth neither exists nor will ever come into being later."
limits: This records the invocation of Spartan authority; it does not assess whether the saying is authentically Laconian or whether Socrates endorses it without qualification.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 261a, Socrates asks whether rhetoric as a whole would be a certain art of soul-leading through words (techne psychagogia tis dia logon), extending its domain beyond courts and public assemblies to private gatherings (en idiois).
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0189

greek_terms:
  - μάλιστα ἐρῶσι λογογραφίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates inverts Phaedrus's claim that the powerful are ashamed of writing speeches, asserting at 257e that the most ambitious politicians are especially fond of speechwriting and even inscribe the names of their admirers first in their texts.
textual_basis: Phaedrus asserts at 257d that the powerful are ashamed to write and leave writings. Socrates counters at 257e that those politicians who think most highly of themselves are especially fond of speechwriting and add the names of those who praise them.
limits: This does not establish whether Socrates speaks ironically or sincerely about politicians' love of speechwriting, nor whether the claim applies to all politicians or only a subset.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml

textual_basis: At 257c, Phaedrus says a politician recently mocked Lysias and throughout the abuse called him a logographer. Phaedrus worries this criticism might make Lysias stop writing.
limits: This observation records the reported criticism as a dramatic datum; it does not assess whether the politician's criticism was justified or what Plato's own view of logography is.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0193
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 260a-260b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 260a-260b
  start_marker: 260a
  end_marker: 260b
  start_char: 65787
  end_char: 66661
  text_sha256: 9532a7239f21512290c0c4273366a7fe87576755944e0f6372f4a7ed8aa8d6a8
greek_terms:
  - δόξει
  - ἀληθείας
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 260a, a position attributed to 'the wise' (sophoi) is reported: the aspiring rhetorician must learn not what is truly just but what seems just to the judging multitude, and not what is truly good or noble but what will seem so, because persuasion comes from these appearances, not from truth. Socrates insists this claim must be examined rather than dismissed."
textual_basis: The reported claim at 260a explicitly contrasts the truly just goods (ta toi onti dikaia, ta ontos agatha) with what seems so to the multitude (ta doxant' an plethei, hosa doxei), and states that persuasion (to peithein) comes from the latter, not from truth (ek tes aletheias). Socrates responds that the word of the wise must not be cast aside but examined (skopein me ti legosi).
limits: This record notes the characterization of rhetoric as operating on appearances rather than knowledge. It does not claim Socrates accepts this view, nor does it assess whether the subsequent analogy successfully refutes it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0194
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 260a-260b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 260a-260b
  start_marker: 260a
  end_marker: 260b
  start_char: 65787
  end_char: 66661
  text_sha256: 9532a7239f21512290c0c4273366a7fe87576755944e0f6372f4a7ed8aa8d6a8
greek_terms:
  - ἵππον
  - ὄνου
  - ἀγνοοῖμεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 260a-260b, Socrates constructs a hypothetical reductio to test the claim that persuasion requires only seeming, not knowledge. He imagines persuading Phaedrus to acquire a horse for battle while both are ignorant of horses, exploiting Phaedrus's misconception that a horse is the domesticated animal with the largest ears. Socrates could then compose a serious encomium of the donkey, calling it a horse — an absurd outcome that Phaedrus immediately recognizes as ridiculous.
textual_basis: The hypothetical at 260b proceeds from a condition of mutual ignorance (ampho de hippon agnooimen) to a scenario where the speaker exploits the hearer's misconception to produce a persuasive speech praising a donkey under the name of a horse. Phaedrus responds that this would be ridiculous (geloion g' an). The analogy tests the claim from 260a that persuasion comes from seeming, not truth.
limits: This record identifies the hypothetical as an elenctic device. It does not assess whether the analogy is logically valid or whether it decisively refutes the target claim about rhetoric.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0195
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 264e-265a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 264e-265a
  start_marker: 264e
  end_marker: 265a
  start_char: 75293
  end_char: 76100
  text_sha256: 1867d9e6ec3ced9a48903172fa2e59b46ef329f0cd4e5d81a29d87ac1dabedf1
greek_terms:
  - μανίαν
  - ἐφήσαμεν
  - ἔρωτα
english_gloss: ""
observation: 'Socrates recalls that in the earlier speech (the palinode) he and Phaedrus stated a definition of eros: "we said that eros is some kind of madness." The definition is presented as a shared, mutually recalled thesis, not as a newly proposed definition.'
textual_basis: At 265a Socrates asks "we said that eros is some kind of madness, did we not?" and Phaedrus confirms. Socrates then immediately proceeds to divide madness into two kinds ("there are two kinds of madness"). The recall of the definition from the earlier speech establishes it as a fixed starting point for the subsequent division.
limits: Does not evaluate whether the definition is correct; only records that a definition was stated earlier and is now recalled as a premise. Does not claim the definition originated in this segment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0196
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 264e-265a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 264e-265a
  start_marker: 264e
  end_marker: 265a
  start_char: 75293
  end_char: 76100
  text_sha256: 1867d9e6ec3ced9a48903172fa2e59b46ef329f0cd4e5d81a29d87ac1dabedf1
greek_terms:
  - μανίας
  - εἴδη
  - δύο
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Immediately after confirming the definition of eros as a kind of madness, Socrates introduces a dichotomous division of madness into two kinds: one caused by human illnesses, the other by a divine alteration of customary norms. This is a clear two-part classification of the genus madness."
textual_basis: At 265a Socrates states "there are two kinds of madness" and then specifies the two kinds with a "one...the other" construction. The division follows directly from the recalled definition and provides the framework for the subsequent analysis of the palinode's argumentative structure.
limits: Does not assess whether the division is exhaustive or whether the two kinds are properly distinguished. Only records that a dichotomous division of a genus is presented in the text.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0197
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 264e-265a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 264e-265a
  start_marker: 264e
  end_marker: 265a
  start_char: 75293
  end_char: 76100
  text_sha256: 1867d9e6ec3ced9a48903172fa2e59b46ef329f0cd4e5d81a29d87ac1dabedf1
greek_terms:
  - σκώπτεις
  - μανικῶς
  - ἐναντίω
  - μανίαν
  - ἐφήσαμεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "At 264e-265a Socrates transitions the discussion from an examination of Lysias's speech to a focused inquiry into the two earlier speeches (his own and the palinode). He notes that the two speeches were \"opposite\": one argued that the lover should be gratified, the other argued the opposite. He then corrects Phaedrus's characterization of the speeches as \"manly\" to \"madly,\" and states that he was seeking \"this very thing\"—that they said eros was a form of madness. This exchange frames the subsequent method: the two speeches are to be examined as a pair of opposed arguments that share a common definitional premise."
textual_basis: "At 265a Socrates says \"they were opposite\" and describes the two speeches' opposed positions. He corrects Phaedrus's characterization from \"manly\" to \"madly,\" and then recalls the definition \"we said that eros is some kind of madness.\" The procedural move is explicit: the two opposed speeches are to be examined together to see what they reveal about the nature of discourse (\"what is fitting for those wishing to examine speeches to see,\" 265a)."
limits: Does not claim that Socrates is making a methodological point about all rhetoric; only records that he sets up an examination of two opposed speeches as a procedure for studying discourse. Does not evaluate the correctness of his characterization.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0198
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 263d-263e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 263d-263e
  start_marker: 263d
  end_marker: 263e
  start_char: 73035
  end_char: 73754
  text_sha256: 2d205c34df9be4a0d49bbc8646de7545c675952911a2f882b01e7a14971a5cf2
greek_terms:
  - ἐνθουσιαστικὸν
  - ἔρωτα
  - τεχνικωτέρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates disavows memory of whether he defined eros at the start of his speech, attributing the lapse to his enthusiastic state. This is an in-text marker of irony or feigned ignorance, since Socrates has just been critiquing the rhetorical structure of speeches.
textual_basis: At 263d Socrates says he, on account of the enthusiastic, does not quite remember whether he defined eros at the beginning of the speech. The disavowal is explicit and paired with a state that typically implies divine or inspired transport, which in Platonic dialogue can signal ironic distance.
limits: Does not prove that Socrates is being ironic; only records that the text provides an explicit knowledge-disavowal framed by enthusiasm, which is a recurring irony marker in the corpus.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0199
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 263d-263e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 263d-263e
  start_marker: 263d
  end_marker: 263e
  start_char: 73035
  end_char: 73754
  text_sha256: 2d205c34df9be4a0d49bbc8646de7545c675952911a2f882b01e7a14971a5cf2
greek_terms:
  - ἠνάγκασεν
  - ὑπολαβεῖν
  - ἔρωτα
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates claims that Lysias compelled the audience to assume eros to be one of the things that are at the start of his speech, then organized everything that followed around that assumed definition. This identifies a structural feature of Lysias's speech: the definiendum is not argued for but forced as a starting assumption."
textual_basis: At 263d-e Socrates states that Lysias, at the beginning of the erotic speech, compelled them to assume eros to be something among the things that are which he himself wished, and having arranged everything toward this he then carried through the rest of the speech. The compulsion verb and the assumption noun mark a forced starting point rather than a reasoned definition.
limits: Observes a structural claim by Socrates about Lysias's speech; does not assess whether the claim is accurate or whether Plato endorses it. Does not trace how this relates to earlier definitions of eros in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states a normative principle that every logos should be composed like a living creature (zoon), possessing a body of its own with head, middle parts, and feet that fit together and with the whole.
textual_basis: At 264c Socrates says every speech must be composed like a living creature having a body, neither headless nor footless, but with middle parts and extremities written fittingly with each other and the whole.
limits: This observation records the stated compositional principle, not an assessment of whether Lysias' speech meets or violates it.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0202
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 264d-264e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - χαλκῆ παρθένος
  - οὐδὲν διαφέρει
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates quotes a funerary epigram for Midas the Phrygian as a negative paradigm of disordered composition, claiming Lysias' speech is no different from it because the epigram's lines lack necessary sequential order.
textual_basis: At 264d Socrates quotes the four-line Midas epitaph, and at 264e he states that it makes no difference whether a line is said first or last. The quotation therefore functions as a negative paradigm for orderless composition.
limits: This observation records the quotation and its argumentative function as a negative compositional paradigm. It does not assess whether the comparison to Lysias' speech is accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0203
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 261d-261e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 261d-261e
  start_marker: 261d
  end_marker: 261e
  start_char: 69128
  end_char: 69890
  text_sha256: 13de99e35e5f7a9d1b6db65dbe82e9363dff71f51e9f9120fb595905ec32b624
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates refers to Zeno of Elea by the periphrastic epithet 'the Eleatic Palamedes,' identifying him as someone known for speaking with a techne that makes the same things appear similar and dissimilar, one and many, at rest and in motion (261d).
textual_basis: "At 261d, Socrates asks: 'Do we not know that the Eleatic Palamedes speaks with art, so that the same things appear to his listeners both like and unlike, one and many, and both at rest and in motion?' The epithet 'Eleatic Palamedes' uses a mythological figure (Palamedes) to indirectly name Zeno of Elea, whose paradoxes are characterized as a techne of making contraries appear."
limits: This observation records the prosopographic epithet and its association with a techne; it does not evaluate whether Socrates' characterization of Zeno's method is accurate or fair.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0204

  end_char: 69890
  text_sha256: 13de99e35e5f7a9d1b6db65dbe82e9363dff71f51e9f9120fb595905ec32b624
greek_terms:
  - μία τις τέχνη
  - πᾶν παντὶ ὁμοιοῦν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims that antilogic is not confined to lawcourts and public speaking but is one single art (mia tis techne) covering all things said, capable of making everything like everything possible and of exposing another's concealed assimilations (261d-e).
textual_basis: "At 261d-e, after establishing with Phaedrus that the same things can be made to appear just or unjust, good or bad, Socrates concludes: 'Antilogic is not only about lawcourts and public speaking, but, as it seems, concerning all things said there is one single art... by which someone will be able to make everything like everything possible, and to bring to light another's making-like and concealment.' The passage moves from restricted domains (courts, assembly) to a universal scope for antilogic as a unified techne."
limits: This observation records the claim that antilogic is a unified techne with universal scope; it does not assess whether Socrates endorses this claim or whether the definition holds up under later examination in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0205
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 266a-266b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 266a-266b
  start_marker: 266a

  end_char: 78380
  text_sha256: 94f3c74e6796a7d2790c16aaee3359babb585aff7ac5bd17a4235594087c4a14
greek_terms:
  - διαιρέσεων
  - συναγωγῶν
english_gloss: divisions and collections
observation: "Socrates explicitly names divisions and collections as the method he loves, and retrospectively describes how the two preceding speeches applied this method: the first speech divided madness on the left side to find a blameworthy love, while the second went to the right side to find a divine love worthy of praise."
textual_basis: At 266a-266b, Socrates says he is a lover of divisions and collections, and describes the two speeches as having divided madness (treated as a single form) into left and right, with the first speech finding a left-handed love to blame and the second finding a divine love to praise.
limits: This observation records Socrates' explicit methodological statement; it does not assess whether the division is valid or whether the two speeches actually follow the method described.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 266b, Socrates says he calls those capable of doing division and collection by a certain name, but adds "whether I address them correctly or not, god knows," which is an explicit hedge on his own knowledge.
limits: This observation records the explicit knowledge disavowal; it does not determine whether irony is present or what Socrates' actual epistemic state is.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 266b, Socrates says he follows such a person "close behind, as if a god," which is a quotation from Homer used to characterize Socrates' relationship to dialecticians.
limits: This observation records the presence of a poetic quotation used in an argumentative context; it does not assess whether the quotation is used ironically or whether Socrates genuinely regards dialecticians as divine.
```

```yaml
limits: This span introduces the cicadas dramatically and does not yet give the mythic origin story.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 258e-259a Phaedrus calls bodily pleasures slavish, and Socrates imagines sleepers as slave-like visitors and sheep by the spring.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0210

greek_terms:
  - Σειρῆνας
  - παραπλέοντάς
  - ἀκηλήτους
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates compares the pair's dialoguing activity to sailing past the cicadas as though they were Sirens, framing philosophical conversation as a sea-voyage that resists enchanted stasis.
textual_basis: At 259a-259b Socrates says that if the cicadas see the pair conversing and sailing past them like Sirens, unenchanted, then the cicadas might grant them the gift they possess from the gods. The Siren-sailing image casts dialogue as movement past a singing danger.
limits: This observation covers only the Siren comparison in this span. It does not address the subsequent cicada origin story or the rest of the Siren tradition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0211
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 262c-262d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 71485
  text_sha256: 3a2b74b00dea7843e5cefaf56daf9948532ac3086d321158a55d03b03e7a66ef
greek_terms:
  - γελοίαν
  - ἄτεχνον
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that the art of speeches produced by someone who does not know the truth but has hunted opinions will be ridiculous and artless. Truth-knowledge is posited as a necessary condition for rhetoric to be a genuine techne.
textual_basis: At 262c, Socrates says the person ignorant of truth but pursuing opinions will provide a techne that is geloian and atechnon — literally without art. The claim denies craft-status to rhetoric when detached from knowledge of truth.
limits: Does not define what counts as knowledge of truth; does not specify whether any existing rhetoric meets the condition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0212

  - τοῦ λέγειν
  - μέτοχος
  - ἐπιπεπνευκότες
  - γέρας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates disclaims any personal possession of the art of speaking, attributing whatever quality the two speeches have to chance and to inspiration from the local gods and the Muses' prophets, the cicadas.
textual_basis: "At 262d, after noting the two speeches provide an example, Socrates says he blames the local gods and suggests the Muses' prophets may have inspired this gift (epipepneukotes...touto to geras), adding: for I myself, I suppose, do not partake of any art of speaking (ou gar pou egoge technes tinos tou legein metochos)."
limits: Consistent with Socratic irony patterns but the text does not internally mark it as ironic; records the disavowal and attribution to divine sources without claiming insincerity.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0213

greek_terms:
  - ψιλῶς
  - παραδείγματα
  - παράδειγμα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates and Phaedrus agree to use Lysias' written speech and their own two prior speeches as paradeigmata for examining what is artful and artless in rhetoric.
textual_basis: At 262c Phaedrus notes they have been speaking abstractly (psilos) without adequate examples (paradeigmata). Socrates proposes examining Lysias' speech and their own for what is artful and artless (262c). At 262d he says the two speeches were spoken by some chance so as to have some example (paradeigma). He then asks Phaedrus to read the beginning of Lysias' speech (262d).
limits: Records the procedural agreement; does not assess whether the speeches succeed as paradeigmata.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - συμφωνοῦμεν
  - πλανώμεθα
  - χαρακτῆρα
  - εἴδους
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates first divides subject matter into agreed and disputed or misleading classes, then says the aspiring rhetorician must grasp the mark of each class.
textual_basis: At 263b-263c Socrates asks where people are more easily deceived and where rhetoric has greater power, then requires methodical division of the two kinds.
limits: This is the general methodological distinction; neighboring records cover the later application to eros.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0215
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 263b-263c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 263b-263c
  start_marker: 263b
  end_marker: 263c
  start_char: 72188
  end_char: 73035
  text_sha256: f188f19cc9fa2c7bf9c5bf0dc1a4bd0d71e6232d7bcf2bf6aa8056f1ae0f2655
greek_terms:
  - χαρακτῆρα
  - εἴδους
  - γένους
english_gloss: a character/mark of each form, genus/kind
observation: "Socrates applies his two-class procedural distinction to the subject of eros itself, asking whether eros belongs to the disputed or the undisputed class. Phaedrus affirms eros belongs to the disputed class, citing as evidence the contradictory speeches just given about it: that eros is a harm to beloved and lover, and conversely that it is the greatest of goods."
textual_basis: At 263c Socrates asks 'what then? Shall we say eros is among the disputed (amphisbetesimon) or the undisputed?' Phaedrus answers 'among the disputed, surely; or do you think I would have allowed you to say what you just said about it, that it is a harm (blabe) to the beloved and the lover, and again that it happens to be the greatest of goods (megiston ton agathon)?' This explicitly marks eros as a contested definiendum whose contrary characterizations have already been displayed in the preceding speeches.
limits: This observation records the explicit classification of eros as a contested term within the procedural division. It does not resolve what eros is, nor does it evaluate the preceding speeches. The passage frames eros as a definiendum needing inquiry rather than providing a definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0216
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 263b-263c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 263b-263c
  start_marker: 263b
  end_marker: 263c
  start_char: 72188
  end_char: 73035
  text_sha256: f188f19cc9fa2c7bf9c5bf0dc1a4bd0d71e6232d7bcf2bf6aa8056f1ae0f2655
greek_terms:
  - βλάβη
english_gloss: harm, greatest of goods
observation: "Phaedrus identifies a contradiction between the two preceding speeches about eros: the first characterized eros as a harm (blabe) to both beloved and lover, while the second characterized it as the greatest of goods (megiston ton agathon). Phaedrus uses this contradiction as evidence that eros belongs to the disputed class of subjects."
textual_basis: "At 263c Phaedrus says of eros: 'as it is a harm (blabe) to the beloved and the lover, and again as it happens to be the greatest of goods (megiston on ton agathon).' He presents these as contrary claims about the same subject recently made in the preceding speeches. The contradiction serves as proof that eros is among the disputed matters (amphisbetesimon), which are precisely the class where, per Socrates' procedural analysis at 263b, the multitude is misled."
limits: This observation records Phaedrus's own identification of a contradiction between prior speeches; it does not adjudicate which speech is correct. It does not claim that Socrates endorses either speech's characterization of eros. The observation is limited to the meta-level use of contradiction as a criterion for classifying the subject matter.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0217
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - διαλεκτικὸν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates assigns the name 'dialectic' to the method of collection and division he has been describing, and Phaedrus confirms the correctness of the name.
textual_basis: At 266c Socrates asks what to call the procedure they have been practicing, then contrasts it with the art of speeches used by Thrasymachus. Phaedrus responds at 266c that Socrates seems to name it correctly by calling it 'dialectic' (dialektikon).
limits: The passage does not define dialectic in detail; the naming occurs in contrast to rhetoric, whose content is said to still escape them. The observation records the terminological act, not a full definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0218
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 266c-266d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - διαφεύγειν
  - λειπόμενον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Phaedrus marks the rhetorical part as still escaping them, and Socrates insists that it must not be dishonored but investigated: they must say what the remaining part of rhetoric is."
textual_basis: At 266c Phaedrus says 'the rhetorical part seems to me still to escape us' (to de rhetorikon dokei moi diapheugein eth' hemas). At 266d Socrates responds that they must not dishonor what is left out but must say what the remaining part (to leipomenon) of rhetoric is.
limits: This observation records that rhetoric is marked as a definiendum requiring investigation. It does not claim that a definition is achieved in this segment; the inquiry is only announced.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - προοίμιον

textual_basis: "At 266d Phaedrus says there is quite a lot in the books written about the art of speeches (tois bibliois tois peri logon technes gegrammenois). Socrates responds by beginning an enumeration: 'preamble (prooimion) I think must first be spoken at the beginning of the speech — these are the refined things (ta kompsa) of the art, aren't they?'"
limits: Only the first item of the catalogue (prooimion) appears in this segment. The observation records the start of a catalogue drawn from existing rhetorical handbooks, not a complete enumeration.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0220
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 268b-268c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 268b-268c

  end_marker: 268c

  end_char: 82506
  text_sha256: 030ae87900501bec704c35b60971c219a4826cb9ab14bb69ac56e81dcc28139b
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρικὸς
  - τέχνης
english_gloss: medical practitioner, craft
observation: Socrates uses a medical-craft analogy to illustrate the insufficiency of knowing isolated techniques without understanding when, to whom, and to what extent to apply them.
textual_basis: At 268b-c Socrates imagines someone who knows how to produce certain bodily effects (heating, cooling, purging) and on that basis claims to be a doctor and to be able to teach the craft. Phaedrus supplies the counter that a listener would ask whether the person also knows whom to treat, when, and how much; absent that knowledge, the person would be called mad, having encountered drugs from a book without understanding the craft.
limits: This observation records the analogy structure, not a claim about what rhetoric or any other specific art actually requires. The observation is limited to the mechanics of the analogy as presented in 268b-c.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0221
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 269e-270b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 269e-270b
  start_marker: 269e
  end_marker: 270b
  start_char: 85005
  end_char: 85988
  text_sha256: 6b0d6c4d2b94f851ccf5f681d0d7bd27bde005777ea81e1fe1e799cc743e0c0c
greek_terms:
  - ἰατρικῆς
  - ῥητορικῆς
  - σώματος
  - ψυχῆς
  - τριβῇ
  - ἐμπειρίᾳ
  - τέχνῃ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asserts that the method of the medical art and the method of rhetoric are the same. Both require distinguishing the nature of their respective subject matter in order to practice by art rather than by mere routine and experience.
textual_basis: "At 270b Socrates states explicitly that the method (tropos) of medicine and rhetoric is the same. He then parallels their operations: medicine supplies drugs and nourishment to the body to produce health and strength; rhetoric supplies speeches and lawful practices to the soul to produce persuasion and virtue. Both are set against tribe (routine) and empeiria (experience) as the alternative, non-art basis of practice."
limits: This records the craft-analogy structure at 270b. It does not assess whether Socrates later develops or qualifies the analogy, nor whether the body/soul parallel withstands scrutiny.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0222
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - Περικλῆς
  - Ἀναξαγόρᾳ
  - μετεωρολογίας
  - εὐφυὴς
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates names Pericles and Anaxagoras as an intellectual teacher-pupil pair, claiming that Pericles acquired his elevated cast of mind and rhetorical completeness from association with Anaxagoras and his natural philosophy.
textual_basis: At 269e Socrates calls Pericles the most accomplished rhetorician and at 270a explains this by saying Pericles fell in with Anaxagoras, became filled with meteorologia, reached the nature of mind and thought (subjects of Anaxagoras' long discourses), and drew from this what was fitting for the art of speeches. Pericles is described as already naturally gifted (euphues) and adding Anaxagorean high-mindedness to his natural talent.
limits: This records the named historical pairing at 269e-270a. It does not evaluate the historical accuracy of the Pericles-Anaxagoras relationship or assess whether the claim is ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0223
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 269e-270b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - τέχνῃ
  - τριβῇ
  - ἐμπειρίᾳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates draws a contrast between practicing by art (techne) and practicing by mere routine (tribe) and experience (empeiria), presenting the latter pair as a deficient alternative to genuine art.
textual_basis: At 270b, in the context of the medicine/rhetoric analogy, Socrates says one must distinguish the nature of the body or soul "if you intend, not by routine alone and experience but by art" (me tribe monon kai empeiria alla techne) to produce the desired effects. The construction sets techne on one side and tribe-plus-empeiria on the other.
limits: This records the techne/tribe/empeiria contrast at 270b. It does not trace this distinction across other Platonic dialogues or claim it is the only formulation of the art-versus-knack distinction in the Phaedrus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0224
source_work: Phaedrus

  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus delimits the conventional domain of rhetorical techne to two genres, lawsuits and public speeches, and denies hearing of it applied beyond these.
textual_basis: At 261b, when Socrates asks whether correct speaking is equally valued concerning serious and trivial matters, Phaedrus responds that rhetorical techne is mostly about lawsuits and also about public speeches, adding that he has not heard it further.
limits: This observation records Phaedrus's report of conventional opinion about rhetoric's scope; it does not assert that Socrates endorses this delimitation, nor does it address whether the dialogue ultimately expands rhetoric's domain.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates and Phaedrus map contemporary sophists and rhetoricians onto Homeric heroes, with Phaedrus identifying Gorgias as a Nestor-figure and Thrasymachus and Theodorus as Odysseus-figures.
textual_basis: At 261b-261c, Socrates asks whether Phaedrus has only heard of the arts of Nestor and Odysseus concerning speeches, composed by them at leisure in Troy, and whether he has not heard of Palamedes. Phaedrus replies by mapping Gorgias onto Nestor and Thrasymachus and Theodorus onto Odysseus.
limits: This observation records the mapping of named contemporary figures to Homeric types; it does not assess the accuracy of the analogy or whether Socrates endorses it, as Socrates responds with "perhaps" and changes the subject.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0226

  end_char: 86877
  text_sha256: 7c2bc7fb8d3096a6bbf709303a7eabd5bed7c7edb61b2362e10d3905a2dda318
greek_terms:
  - τεχνικοὶ
  - ἁπλοῦν
  - πολυειδές
  - δύναμιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates outlines a two-step method for studying any nature with the aim of becoming a craftsman (technikos): first determine whether the thing is simple or multiform, then examine its active and passive powers — what it naturally does and what it naturally undergoes."
textual_basis: At 270c-d Socrates asks whether understanding the soul's nature requires understanding the nature of the whole, and then explicitly lays out the method beginning at 270d with "first, whether it is simple or multiform..." and continuing through the examination of active and passive powers.
limits: This records a methodological program stated by Socrates; it does not assess whether the method is successfully executed, whether it is original to Plato, or whether the soul in fact requires study of the whole.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  - συμφωνεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus cites Hippocrates of the Asclepiads as an authority for the claim that even the body cannot be understood without studying the nature of the whole. Socrates endorses the statement but immediately adds that one must examine the argument to see whether it agrees with Hippocrates.
textual_basis: At 270c Phaedrus says "if one must place some trust in Hippocrates of the Asclepiads..." and Socrates responds "he speaks well, my friend; but one must, in addition to Hippocrates, examine the argument and consider whether it agrees."
limits: This records the pattern of citing an expert authority while simultaneously requiring independent rational examination of the claim. It does not claim that Socrates rejects Hippocrates, nor does it evaluate whether the method described is authentically Hippocratic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0229

  start_char: 82506
  end_char: 83261
  text_sha256: 8230113c641328459526abeb32ca632ecc1810c80aa71f6980239515a1d621e3
greek_terms:
  - ἁρμονικῷ
  - ἁρμονίας
  - ἁρμονικά
  - μουσικὸς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates contrasts two modes of correction: the harsh, rustic rebuke (agroikos loidoria) and the gentle, musician-like correction. He has the mousikos explicitly avoid the crude rebuke 'Wretch, you're mad' in favor of a mild explanation that the man knows only preliminaries, not the art itself."
textual_basis: At 268d-e, Socrates says the musical man would not speak agroikos (agroikos) nor would he loidorein (insult), but would speak praoteron (more gently). He supplies both the rejected harsh speech (O mokhthere, melancholais) and the preferred gentle correction (O ariste, ananke men kai taut' epistasthai...). Phaedrus endorses this as most correct (orthotata ge).
limits: This observation records the explicit contrast between two correction modes and the supplied specimen speeches. It does not claim that this models Socratic method or that Socrates himself always uses gentle correction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0230

greek_terms:
  - πρὸ ἁρμονίας
  - ἀναγκαῖα μαθήματα
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces a distinction between necessary preliminary learning (ta pro harmonias anankaia mathemata) and the art proper (ta harmonika), in which mastery of elements does not guarantee even slight understanding of the art. This maps onto the earlier tragic-poetry example where knowing the elements of tragedy does not constitute knowing tragedy.
textual_basis: "At 268e, the musical man's speech states: 'you know the necessary preliminary learnings before harmonics, but not the things of harmonics themselves' (ta gar pro harmonias anankaia mathemata epistasai all' ou ta harmonika). He adds that nothing prevents someone with this disposition from having not even a little comprehension of harmonia (ouden men kolyei mede smikron harmonias epaiein)."
limits: This observation records the conceptual distinction between preliminaries and the art itself. It does not claim that Socrates offers a definition of any particular art here, nor does it address how this distinction applies to rhetoric elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: This observation does not assess whether the speech actually lacks order; it only notes the critical question Socrates poses.
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates distinguishes between the complete art of rhetoric and the mere preliminaries taught by handbook-writers. The handbook-writers teach elements like prooemia and narrative but leave the task of persuasive arrangement and the whole composition to the students themselves.
textual_basis: At 269c Socrates states that those who have discovered and teach these precepts think they have taught rhetoric perfectly, but they consider persuasive speaking and the whole composition to be no work of theirs — rather the students must supply it from themselves. Phaedrus agrees this characterizes the art as taught by these men.
limits: This observation notes the craft-demarcation between the whole art and its parts. It does not evaluate whether Socrates' account is fair to the actual rhetorical manuals of the period.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0236

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus asks Socrates how and from where one could acquire the art of the truly rhetorical and persuasive person. Socrates does not answer this directly but instead states the conditions for becoming a complete competitor and then begins to distinguish his own methodological approach from that of Lysias and Thrasymachus. The question is deferred.
textual_basis: 'At 269d Phaedrus asks: "But now, the art of the one who is truly rhetorical and persuasive — how and from where could one acquire it?" Socrates responds with the natural-acquired conditions and then says that as for the art itself, the method does not seem to follow the path of Lysias and Thrasymachus, leading Phaedrus to ask "which way then?"'
limits: This observation notes that the question is deferred, not that it is definitively answered later.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0237

  text_sha256: 0651e2e782a935818730c95edddd5aecaa55c4ced6668701e537acb055990443
greek_terms:
  - τὸ εἰκὸς
  - τὸ τῷ πλήθει δοκοῦν
  - σοφὸν
  - τέχνῃ
  - ἀποκεκρυμμένην τέχνην
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 273b-c Socrates expresses mock-admiration for Tisias's rhetorical method, exclaiming that he seems to have discovered a fearsomely hidden art. The irony operates through the contrast between the elevated language of cleverness and craft on one hand, and the content of the method — telling the likely rather than the true, manipulating courtroom appearances — on the other.
textual_basis: Socrates exclaims at 273c that Tisias seems to have discovered a 'terribly hidden art.' At 273b he describes the method as 'clever and artful.' The irony is established by the mismatch between the praise-language and the described method, which consists of suppressing truth in favor of what seems likely to the multitude.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates is being insincere or that he has a hidden agenda. It notes only the ironic texture of the praise within the passage.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0238

greek_terms:
  - δικαστήριον
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports that Tisias illustrated his rhetorical method with a hypothetical courtroom case in which a weak-but-brave man beats a strong-but-cowardly man and takes his cloak. Neither litigant is to tell the truth; each must argue from what seems likely rather than what happened.
textual_basis: "At 273b-c Socrates describes the scenario: a weak and brave man strikes a strong and cowardly one, takes his cloak, and the case goes to court. Socrates explicitly states that neither should tell the truth, and the rhetorical tactics for each litigant are prescribed."
limits: This observation identifies the courtroom scenario as a dramatic setup within the dialogue's argument. It does not assess whether Tisias's method was historically accurate or whether the scenario reflects real legal practice.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - σοφὸν
  - τέχνῃ
  - τέχνην
english_gloss: ""
observation: In this passage rhetoric is repeatedly framed as a techne (art or craft). Tisias's writing is described as clever and artful, his examples are called things spoken by art, and Socrates exclaims that Tisias discovered a hidden art.
textual_basis: At 273b Socrates says Tisias wrote something clever and artful. At 273c the examples are described as things spoken by art. Socrates then exclaims that Tisias discovered a fearsomely hidden art.
limits: This observation notes the craft language applied to rhetoric in the passage. It does not claim that rhetoric actually is a techne in Socrates's view, nor does it assess whether the craft analogy holds. The passage may be using craft language ironically.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0240
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 272e-273a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 272e-273a

  end_char: 91433
  text_sha256: d774b489600635d90aa069e762037e5c9672f6f1ca965801e05e6fe5726ee7bb
greek_terms:
  - τὸ εἰκός
  - τέχνῃ
  - τῷ ἀληθεῖ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports the doctrine of the technical rhetoricians that the craft of speaking consists in pursuing the probable rather than truth. The rhetoricians hold that one should not always say what actually happened if it is not probable, but should say what is probable, in both accusation and defense, bidding farewell to the truth. This pursuit of the probable throughout the speech is said to provide the whole craft.
textual_basis: Socrates states at 272e that the probable is what the one intending to speak with craft must attend to. At 273a he says that this pursuit of the probable, occurring throughout the whole speech, provides the entire craft, explicitly opposing it to saying what is true.
limits: This records Socrates's report of the rhetoricians' doctrine, not his own endorsement of it. Does not evaluate whether this is an accurate characterization of Tisias's actual teaching.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0244
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 272a-272b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 272a-272b
  start_marker: 272a

  end_char: 89905
  text_sha256: 51b0801fe267193af28dd791151921d8b607ada29a5c12d1fb53178dc8f50acf
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 272a-272b Socrates imagines a hypothetical complaint from a rhetorical handbook writer, who addresses both Phaedrus and Socrates by name, dismissing the art of speeches as insufficiently demonstrated. Socrates then draws Phaedrus back into assent by affirming the difficulty of the task and proposing further examination of all the arguments.
textual_basis: Socrates embeds a direct-speech intervention from an imaginary composer who objects that the art of speeches has not yet been accepted on the terms offered (272b). This is a projected interlocutor, not a character physically present. Socrates then secures Phaedrus's agreement that the task is not small and proposes to turn the arguments over and examine them from all sides (272a-b).
limits: This observation records only the ventriloquism device and its procedural effect of binding Phaedrus to further inquiry. It does not assess whether the imagined speech-giver represents a real figure or evaluate the adequacy of the art of speeches under discussion.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0245

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 272a-272b, after a hypothetical objector voices a challenge to the completeness of the art of speeches, Socrates secures Phaedrus's agreement that the inquiry must continue by re-examining all the arguments thoroughly. Phaedrus characterizes the undertaking as 'not small' but does not refuse it.
textual_basis: Phaedrus responds to the imagined objector (and to Socrates) by saying that it is surely impossible otherwise and that the task appears no small work (272b). Socrates agrees and proposes turning all the arguments upside down and examining them to see whether anything further can be found (272a-b). The exchange restores a procedural agreement to continue the investigation into the art of speeches.
limits: This observation records the procedural re-commitment to inquiry at this juncture. It does not determine whether the inquiry succeeds or whether Phaedrus's assent implies conviction or mere compliance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0246

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates explicitly closes the preceding discussion of art and artlessness in speeches and marks a transition to a new topic.
textual_basis: "At 274b Socrates says 'let the matter of art and artlessness in speeches be sufficiently dealt with' (to men technes te kai atekhnias logon peri hikanos ekheto), then introduces the next subject: the propriety and impropriety of writing (to d' euprepeias de graphes peri kai aprepeias)."
limits: This observation records the procedural transition; it does not evaluate whether the closure is adequate or what follows.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates offers Tisias a procedural choice: if Tisias has something else to say about the craft of speeches (techne logon), Socrates will listen; otherwise they will be persuaded by the points just reviewed at 273d-e.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates says at 273d: "if you say something else about the craft of speeches, we would listen; but if not, we will be persuaded by what we have just gone through" (ei men allo ti peri technes logon legeis, akouoimen an; ei de me, hois nynde dielthomen peisometha). The conditional structure presents a clear binary procedural fork for Tisias.
limits: |
  This records the conditional offer at 273d-e, not whether Tisias accepts it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0248

greek_terms:
  - ψυχαγωγία
  - λόγου δύναμις
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates defines the power of speech (logou dynamis) as psychagogia (soul-leading).
textual_basis: At 271c, Socrates begins his explanation of how to write with art by stating that the power of speech happens to be soul-leading. This is a compact definitional equation that serves as the premise for the subsequent account of artful rhetoric.
limits: The definition is stated as a starting point; the passage does not develop or defend it, only deploys it as a premise.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0249
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 271b-271c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 271b-271c
  start_marker: 271b
  end_marker: 271c
  start_char: 87571
  end_char: 88377
  text_sha256: 1466168d1d35b1026775b7f2a4aaa95c76a2ec5a998e659a46a491110e3cd33d
greek_terms:
  - γένη
  - παθήματα
  - αἰτίας
  - προσαρμόττων
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates declares a three-part methodological program for a genuine art of speeches: classifying kinds of speeches and souls with their affections, matching each speech-type to each soul-type, and providing causal explanations for why a given soul is persuaded or not by given speeches. He then claims that no speech or writing on any subject can be produced by art in any other way."
textual_basis: At 271b, Socrates says one must arrange the kinds of speeches and soul along with their affections, go through all causes, fitting each to each and teaching what kind of soul is necessarily persuaded by what kind of speeches and through what cause. At 271c, he states that nothing will ever be said or written by art in any other way.
limits: This is a programmatic declaration of what a techne of rhetoric would require. The passage does not execute the program; it only asserts the conditions.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0250
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 271d-271e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 271d-271e
  start_marker: 271d
  end_marker: 271e
  start_char: 88377
  end_char: 89066
  text_sha256: 7460dc376b29bb845e4c604409b465a574db0c9d441fcaefc05c566b370fa0a6
greek_terms:
  - ψυχὴ ὅσα εἴδη ἔχει
  - τόσα καὶ τόσα
  - τοῖα καὶ τοῖα
english_gloss: the soul has so many forms, of such and such kinds
observation: "Socrates describes a rhetorical art (rhetorike techne) that must begin by classifying the soul into its eide (forms/kinds): the prospective rhetor must know how many forms the soul has, of what kinds, and from these how different types of men arise. This mirrors the craft principle that genuine techne requires knowledge of the nature of its object domain before applying methods."
textual_basis: At 271d Socrates states that 'the one who is going to be rhetorical must know how many forms the soul has' and then enumerates a schema of kinds and corresponding logoi kinds, making classification of the soul's nature the prerequisite for rhetorical expertise.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates endorses or rejects the possibility of such a true rhetorical art — only that the passage models rhetoric on a craft pattern of domain classification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0252
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 271d-271e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 271d-271e
  start_marker: 271d
  end_marker: 271e
  start_char: 88377
  end_char: 89066
  text_sha256: 7460dc376b29bb845e4c604409b465a574db0c9d441fcaefc05c566b370fa0a6
greek_terms:
  - ὀξέως τῇ αἰσθήσει δύνασθαι ἐπακολουθεῖν
  - πράξεσιν
english_gloss: to be able to follow sharply with perception in actions
observation: After mastering the theoretical classification of soul-kinds and speech-kinds, the student must then observe these kinds 'in actions being done' (en tais praxesin onta te kai prattomena) and be able to follow them sharply with perception (oxeos tei aisthesei dynasthai epakolouthein). This is a demand that craft knowledge be applied in live perception and practice, not merely known in theory.
textual_basis: At 271d-271e, having described the classification schema, Socrates says that after grasping these things sufficiently, one must observe them in actions and follow them sharply with perception — or else one has gained nothing beyond the logoi heard while together with a teacher.
limits: This observation notes the theory-to-practice transition requirement in the rhetorical craft model. It does not evaluate whether such a craft is achievable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0253
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 272c-272d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 272c-272d
  start_marker: 272c
  end_marker: 272d
  start_char: 89905
  end_char: 90747
  text_sha256: 47a1eb835f1b876e8b481be43c9d459e463cfb8ba12000d075393764ceb5c407
greek_terms:
  - τὸ τοῦ λύκου εἰπεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates introduces a reported speech by invoking a proverb about the wolf's story. The proverb functions to license the telling of a view that might otherwise seem disreputable or one-sided, framing the upcoming reported speech as permissible in the name of fairness.
textual_basis: |
  At 272c, when Phaedrus says he cannot produce a shorter path to rhetoric at the moment, Socrates offers to tell a logos he has heard. Before launching into it he prefaces it with the saying that it is just to tell even the wolf's story — the proverb is stated as a reported saying itself and serves as a procedural justification for recounting the anonymous view that follows.
limits: |
  The observation identifies a proverbial framing device; it does not interpret the wolf proverb's content or origin, nor does it claim that the proverb signals esotericism or hidden intent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0254
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 272c-272d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 272c-272d
  start_marker: 272c
  end_marker: 272d
  start_char: 89905
  end_char: 90747
  text_sha256: 47a1eb835f1b876e8b481be43c9d459e463cfb8ba12000d075393764ceb5c407
greek_terms:
  - φασὶ τοίνυν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates introduces an argumentative position using an anonymous plural verb of saying, attributing the view that rhetoric need not concern itself with truth to unnamed sources. The view is presented as a received position circulating among certain people rather than as Socrates' own thesis.
textual_basis: |
  At 272d, after Phaedrus agrees to hear the speech, Socrates opens with an anonymous "they say" and proceeds to report the position that one need not ascend to truth about just or good matters to be a competent rhetorician. The anonymous attribution distances the speaker from the view while allowing it to be examined.
limits: |
  The observation registers the anonymous-report framing device; it does not interpret whether Socrates endorses, rejects, or ironizes the reported view, nor does it identify the unnamed sources.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0255
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 272c-272d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 272c-272d
  start_marker: 272c
  end_marker: 272d
  start_char: 89905
  end_char: 90747
  text_sha256: 47a1eb835f1b876e8b481be43c9d459e463cfb8ba12000d075393764ceb5c407
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Within the reported anonymous speech at 272d, Socrates explicitly recalls a point made at the beginning of the current discussion: that the would-be rhetorician need not share in truth about just or good matters. This internal recapitulation ties the reported view back to the earlier stage of the dialogue's argument.
textual_basis: |
  At 272d Socrates states that what was said at the beginning of this speech is that the person who will be sufficiently rhetorical need not share in truth about just or good matters, or about people who are such by nature or nurture. The phrase referring back to the start of the current logos marks a recapitulation that anchors the anonymous reported position in the dialogue's own argumentative history.
limits: |
  The observation notes the formal recapitulation marker; it does not assess whether the recalled claim is identical to what was actually said earlier, nor does it evaluate the truth of the anonymous view.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0256
source_work: Phaedrus

  end_char: 95632
  text_sha256: f81aee040d84f4445d24d775526013207f13444bd3e4aff2562e828be6cc3c3c
greek_terms:
  - φάρμακον
  - μνήμης
  - σοφίας
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports a mythic narrative in which the Egyptian king Thamous critiques the god Theuth's invention of writing. Both Theuth's praise and Thamous's counter-evaluation are framed as reported speech within the myth.
textual_basis: The passage opens with a report that Theuth presented letters to Thamous (274e), and then embeds Theuth's claim that writing will make Egyptians wiser and more mindful, and Thamous's reply (274e-275a). The whole evaluation is presented as a mythic king-god exchange reported by Socrates.
limits: This record notes the mythic frame and embedded evaluation structure. It does not claim that Plato endorses Thamous's critique, nor does it assess whether the myth is presented as authoritative or ironic.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0257
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 274e-275a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 274e-275a
  start_marker: 274e
  end_marker: 275a
  start_char: 94716
  end_char: 95632
  text_sha256: f81aee040d84f4445d24d775526013207f13444bd3e4aff2562e828be6cc3c3c
greek_terms:
  - λήθην
  - μνήμης
  - ὑπομνήσεως
  - φάρμακον
  - δόξαν
  - ἀλήθειαν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Thamous revises Theuth's key terms: Theuth claims writing is a drug for memory and wisdom; Thamous counters that it produces forgetfulness in souls, is a drug for reminding not memory, and provides the appearance of wisdom not truth."
textual_basis: "Theuth claims writing will make people more mindful and is a drug for memory and wisdom (274e). Thamous replies with paired counter-terms: forgetfulness against memory, reminding against memory, and appearance against wisdom, with truth as what writing fails to provide (275a). Each counter-term forms a direct revision of Theuth's original claim."
limits: This record maps the term-revision structure. It does not assert that Thamous's revision represents Socratic or Platonic doctrine, nor does it evaluate whether the revision is valid.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἔνδοθεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Thamous explains writing's harm through an inside-outside contrast: learners rely on external alien marks instead of recollecting from within themselves."
textual_basis: At 275a the contrast is explicit in the account of trust in writing and external marks versus internal self-reminding.
limits: This is a structural contrast in the critique of writing, not a metaphysical claim about Forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0259
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 276a-276b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - εἴδωλον
english_gloss: living and ensouled speech / image
observation: Phaedrus characterizes the written word as an image (eidolon) of the living, ensouled speech (logos) of one who knows, at 276a.
textual_basis: After Socrates describes a logos written with knowledge in the soul of the learner, Phaedrus replies with 'the speech of the knower, living and ensouled, of which the written one would justly be called an image (eidolon)' at 276a.
limits: This observation records only that a contrast between living speech and dead written image is drawn; it does not claim that the dialogue endorses or rejects writing, nor that Phaedrus speaks for Socrates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0260
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 276a-276b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 276a-276b
  start_marker: 276a
  end_marker: 276b
  start_char: 97204
  end_char: 98031
  text_sha256: be1163aae45b7e0e02d1280cd017bd724bfd4c9b46237cad448e906af029389c
greek_terms:
  - Ἀδώνιδος κήπους
  - γεωργικῇ χρώμενος ἂν τέχνῃ
english_gloss: Gardens of Adonis / using the craft of farming
observation: Socrates introduces the Gardens of Adonis as a contrastive analogy between serious farming and writing-for-play at 276b.
textual_basis: At 276b Socrates asks whether an intelligent farmer would seriously sow seeds in Adonis gardens for rapid eight-day growth, or do that for play and festival, while using the craft of farming (georgike techne) seriously in proper soil for an eight-month yield — framing the writing/speech contrast through agricultural cultivation.
limits: This observation does not assert that Socrates rejects writing altogether, only that the agricultural analogy distinguishes playful from serious cultivation as a way of distinguishing modes of discourse.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0261
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 276a-276b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 276a-276b

  start_char: 97204
  end_char: 98031
  text_sha256: be1163aae45b7e0e02d1280cd017bd724bfd4c9b46237cad448e906af029389c
greek_terms:
  - παιδιᾶς τε καὶ ἑορτῆς χάριν
  - σπουδῇ
english_gloss: for the sake of play and festival / seriously
observation: Socrates explicitly contrasts serious (spoude) and playful (paidia) modes of activity as a procedural framework for evaluating the value of written discourse at 276b.
textual_basis: The Adonis-garden analogy at 276b turns on the opposition between doing something 'seriously' (spoude) and doing it 'for the sake of play and festival' (paidias te kai heortes charin), establishing that the mode of engagement — not just the content — determines a discourse's status.
limits: This observation records the procedural framing distinction; it does not interpret whether Socrates judges writing playful or serious, only that the play/serious criterion is explicitly employed as a governing distinction.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0262
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 266e-267a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 266e-267a
  start_marker: 266e
  end_marker: 267a
  start_char: 79269
  end_char: 79938
  text_sha256: 49a7f74b0ca36a96a467280834dff9c2027d95fd671dad71276b310b51690dd2
greek_terms:
  - λογοδαίδαλον
english_gloss: speech-craftsman, a coinage for a rhetorician who weaves arguments
observation: Socrates refers to the best rhetorical technician as 'the Byzantian speech-craftsman' (logodaidalon), a compound epithet that characterizes a named practitioner by a craft-like skill in argument construction rather than by name alone.
textual_basis: At 266e Socrates introduces a catalogue of rhetorical devices (narration, testimonial evidence, proofs, probabilities) and attributes this technical vocabulary to 'the best logodaidalon, a Byzantine man.' Phaedrus supplies the proper name Theodorus (ton chreston Theodoron). The coinage logodaidalon is a composite noun built on Daedalus, the mythic craftsman, applied here to rhetorical production.
limits: This observation does not analyze the rhetorical techniques listed, nor does it assess Socrates' attitude toward Theodorus. It records only the prosopographic naming practice of using a compound epithet with craft connotations.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0263
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 266e-267a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 266e-267a
  start_marker: 266e
  end_marker: 267a
  start_char: 79269
  end_char: 79938
  text_sha256: 49a7f74b0ca36a96a467280834dff9c2027d95fd671dad71276b310b51690dd2
greek_terms:
  - πρὸ τῶν ἀληθῶν τὰ εἰκότα
  - τιμητέα μᾶλλον
english_gloss: they saw that probabilities should be honored more than truths
observation: Socrates reports that Teisias and Gorgias 'saw that probabilities should be honored more than truths' (pro ton alethon ta eikota eidon hos timetea mallon), a formulation that presents a reversal of epistemic priorities without explicit condemnation, characteristic of mock-deferential irony.
textual_basis: At 267a Socrates says Teisias and Gorgias are to be left to sleep, having discovered that probabilities merit greater honor than truths, and that they make small things appear great and great things small through the power of speech. The description attributes to them a deliberate preference for the merely probable over the true, framed with the ironic suggestion that the orators themselves are now asleep.
limits: This observation identifies the ironic framing. It does not evaluate whether the characterization of Teisias and Gorgias is historically accurate, nor does it make claims about Plato's doctrine of rhetoric.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0264
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 266e-267a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 266e-267a
  start_marker: 266e
  end_marker: 267a
  start_char: 79269
  end_char: 79938
  text_sha256: 49a7f74b0ca36a96a467280834dff9c2027d95fd671dad71276b310b51690dd2
greek_terms:
  - σμικρὰ μεγάλα
  - μεγάλα σμικρὰ
  - ῥώμην λόγου
english_gloss: small things great and great things small through the power of speech
observation: Socrates attributes to Teisias and Gorgias a rhetorical technique characterized as making small things appear great and great things appear small through the power of speech (dia rhomen logou), presenting this scaling reversal as a teachable method.
textual_basis: At 267a, directly after the probability-over-truth characterization, Socrates adds that Teisias and Gorgias make small things great and great things small appear through the might of speech. The language of magnitude reversal (smikra megala, megala smikra) treated as a technique caused by speech-force presents a deliberate scaling manipulation as a rhetorical craft operation.
limits: This observation records the scale-manipulation claim attributed to these rhetoricians. It does not assess whether such scale reversal is possible or whether Plato endorses any alternative account of scale in speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0265
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 277b-277c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 277b-277c
  start_marker: 277b
  end_marker: 277c
  start_char: 99676
  end_char: 100456
  text_sha256: eee183fdd14c4febe782261b53ef9d224e0261322f0a6add3f919dbfded2c9a3
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates recapitulates the conditions for a speech's being 'with craft' (entechnon) that a logos in whatever form (written or spoken) must meet: knowing the truth about each subject, being able to define it, dividing it by forms down to the indivisible, discerning the nature of soul and fitting the appropriate form of logos to each soul type. He then declares that these conditions showed that appearing with craft is not possible otherwise, referring back to the preceding account."
textual_basis: "At 277b Socrates says that what is entechnon and what is not has been moderately shown (dedelosthai metrios), then at Phaedrus's request restates the conditions: truth about subjects (to alethes hekaston), definition kata te pan, division by eide down to the indivisible (mechri tou atmetou temnein), discerning soul's nature, and fitting logoi to soul types. At 277c he concludes with the reference 'as the entire preceding logos has indicated to us' (hos ho emprosthen pas memenyken hemin logos)."
limits: This is a recapitulation of methodological conditions, not a new substantive claim. The observation does not evaluate whether the conditions are satisfied by the preceding speeches.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0266
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 277b-277c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 277b-277c
  start_marker: 277b
  end_marker: 277c
  start_char: 99676
  end_char: 100456
  text_sha256: eee183fdd14c4febe782261b53ef9d224e0261322f0a6add3f919dbfded2c9a3
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates specifies the method of division by forms (kat' eide) down to the indivisible (mechri tou atmetou) as a necessary condition for speaking or writing with craft. This restates the diairetic method as a formal requirement for technically competent logos.
textual_basis: At 277b Socrates says that one must be able to divide by forms 'down to the indivisible' (kat' eide mechri tou atmetou temnein epistethe). This repeats the diairetic procedure earlier exemplified in the dialogue and now elevated to a general condition.
limits: This observation identifies a restatement of the diairetic method as a craft-condition. It does not assess whether the division has been successfully carried out or whether the method is adequate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates states that a technically competent logos must fit the appropriate form to each soul nature: variegated and all-harmonious logoi for a variegated soul, simple for a simple soul. This articulates a craft principle of matching speech type to psychic type as a condition for rhetorical techne."
textual_basis: At 277b-277c Socrates says that one must discover the form fitting each soul nature (to prosarmotton hekaste physei eidos aneuriskon) and thus set and arrange the logos, 'giving variegated and all-harmonious logoi to a variegated soul, simple to a simple' (poikile men poikilous psyche kai panarmonious didous logous, haplous de haple).
limits: This observation describes the soul-type-to-logos-fitting condition as stated. It does not evaluate whether this condition is met or whether the psychology assumed is defensible.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0268
source_work: Phaedrus

  end_char: 94716
  text_sha256: 58fe6d4c3fd378028f35135dd2587e53fce6b6055faae505587d902e6d6e5b74
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates introduces the account of Theuth and Thamus with an explicit truth-hedge, stating that he has a report (akoe) from the men of old, but the truth they themselves know, and asking whether discovering the truth on their own would render human opinions irrelevant.
textual_basis: At the opening of 274c Socrates says he has a hearing-report from the men of old (akoe), disclaims direct knowledge of the truth (to d'alethes autoi isasin), and contrasts hearsay with self-discovery. Phaedrus dismisses the question as ridiculous (geloion) and directs Socrates to proceed with the account he has heard, confirming the reported-tradition frame.
limits: This observation records the truth-hedge and the transition into reported mythic narrative. It does not assess whether the subsequent account of Theuth is treated as true or merely illustrative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0269
source_work: Phaedrus
```
