```yaml
  end_char: 94716
  text_sha256: 58fe6d4c3fd378028f35135dd2587e53fce6b6055faae505587d902e6d6e5b74
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Theuth, identified as an ancient Egyptian god (daimon) whose sacred bird is the ibis, is credited with the first discovery of number and calculation, geometry and astronomy, draughts and dice, and especially writing (grammata).
textual_basis: "At 274c-d Socrates identifies Theuth as one of the ancient gods near Naucratis, associates him with the sacred ibis, and lists his inventions in sequence: number (arithmon), calculation (logismon), geometry (geometrian), astronomy (astronomian), draughts (petteias), dice (kybeias), and letters (grammata). The catalogue attributes foundational cultural arts to a single divine inventor, a classic mythic aetiology pattern."
limits: This observation records the mythic invention catalogue. It does not assess whether Plato endorses the account or treats it as allegorical.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0270
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 265d-265e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 265d-265e
  start_marker: 265d
  end_marker: 265e
  start_char: 76906
  end_char: 77541
  text_sha256: 1c722e7f8f4c98b804bfd1da98d59b9cf19cf0ad56b06949fd3f3989c62df5dc
greek_terms:
  - εἰς μίαν τε ἰδέαν συνορῶντα ἄγειν τὰ πολλαχῇ διεσπαρμένα
  - κατ’ εἴδη δύνασθαι διατέμνειν κατ’ ἄρθρα ᾗ πέφυκεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates describes two complementary dialectical procedures: (1) bringing scattered multiplicities together into a single form by seeing them together and defining each, and (2) dividing along natural joints according to forms, avoiding the method of a bad butcher who shatters parts. He explicitly calls these two procedures by the same root term 'power' (dynamis) and treats them as a pair of forms or kinds (eidos) of discursive procedure.
textual_basis: |
  At 265d, Socrates asks whether one could grasp the power of 'the two' by art, then names the first procedure: bringing together into one form things scattered in many places, so that by defining each one makes clear whatever one wishes to teach (265d). At 265e, he names the second: being able to cut again according to forms at the natural joints, and not attempting to shatter any part in the manner of a bad butcher. Phaedrus's question at 265e explicitly frames the second as 'the other form' (to d' heteron eidos), confirming that Socrates presents these as a paired classification of dialectical method.
limits: |
  The passage introduces the pair of procedures but does not here evaluate their success; Socrates defers judgment on whether the earlier speeches about Eros were done well or badly (265d). The observation does not claim that this is a formal definition of dialectic, only that the text presents two named procedures under a single art-like power.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0271
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 265d-265e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 265d-265e
  start_marker: 265d
  end_marker: 265e
  start_char: 76906
  end_char: 77541
  text_sha256: 1c722e7f8f4c98b804bfd1da98d59b9cf19cf0ad56b06949fd3f3989c62df5dc
greek_terms:
  - κακοῦ μαγείρου τρόπῳ
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates contrasts proper division according to natural joints with the method of a bad butcher (kakos mageiros) who shatters parts. He also frames both collection and division as a 'power' (dynamis) that could be grasped 'by art' (techne), and illustrates collection by reference to the earlier speeches about Eros, which he says gained clarity and self-consistency through definition. The passage thus maps the procedures of dialectic onto craft domains — butchery as a negative analogue, and the art-like grasp of a power as a positive one.
textual_basis: |
  At 265d Socrates says: 'if one could grasp the power of the two by art (techne), it would not be without grace.' At 265e he warns against shattering any part 'in the manner of a bad butcher' (kakou mageirou tropo), which is explicitly a craft-domain comparison used to regulate the procedure of division. The conjunction of techne language with the butchery simile establishes a craft analogy governing dialectical method.
limits: |
  The butchery reference is a simile, not a full-blown analogy mapped point-by-point. The observation records the craft-comparative framing but does not claim that dialectic is formally classified as a techne in this passage; Socrates only says it would be 'not without grace' to grasp its power by art.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates retroactively characterizes the palinode speech they have just delivered as a "mythic hymn," explicitly classifying the discourse as belonging to the mythic register (265b-265c).
textual_basis: At 265b-265c, Socrates says they "played a mythic hymn" after recapitulating the four-part divine madness division. This is a meta-discursive label applied by the speaker to his own immediately preceding speech, demarcating it as myth rather than logos.
limits: This observation records the explicit mythic self-classification of the palinode; it does not assess whether the speech is actually mythical by other criteria or analyze its content.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates asserts that most of what was said in the preceding speeches has been played in play, framing the prior discourse as unserious while singling out two forms that emerged by chance as worthy of serious attention (265c).
textual_basis: At 265c, Socrates states that "the rest seems to me really to have been played in play," immediately before identifying two forms that arose by chance from what was said. This creates an explicit play/seriousness boundary within the discourse.
limits: This observation notes the procedural framing of the prior speeches as play; it does not determine whether Socrates is being ironic or whether the palinode is genuinely unserious.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 265c, Socrates proposes to examine how the speech was able to pass from blaming to praising. This is an explicit procedural marker that identifies the turn from one rhetorical mode to its opposite as the subject of methodological reflection.
limits: This observation records the marking of the blame-to-praise transition; it does not analyze the method of division and collection that Socrates will introduce in the immediately following lines.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0275
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278c-278d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278c-278d
  start_marker: 278c
  end_marker: 278d
  start_char: 102186
  end_char: 102979
  text_sha256: 39a08fe195e5bfe3daa9ca4f7e538aced37b1ac9007ca9a973ee5c48506627be
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates constructs a ranked taxonomy of logoi-composers — Lysias or any other speech-writer (logoi), Homer or any other poet composing bare poetry or poetry in song, Solon or any political law-writer — and subsumes them under a single criterion: whether the composer composed with knowledge of the truth and can defend his compositions in cross-examination.
textual_basis: |
  At 278c Socrates enumerates three classes: Lysias and other composers of logoi, Homer and other poets, Solon and those who write political law-nomoi (sugggrammata). He then states the criterion: if the composer composed these knowing how the truth holds (eidōs hēi to alēthes echei), can come to the aid of his writings in elenchus, and can himself demonstrate that his written compositions are phaula (worthless), then such a one is not to be called by the craft-names attached to what he wrote but by what he spouded upon.
limits: |
  The observation does not claim that this is a definition of philosophy or that the taxonomy is exhaustive. It notes a structured definitional target — an unnamed activity — offered via a criterion and a threefold composer catalogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0276
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278c-278d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278c-278d
  start_marker: 278c
  end_marker: 278d
  start_char: 102186
  end_char: 102979
  text_sha256: 39a08fe195e5bfe3daa9ca4f7e538aced37b1ac9007ca9a973ee5c48506627be
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates proposes that the proper appellation for the composer who knows the truth and can defend his writings is not 'poet,' 'speech-writer,' or 'lawgiver' but 'philosopher' or something of that sort, and reserves 'sophos' (wise) for the god alone.
textual_basis: |
  At 278c-d Socrates states that the composer who meets the knowledge-and-defense criterion should not be called by the epōnymiai (titles) that attach to what he wrote but by those things upon which he was earnest (eph' hois espoudaken ekeinōn). When Phaedrus asks what titles Socrates assigns (278d), Socrates replies that to call the man sophon seems a great thing that befits god alone, while philososophos or something of that sort would be more fitting and more harmonious (harmottoi kai emmelesterōs echoi).
limits: |
  This observation records a specific naming proposal, not a full definition of philosophy. The term 'philosopher' is offered as a preferred epōnymia (name) for the truth-knowing composer, not a settled definition.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0277
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278c-278d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278c-278d
  start_marker: 278c
  end_marker: 278d
  start_char: 102186
  end_char: 102979
  text_sha256: 39a08fe195e5bfe3daa9ca4f7e538aced37b1ac9007ca9a973ee5c48506627be
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  Socrates defers the term 'sophos' (wise) to the god alone, calling it too great (mega) for a human, and names 'philosopher' or something akin as the more fitting alternative for the truth-knowing writer — a self-deprecating move that simultaneously elevates the divine and lowers the human epistemic claim.
textual_basis: |
  At 278d Socrates says: to call him sophon seems to me a great thing and belongs to god alone. The contrast between the divine-only predicate sophon and the human-accessible predicate philosophos describes a differential epistemic status that is self-deprecating in its refusal to claim sophia for Socrates or any human.
limits: |
  This observation notes the irony of self-deprecation through divine reservation, not a claim that Socrates secretly believes he is wise. The text explicitly says sophon befits only the god.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0278
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 275d-275e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 97204
  text_sha256: 548e8e0801ad6c6d43eead4b1f8f3776acfd172467c12012b6172ec8d77695c4
greek_terms:
  - γραφή
  - ζωγραφίᾳ
english_gloss: writing ... painting
observation: "Socrates compares written discourse to painting (275d): the products of painting stand as if alive but remain solemnly silent when questioned; written logoi behave identically, seeming to speak with intelligence yet always signifying only one and the same thing when interrogated."
textual_basis: "At 275d Socrates explicitly asserts that writing has the same defect as painting: the offspring of painting stand as though living but keep a very solemn silence when questioned. He then applies the identical pattern to written logoi."
limits: This analogy between writing and painting occurs within a broader argument about the limitations of writing; it does not evaluate painting as a craft in itself but uses it instrumentally to characterize a defect of written discourse.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0279
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 275d-275e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 275d-275e
  start_marker: 275d
  end_marker: 275e
  start_char: 96468
  end_char: 97204
  text_sha256: 548e8e0801ad6c6d43eead4b1f8f3776acfd172467c12012b6172ec8d77695c4
greek_terms:
  - κυλινδεῖται
  - ἐπαΐουσιν
english_gloss: is rolled about ... to those who understand
observation: "At 275d-275e Socrates claims that once a logos is written down it rolls around everywhere, reaching both those who understand and those for whom it is inappropriate, and cannot defend itself when mistreated: it always needs its father to come to its aid."
textual_basis: At 275e Socrates says a written logos once committed to writing rolls about everywhere alike among those who understand and those to whom it does not belong, does not know to whom it should speak and to whom not, and when abused or reviled unjustly always needs its father as defender, being unable to help or defend itself.
limits: This observation records the helplessness image, not a claim about the validity or invalidity of writing as such. The father-son language is metaphorical and does not establish a formal craft analogy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - παιδιάν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus characterizes Socrates' account of dialectical sowing of speeches as a 'most beautiful play (paidia) contrasted with a frivolous play' at 276e, and Socrates immediately elevates the contrast by calling the serious practice (spoude) of dialectical art planted in a fitting soul 'much finer' (276e).
textual_basis: At 276e Phaedrus uses paidia to bracket the preceding mythologizing as play while distinguishing it as a beautiful kind of play. Socrates at 276e amplifies the play/serious (paidia/spoude) distinction by introducing dialectical planting as the truly serious enterprise.
limits: This observation does not adjudicate whether the entire Palinode or earlier speeches are being retrospectively demoted to play; it records only the explicit play-serious contrast vocabulary in this span.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0282
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 276e-277a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 276e-277a
  start_marker: 276e
  end_marker: 277a
  start_char: 98874
  end_char: 99676
  text_sha256: e7368158642c0ab059377bc972aa7bca283d72090b3b46aede86e439c174ce84
greek_terms:
  - διαλεκτικῇ τέχνῃ
  - ψυχὴν προσήκουσαν
  - φυτεύῃ
  - σπείρῃ
  - σπέρμα
  - ἀθάνατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates at 276e-277a describes the dialectical art through an extended agricultural metaphor: using dialectical art (dialektike techne) one takes a fitting soul (psychen prosekousan), plants and sows speeches with knowledge (phuteue te kai speire met' epistemes logous), and these speeches contain seed (sperma) that grows in other characters (ethe) and renders the possessor happy to the extent possible for a human being."
textual_basis: The passage at 276e-277a elaborates a coherent metaphor mapping farming operations (planting, sowing, bearing seed, producing new growth in other soil/characters) onto the dialectician's activity of implanting living speeches in souls, with the product described as immortal (athanaton).
limits: This observation records the agricultural metaphor for dialectical speech; it does not assess whether the metaphor acknowledges the earlier critique of writing in the Theuth-Ammon myth or resolves the tension between written and spoken discourse.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0283
source_work: Phaedrus

  - τούτων ὡμολογημένων
  - ὄνειδος

observation: "Socrates at 277a marks the transition from the discussion of dialectical planting to a judgment (krinein) about earlier topics: 'Now, Phaedrus, we are able to judge those matters, these things having been agreed' (nun de ekeina ede dunametha krinein, touton homologemenon). Phaedrus prompts clarification ('What things?') and Socrates references the original purpose of examining Lysias' reproach (oneidos) concerning speech-writing."
textual_basis: At the end of 277a Socrates explicitly signals a procedural turn by using 'now already' (nun de ede) to mark that the preceding agreements now enable judgment of the earlier questions. The exchange recovers the stated purpose from earlier in the dialogue (examining Lysias' reproach).
limits: This observation records only the structural transition marker; it does not evaluate whether the judgment actually occurs in what follows or whether the intervening agricultural imagery adequately prepared for it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - Ἄδραστον
  - Περικλέα
  - ὦ Φαῖδρέ τε καὶ Σώκρατες
  - ἐπιπλῆξαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates uses Sophocles and Acumenus as parallel cases to distinguish preparatory material (ta pro) from the craft proper (ta tragika, ta iatrika), then extends this distinction to rhetoric.
textual_basis: At 269a, Socrates says Sophocles would say that someone displaying what comes before tragedy (ta pro tragodias) is not displaying tragic matters (ta tragika), and Acumenus would say the same about what comes before medicine (ta pro iatrikes) versus medical matters (ta iatrika). The analogy is immediately applied to rhetoric.
limits: The passage does not define what constitutes the craft proper of rhetoric; it only marks a boundary between preliminary learning and the craft itself. The analogy is asserted, not argued for.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates invokes Sophocles and Acumenus as authorities for distinguishing preparatory studies from a craft, then imagines Adrastus and Pericles rebuking those who mistake preliminaries for rhetoric itself.
textual_basis: At 269a, Sophocles and Acumenus are named as parallel authority cases. At 269a-b, the 'sweet-voiced Adrastus' and Pericles are imagined as potential critics who would reproach Phaedrus and Socrates for harshness, and who would diagnose the error of those who possess only preliminary learning (ta pro tes technes) but think they have found rhetoric.
limits: This observation records the use of named figures as argumentative touchstones, not their historical accuracy. The passage does not quote any actual speech by Pericles or Adrastus; it constructs a hypothetical rebuke.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0286
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 269a-269b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 269a-269b
  start_marker: 269a
  end_marker: 269b
  start_char: 83261
  end_char: 84104
  text_sha256: ee459fb4d6b99a7f8e1e1c4eb7b8f788341160e323ab1411baa98634e4436747
greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates constructs an imagined speech by Adrastus and Pericles that rebukes himself and Phaedrus, then offers a diagnosis of those who mistake preparatory learning for the craft of rhetoric.
textual_basis: At 269b, Socrates shifts into a hypothetical direct quotation attributed to Adrastus and Pericles, addressing himself and Phaedrus by name ('O Phaedrus and Socrates'). The imagined speakers diagnose that those who cannot engage in dialectic (dialegesthai) were unable to define what rhetoric is, and from this condition (pathos) they possessed only the necessary preliminary learning but thought they had found rhetoric.
limits: This observation records the ventriloquism device; it does not assess whether the imagined speech accurately represents Pericles' views. The passage does not claim Pericles actually said this.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0287

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates names Isocrates as his companion (hetairos) and offers a prophetic assessment comparing Isocrates's nature favorably against Lysias and his circle.
textual_basis: At 278e-279a Phaedrus asks Socrates what message he will bring to 'Isocrates the beautiful' (Isokrate ton kalon), explicitly calling Isocrates Socrates's companion. Socrates responds by contrasting Isocrates's nature (ta tes physeos) favorably against 'those around Lysias' (tous peri Lysian), predicting Isocrates may surpass all previous speechwriters.
limits: This observation records the naming and comparison as dramatic data. It does not assess the historical accuracy of the prophecy or Socrates's actual relationship with Isocrates.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0288

greek_terms:
  - μαντεύομαι
english_gloss: I prophesy
observation: Socrates prefaces his assessment of Isocrates with an explicit prophecy disclaimer, marking his prediction as divinatory rather than certain knowledge.
textual_basis: At 279a Socrates says 'what I prophesy (manteuomai) concerning him, I wish to speak.' The verb manteuomai frames the subsequent praise of Isocrates as prophecy, distancing Socrates from a claim of settled knowledge about Isocrates's future.
limits: This records the prophecy marker at the surface level. It does not claim that the disclaimer is ironic or that Socrates is concealing criticism, only that the text marks the statement as prophecy rather than knowledge.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0290
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 270e-271a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 270e-271a
  start_marker: 270e
  end_marker: 271a
  start_char: 86877
  end_char: 87571
  text_sha256: 5b9cc5ad45e595379af8b1553e091278f9f9800025183fba4336638cd7efee2b
greek_terms:
  - τέχνῃ
  - οὐσίαν
  - φύσεως
  - ψυχὴ
english_gloss: Socrates argues that someone who practices an art of giving speeches should, with precision, reveal the nature (ousia) of the thing to which the speeches are addressed — in this case, the soul.
observation: Socrates stipulates that anyone who gives speeches with art (technê) must show the precise nature (ousia) of the soul, since the soul is the target to which speeches are addressed. This imports a craft-like requirement of accurate knowledge of the object into rhetorical teaching (270e-271a).
textual_basis: At 270e Socrates says one must not liken the art-practitioner to a blind or deaf person, but must demonstrate with exactness the ousia of the nature to which speeches are applied; then at 271a he names the soul (psychê) as that object. The passage frames rhetorical skill as a technê that requires knowledge of the soul's nature.
limits: The passage does not yet define what kind of soul-nature knowledge suffices; it only establishes a criterion for the art of speech-making.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ποιεῖν
  - ἐπιχειρεῖ
  - Θρασύμαχός
  - τέχνην ῥητορικὴν
english_gloss: Socrates claims that the contest (hamilla) is entirely devoted to producing persuasion in the soul, and mentions Thrasymachus and anyone else who seriously teaches rhetorical art.
observation: Socrates names Thrasymachus as an example of someone who would seriously teach rhetorical art and who would therefore need to produce a precise account of the soul's nature (271a). The reference anchors the methodological demand in a recognizable contemporary figure.
textual_basis: At 271a Socrates names Thrasymachus alongside whoever else seriously teaches rhetorical art. This is a prosopographic reference to a known historical figure, used within an argument about what rhetorical technê requires.
limits: The reference does not provide any biographical detail about Thrasymachus beyond his association with rhetorical teaching; it merely uses his name as a representative type.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0292
source_work: Phaedrus

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 270e-271a
  start_marker: 270e
  end_marker: 271a
  start_char: 86877
  end_char: 87571
  text_sha256: 5b9cc5ad45e595379af8b1553e091278f9f9800025183fba4336638cd7efee2b
greek_terms:
  - ἓν
  - ὅμοιον
  - πέφυκεν
  - πολυειδές
  - μορφὴν
english_gloss: Socrates states that the first task is to determine whether the soul is one and uniform (hen kai homoion) by nature or multiform (polueides) like the shape of the body.
observation: "Socrates sets a two-step program: first, determine whether the soul is one and uniform by nature or multiform (like bodily shape); second, determine what the soul naturally does or suffers. This marks the soul's nature as a definiendum requiring systematic investigation (271a)."
textual_basis: At 271a Socrates announces a first step — to see whether the soul is one and alike by nature or multiform after the manner of the body's shape — and a second step about what the soul does or suffers. This is a structured program for investigating the soul's nature.
limits: The passage only announces the program; it does not execute the investigation or reach any conclusion about the soul's nature.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - σπείρων
  - κήπους
  - σπερεῖ
  - φυομένους ἁπαλούς
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates extends the agriculture-writing analogy: the philosopher writes in gardens of writing and sows seeds through a reed pen, enjoying watching the written words grow tender (276d)."
textual_basis: The sustained agricultural vocabulary — seeds, farmer, sowing, gardens, will sow, growing tender — maps the activity of writing onto the domain of farming across 276c-276d.
limits: This observation tracks the recurrence of the agriculture-writing analogy; it does not evaluate whether the analogy is sound or assess Plato's theory of writing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0294
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 276c-276d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  text_sha256: 2a3dcd2aa81e2ac3b066464cb798cc9233adcf9b5d3e8265a1d31161a01b6b4d
greek_terms:
  - σπουδῇ
  - παιδιᾶς χάριν
  - παίζων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates frames writing as play in explicit contrast with serious philosophical activity, and characterizes the written text as a pastime alternative to symposia and similar amusements (276d).
textual_basis: At 276c Socrates asks whether the knower of just, beautiful, and good things would write seriously in water; at 276d he says such a person writes for the sake of play, and that when others use drinking parties and similar amusements, this person will pass time playing with written discourses.
limits: This observation notes the explicit play/seriousness contrast framing writing; it does not claim that Plato secretly valued writing or that all writing is play for Plato.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  - ἀδυνάτων δὲ ἱκανῶς τἀληθῆ διδάξαι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates states that written discourses are unable to defend themselves with speech and unable to teach the truth adequately (276c).
textual_basis: At 276c Socrates directly predicates two incapacities of written logoi — inability to defend themselves and inability to teach truth adequately — as part of the argument that the knowledgeable person would not write seriously.
limits: This observation records the stated deficiencies of written discourse at 276c; it does not evaluate whether these claims are consistent with Plato's own practice of writing dialogues.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0296

greek_terms:
  - παιδιάν
  - ἀνακρίσεως
  - διδαχῆς
  - ἐν ψυχῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates frames the entire practice of writing logoi as play (paidia) rather than great seriousness (spoude), directly subordinating written composition to a category of leisure activity.
textual_basis: At 277e Socrates states that the one who holds that in written discourse concerning each subject much play is necessary (paidia ... pollē anankaion einai) and that no discourse in meter or without meter has ever been written worthy of great seriousness (megalēs axion spoudēs).
limits: This observation records the play/seriousness contrast at the segment level. It does not assess whether Socrates is consistent with his own earlier mythic or erotic speeches.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0297
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 277d-277e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  []
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates claims that ignorance of just and unjust things, both waking and dreaming, cannot escape being truly shameful regardless of the praise of the whole mob.
textual_basis: At 277d-277e Socrates says that not knowing (to agnoein) just and unjust, bad and good things, waking and dreaming (hypar te kai onar), does not escape being in truth reproachable (ouk ekpheugei tē alētheia mē ouk eponeidiston einai), not even if the entire crowd praises it.
limits: This observation notes the epistemic criterion for shame attached to written compositions. It does not address whether the claim applies to Socrates' own activity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0298
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 277e-278a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 101753

greek_terms:
  - παιδιάν
  - ἀνακρίσεως
  - διδαχῆς
  - ἐν ψυχῇ
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates contrasts written discourse treated as play with the discourse that is taught and learned for the sake of instruction and is truly written in the soul.
textual_basis: At 277e Socrates describes written discourse as involving much play, while rhapsodized discourse without examination or teaching is not worthy of serious effort. At 278a he distinguishes the discourse taught for learning and truly written in the soul about just, beautiful, and good things as clear, complete, and worthy of seriousness.
limits: This observation covers the written-play and soul-written contrast in this span. It does not evaluate whether the earlier speeches in the Phaedrus themselves meet the standard of examined speech.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0299
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 275b-275c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 275b-275c
  start_marker: 275b

  end_char: 96468
  text_sha256: 612dc1a12a614c9af0dbf91c547019790b3455b0bb95b8897ebe43ae4fe8bdda
greek_terms:
  - δρυὸς
  - πέτρας
  - μαντικοὺς
  - Δωδωναίου
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates reports that the first prophetic speeches arose from the oak at the sanctuary of Zeus at Dodona, introducing a new mythic or religious source frame for originary logos immediately after Phaedrus remarks on Socrates' facility in producing Egyptian or any other kind of stories at 275b.
textual_basis: At 275b–c, after Phaedrus comments on Socrates' ease in fabricating Egyptian-style tales, Socrates counters by citing a tradition that prophetic speeches first came from the Dodonian oak, shifting the mythic frame from Egypt to a Greek oracular site.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates endorses the Dodona tradition; it only records the introduction of a new reported divine-source frame within the argument about writing.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0300
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 275b-275c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 275b-275c
  start_marker: 275b
  end_marker: 275c
  start_char: 95632
  end_char: 96468
  text_sha256: 612dc1a12a614c9af0dbf91c547019790b3455b0bb95b8897ebe43ae4fe8bdda
greek_terms:
  - εὐηθείας
  - σοφοῖς
  - δοξόσοφοι
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates applies ironic contrast between the simplicity of earlier generations who were content to listen to an oak or a rock so long as what they said was true, and Phaedrus and 'you young men' who are sophoi (wise), describing the earlier listeners as acting out of naivety (euetheia) at 275c.
textual_basis: At 275c Socrates says the men of that earlier time, since they were not wise like 'you young men' (hymeis hoi neoi), were content to listen to an oak and a rock out of euetheia, provided only that they spoke truth; this reverses the expected valuation of wisdom and naivety for ironic effect.
limits: This observation records the ironic framing; it does not assert that Socrates actually considers Phaedrus wise or the ancients naive.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 262e, Socrates halts Phaedrus' recitation, then declares they must state what fault the speech commits and what makes it artless.
limits: This observation records the interruption and methodological pivot. It does not claim Socrates is rejecting rhetoric wholesale or that the speech is worthless.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0303

  - ὁμονοητικῶς
  - στασιωτικῶς
  - σιδήρου
  - ἀργύρου
  - δικαίου
  - ἀγαθοῦ
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates distinguishes between two classes of terms: some (like iron and silver) about which people agree, and others (like the just and the good) about which people dispute and are carried in different directions."
textual_basis: At 263a, Socrates asks whether it is clear to everyone that about some things we agree while about others we are in strife. He gives the examples of iron/silver (unanimous understanding) versus just/good (people disagree with each other and with themselves).
limits: This observation records the distinction between term types. It does not claim Socrates is offering a theory of definition or that this exhausts his taxonomy of terms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0304
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278a-278b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278a-278b
  start_marker: 278a
  end_marker: 278b
  start_char: 101339
  end_char: 102186
  text_sha256: d12f0064a210764d1e666e2776d291dd578ffeca895a36706d84294ececd512b
greek_terms:
  - ὑπόμνησιν
english_gloss: reminder
observation: "Socrates reinterprets the Thamus-ammon story by applying its written/spoken distinction to contemporary discursive practices: written logoi are a reminder for those who already know, while genuinely instructive spoken logoi are written in the soul."
textual_basis: Socrates says at 278a that in the written logoi the best ones serve as a reminder for those who already know, but in the spoken logoi taught and learned for the sake of learning he holds that only these are clear, complete, and worthy of serious effort. The myth's judgment is applied as a criterion for evaluating discourse types.
limits: This observation only notes that Socrates applies the myth's distinction post-narrative. It does not claim that the myth is an allegory for Platonic doctrine or that the application is meant to be esoteric. The passage is at 278a, not earlier in the myth narrative.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0305
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278a-278b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278a-278b
  start_marker: 278a
  end_marker: 278b
  start_char: 101339
  end_char: 102186
  text_sha256: d12f0064a210764d1e666e2776d291dd578ffeca895a36706d84294ececd512b
greek_terms:
  - ὑεῖς γνησίους
  - ἀδελφοὶ
english_gloss: legitimate sons, offspring, brothers
observation: "Socrates uses a kinship metaphor to distinguish the spoken logos in the soul from its written counterparts: the internal logos is a legitimate son, while external logoi planted in other souls are its offspring and brothers."
textual_basis: "Socrates says at 278a-b that such spoken logoi should be called legitimate sons: first the one within himself, if it is found within him, then any offspring and brothers of this one that are planted in other souls according to their worth. The metaphor applies the kinship language directly to logoi."
limits: This observation only records the kinship metaphor applied to logoi at 278a-b. It does not analyze whether this implies a hierarchy of logoi or whether the metaphor is consistent with the earlier myth. It does not claim that the legitimate son is a reference to Platonic forms.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0306
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 278a-278b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 278a-278b
  start_marker: 278a
  end_marker: 278b
  start_char: 101339
  end_char: 102186
  text_sha256: d12f0064a210764d1e666e2776d291dd578ffeca895a36706d84294ececd512b
greek_terms:
  - εὔχομαι
english_gloss: we would pray, I pray
observation: "Socrates and Phaedrus end the discourse evaluation with a shared prayer: Socrates says that the sort of man they have described is the kind they would both pray to become, and Phaedrus responds that he entirely wishes and prays for what Socrates says."
textual_basis: Socrates says at 278b that this sort of man, Phaedrus, is such as both I and you would pray that you and I might become. Phaedrus responds at 278b that absolutely he both wishes and prays for what Socrates says. This is a joint prayer closing, not a single-speaker prayer or a departure.
limits: This observation only records the joint prayer formula at 278b. It does not claim that the prayer is a veiled commitment to a philosophical life or that it signals an esoteric initiation. The observation is about the closure type, not about the content of what is prayed for.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0307
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - εὐξαμένῳ
  - συνεύχου
  - ἴωμεν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates closes the dialogue by offering a prayer to Pan and the local gods, and Phaedrus asks to share in it before they depart.
textual_basis: At 279b, Socrates proposes that it is fitting to pray before departing (279b), then addresses Pan and the other local gods with a prayer (279b-c). At 279c, Phaedrus requests that Socrates pray for these same things with him too and cites the proverb about friends' things being common. Socrates then says 'let us go' (279c), ending the dialogue.
limits: This observation identifies the structural function of the prayer as dialogue closure and its shared character. It does not interpret the theological content of the prayer.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0308
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 279b-279c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 279b-279c
  start_marker: 279b
  end_marker: 279c
  start_char: 103778
  end_char: 104425
  text_sha256: c32bb282a3c82668bf8aba792d79e043565ec4ba2400fcaa26a50f62a1b93221
greek_terms:
  - πλούσιον
  - σοφόν
  - σώφρων
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates' closing prayer inverts common valuations by equating wisdom with wealth and by setting a limit on gold possession that only the moderate person can meet.
textual_basis: At 279c, Socrates prays that he may consider the wise person rich and that he may have only as much gold as the moderate person alone could carry. This formulation inverts the ordinary identification of wealth with material possession and ties the capacity for gold-holding to sophrosyne rather than to acquisitiveness.
limits: This observation notes the inversion structure within the prayer. It does not assert that Socrates is offering a definition of wealth or that this is an esoteric doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0309

english_gloss: ""
observation: Phaedrus deploys the proverb 'the things of friends are common' to justify sharing in Socrates' closing prayer.
textual_basis: At 279c, after Socrates completes his prayer, Phaedrus says 'pray for these things with me too, for the things of friends are common.' The proverb serves as the explicit rationale for extending the prayer's content to Phaedrus.
limits: This observation identifies the pragmatic function of the proverb citation in closing the dialogue. It does not assess the proverb's truth or its broader Platonic reception.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0310
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 267d-268a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 267d-268a

  end_marker: 268a
  start_char: 80749
  end_char: 81637
  text_sha256: fcd9e561f83e5d47c4f61001792d7b0ad6eac58522ab677622b08075c65d7437
greek_terms:
  - ἐπάνοδον
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates notes that the name for the closing summary device of speeches is disputed: some call it 'epanodos' (recapitulation), others assign a different name, though all agree the thing itself exists (267d)."
textual_basis: At 267d Socrates says the end (telos) of speeches seems commonly agreed on, but some call it epanodos while others assign a different name. Socrates is cataloguing rhetorical devices attributed to various teachers.
limits: Socrates attributes this terminological observation to the culture of rhetorical handbooks, not to his own view. The observation does not settle whether the device is genuine techne.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0311

greek_terms:
  - σμικρά
  - ὑπ’ αὐγὰς
  - τὴν τῆς τέχνης δύναμιν
english_gloss: ""
observation: Socrates proposes to set aside small points (smi/ kra) and examine the rhetorical devices under a better light, asking what power of art (tekhne) they actually possess (268a).
textual_basis: At 268a Socrates says 'let us leave aside the small things' and proposes to look at the devices under light to see what and when they have the power of art. Phaedrus responds that they have great power in public assemblies.
limits: The passage signals a methodological turn but does not yet execute the examination. The scope delimitation is proposed, not completed.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0312
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 268a-268b

  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

  end_char: 82056
  text_sha256: a9a93f4587d7fc50aa057d659fe9b8212822341866f624abc6f6460990286fb8
greek_terms:
  - Ἐρυξιμάχῳ
  - Ἀκουμενῷ
  - σώμασι προσφέρειν
  - θερμαίνειν
  - ψύχειν
english_gloss: ""
observation: "Socrates introduces a medical craft test: a person who knows how to apply things to bodies to heat, cool, induce vomiting, or purge would still need the physician to ask whether he knows when, for whom, and how far to use them."
textual_basis: At 268a-268b Socrates imagines someone approaching Eryximachus or Acumenus and claiming medical expertise because he knows bodily applications that heat, cool, induce vomiting, and purge. Phaedrus answers that the physicians would ask whether he also knows whom to treat, when to use each item, and to what extent.
limits: This observation records the medical-craft analogy as a test for whether preliminary devices amount to a genuine art; it does not cover the later tragic and musical analogies.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 249d Socrates identifies the fourth madness as the case in which earthly beauty prompts recollection of true beauty and the soul begins to grow wings, while observers blame the person as mad.
textual_basis: The span names the fourth madness and links seeing beauty here with recollection of the true, wing-growth, upward-looking neglect of things below, and the charge of madness.
limits: This records the classification and symptoms stated in the span; it does not interpret the madness as pathology or doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The span explicitly calls this enthusiasm best among enthusiasms and ties the name lover to participation in this madness.
```

```yaml
observation: At 252e Zeus-following lovers seek a beloved whose soul is divine, philosophical, and fit to lead, and then try to make him that kind of person.
textual_basis: The span says these lovers examine whether the beloved is philosophical and leading by nature, and once they find and love him they do everything so that he will be such.
limits: This records the Zeus-specific selection pattern in the span; it does not generalize to every divine retinue.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The span states that lovers look intensely toward their god, touch him by memory, receive habits and practices from him while inspired, and pour these onto the beloved's soul.
limits: This records the paradigm-and-transfer structure; it does not claim a hidden doctrine about divine possession.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: The span gives the unruly horse a claim to make before the charioteer, then contrasts that pressure with the counter-tension of the yokemate and charioteer under shame and reason.
```

```yaml

observation: At 256b the philosophical victory of the better elements yields a blessed, harmonious, self-controlled life and a postmortem state in which the lovers become winged and light.
textual_basis: The span connects ordered life and philosophy with self-control, enslavement of the vice-producing element, liberation of the virtue-producing element, and becoming winged and light after death.
limits: This records the positive outcome for this pair; it does not cover the less philosophical path introduced after the span.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0319

observation: At 258a Socrates treats a political decree as a written speech by quoting its opening formula and noting that the writer praises himself in the record of who spoke.
textual_basis: The span cites the decree formula involving council or people, identifies the named speaker as self-praise by the writer, and then asks whether such a document is anything other than a written speech.
limits: This records Socrates' example of political writing; it does not assess the legal form or historical accuracy of the decree formula.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0320

  - τεθαυμακότες
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 258b Socrates says political writers rejoice if their written text remains, grieve if it is erased, and therefore do not disdain the practice but admire it.
textual_basis: The span contrasts the writer leaving the theater glad when the text remains with grief when it is erased and deprived of speechwriting, then states that such people admire the practice.
limits: This records Socrates' inversion of the shame claim; it does not prove that all political actors value writing in the same way.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The span frames the cicadas' gift as something from the gods, prompts Phaedrus's request for explanation, and begins a narrative about humans before the Muses and the appearance of song.
```

```yaml

textual_basis: The span narrates the neglect of food and drink, the unnoticed death, the emergence of the cicada race, its foodless and drinkless singing, and its reporting function before the Muses.
limits: This records the origin and function of the cicadas within the myth; it does not interpret the full ranking of individual Muses that continues after the span.
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0323
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240a-240c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 240a-240c

greek_terms:
  - ἄγαμον
  - ἄπαιδα
  - ἄοικον
  - φθονεῖν
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 240a the speech lists three deprivations the lover wishes upon the beloved — that he remain unmarried, childless, and homeless — so that the lover can enjoy his pleasure for the longest possible time. The passage also states that the lover envies the beloved's possessions and rejoices when they are lost.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 240a explicitly says the lover would pray for the beloved to become unmarried, childless, and homeless for the longest time, desiring to harvest his own sweet pleasure as long as possible. It also says the lover necessarily envies those with property and rejoices at its loss.
limits: |
  This observation records the speech's enumerated claims about the lover's desires; it does not assess the argument's validity or Socrates' later response to it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0324
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240a-240c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 240a-240c
  start_marker: 240a
  end_marker: 240c
  start_char: 25178
  end_char: 26411
  text_sha256: 2a22c358d5d447f10a4ace84803c646772b6db68372a253ef997b3070b09508f
greek_terms:
  - ἡδονήν
  - ἀηδέστατον
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 240b the speech argues that other harmful things — flatterers, wild beasts, courtesans — at least have some pleasure mixed in by nature, making them tolerable for daily association; but the lover, in addition to being harmful, is the most unpleasant of all for daily company, combining both harm and displeasure.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 240b states that a daemon has mixed immediate pleasure with most other bad things, and that although one might criticize a courtesan as harmful, such things provide the most daily pleasure. It then contrasts the lover, who is both harmful and most unpleasant for daily association with the beloved.
limits: |
  This observation records the speech's comparative structure; it does not evaluate whether the comparison is fair or how Socrates later engages it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0325
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 240a-240c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt

greek_terms:
  - παλαιὸς λόγος
  - ἥλικα
  - ἰσότης
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 240c the speech invokes an old saying — that like delights like — as authority for the claim that equality of age produces friendship through similarity. It then turns this proverb against the lover-beloved relationship, arguing that the inequality of age between an older lover and a younger beloved makes the association burdensome and compulsory.
textual_basis: |
  The text at 240c explicitly cites an old saying that like delights like, explains it through equality of time producing equal pleasures and friendship through similarity, and then applies it to show that the inequality between lover and beloved makes the older man's constant presence unwelcome and compulsory.
limits: |
  This observation records the speech's use of a traditional saying as argumentative support; it does not assess the saying's origin or its later role in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

textual_basis: The text states at 255c that the source of that stream which Zeus desiring Ganymede named "himeros" flows abundantly toward the beloved, presenting a mythic naming as part of the explanatory account.
limits: The observation notes the mythic reference's function within the passage; it does not assess the palinode's truth or Plato's intent.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0327
source_work: Phaedrus

greek_terms:
  - πνεῦμα
  - ἠχὼ
english_gloss: ""
observation: At 255c-255d, the passage likens the stream of beauty returning through the eyes into the beloved's soul to wind or an echo leaping from smooth and solid surfaces and bouncing back to its origin.
textual_basis: The text introduces the comparison with a word meaning "like" at 255c, stating that just as wind or echo from smooth, solid surfaces springs back to where it started, so the stream of beauty goes back through the eyes to the soul, watering the feather-paths and causing wings to grow (255c-255d).
limits: The observation identifies the analogy and its source and target domains; it does not evaluate the analogy's adequacy or claim it is central to the palinode's argument.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0328

  - κατόπτρῳ

observation: "At 255d, two analogies explain the beloved's unrecognized erotic state: a medical analogy to someone who has caught eye-inflammation from another and cannot state the cause, and a mirror analogy in which the beloved unknowingly sees himself in the lover."
textual_basis: The text states at 255d that the beloved does not know what he suffers and cannot express it, like one who has caught eye-inflammation from another without being able to name the cause, and that he unknowingly sees himself in the lover as in a mirror.
limits: The observation identifies the two analogies and their target phenomenon; it does not assess their relationship to the palinode's larger argument or claim interpretive significance.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The text uses direct second-person address to Eros with prayer vocabulary including requests for forgiveness and favor at 257a; the speech just delivered is explicitly called a palinode at 257a, marking formal completion of the recantation speech.
limits: The observation records the prayer as a textual speech-act closing the palinode; it does not characterize Socrates' sincerity or the religious status of the address.

```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0330
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 256e-257b
source_ref:

  stephanus_span: 256e-257b

  end_marker: 257b
  start_char: 59358
  end_char: 60546
  text_sha256: 9b4a76f5e34fada7af63924cefda2e20e57fc3d53e0efb4d31efa6d656f8a47f
greek_terms:
  - Λυσίαν
  - Πολέμαρχος
  - φιλοσοφίαν
english_gloss: Lysias and Polemarchus named; Polemarchus said to have turned to philosophy.
observation: At 257b Socrates names Lysias as the father of the speech and Polemarchus as his brother who has turned toward philosophy, praying that Lysias likewise be turned so that his lover no longer wavers.
textual_basis: Both Lysias and Polemarchus are identified by name with their familial relationship stated at 257b; Polemarchus is described as having turned to philosophy, and the prayer requests the same for Lysias.
limits: The observation records the naming and familial link; it does not claim knowledge from other dialogues about these figures, though Polemarchus recurs elsewhere in the Platonic corpus.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0332
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 258c-258e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 258c-258e
  start_marker: 258c

  start_char: 62627
  end_char: 63872
  text_sha256: 82a85df8a43855b56c9a1abafccf80be6f9317d56d42eebefdad30fbcff48d80
greek_terms:
  - Λυκούργου
  - Σόλωνος
  - Δαρείου
  - λογογράφος
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 258c Socrates names Lycurgus, Solon, and Darius as examples of powerful lawgivers, asking whether a speechwriter who obtained the power of any of them would be considered immortal and equal to a god both in life and after.
textual_basis: |
  The three named figures are introduced as comparanda for the status of a speechwriter (logographer). Socrates' question at 258c uses them to frame the social prestige of written law and speech, and Phaedrus assents ("very much so") to the suggestion that such a writer would be considered divine.
limits: |
  The observation records the naming and function of these three figures as argumentative examples at 258c; it does not claim anything about Plato's view of their historical roles.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0333
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 258c-258e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 258c-258e
  start_marker: 258c
  end_marker: 258e
  start_char: 62627
  end_char: 63872
  text_sha256: 82a85df8a43855b56c9a1abafccf80be6f9317d56d42eebefdad30fbcff48d80
greek_terms:
  - αἰσχρὸν
  - καλῶς
english_gloss: ""
observation: |
  At 258d Socrates refines the inquiry from whether writing speeches is shameful to whether writing badly is shameful, concluding that writing itself is not shameful but writing badly is, then asking what constitutes writing well versus badly.
textual_basis: |
  Socrates states at 258d that "it is clear to everyone that writing speeches is not itself shameful," then immediately adds that what is shameful is "speaking and writing not well but shamefully and badly," and proceeds to ask Phaedrus what the manner of writing well and not badly is. The question shifts from the fact of writing to the quality of writing.
limits: |
  The observation records the shift in the definiendum from the act of writing to the quality of writing at 258d; it does not characterize Socrates' subsequent answer or the broader writing critique later in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0335

source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 260c-260e
  start_marker: 260c
  end_marker: 260e
  start_char: 66661
  end_char: 67881
  text_sha256: fdd9b2cb8519aaa202ea4a3228ce4071b311409f2ddbbf540d6dc7216f57b815
greek_terms:
  - ῥητορικὴν
  - τέχνη
  - ἄτεχνος τριβή
english_gloss: Socrates compares rhetoric without knowledge of good and evil to praising a donkey's shadow as a horse, then personifies rhetoric defending itself, and reports hearing arguments that deny it is a true art.
observation: At 260c-d Socrates uses the analogy of someone praising a donkey's shadow as though it were a horse to describe rhetoric that persuades a city to pursue evil as good without knowing what is truly good or bad.
textual_basis: Socrates asks what harvest rhetoric would reap after sowing such persuasion, using the donkey-shadow-as-horse image at 260c and the question about the fruit of rhetoric at 260d, to which Phaedrus replies that it would not be fair.
limits: The analogy illustrates one failure mode of rhetoric; it does not settle whether rhetoric is always thus defective or only when practiced without knowledge.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_phaedrus_0336
source_work: Phaedrus
stephanus_span: 260c-260e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/phaedrus.txt
  stephanus_span: 260c-260e
  start_marker: 260c
  end_marker: 260e
  start_char: 66661
  end_char: 67881
  text_sha256: fdd9b2cb8519aaa202ea4a3228ce4071b311409f2ddbbf540d6dc7216f57b815
greek_terms:
  - ῥητορικὴ
  - τέχνη
english_gloss: Socrates personifies rhetoric as speaking in its own defense, claiming it does not compel the ignorant but advises first acquiring truth.
observation: At 260d Socrates personifies the art of rhetoric, giving it direct quoted speech to defend itself, claiming it does not force anyone ignorant of the truth to speak falsely but advises first acquiring truth and then coming to it.
textual_basis: Socrates introduces the personified voice with a quotation marker at 260d, and Phaedrus responds at 260e asking whether rhetoric speaks justly, treating the art as a respondent in the argument.
limits: The personification is a dramatic device within the argument; it does not establish that rhetoric's self-defense is correct, since Socrates immediately conditions acceptance on further testimony.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
greek_terms:
  - ἄτεχνος τριβή
  - τέχνη
  - Λάκων
english_gloss: Socrates reports hearing approaching arguments that testify rhetoric is not an art but an artless practice, and cites the Spartan saying that true art of speaking requires grasp of truth.
observation: At 260e Socrates says he seems to hear approaching arguments testifying that rhetoric is not a technē but an artless practice, and cites a Lacedaemonian saying that a true art of speaking cannot exist without grasping truth.
textual_basis: Socrates uses a hearing metaphor at 260e, reporting testimony from arguments he says are approaching, then attributes a saying to the Lacedaemonian about the relationship between speaking and truth.
limits: The approaching-arguments device is a rhetorical dramatization; it does not identify who the arguments are or provide an independent source for the Spartan saying.
review_status: rejected
```

```yaml
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
```

```yaml
textual_basis: The exact cited Greek interval explicitly supplies this attributed proposition.
limits: This observation records only the exact source attribution; it does not infer endorsement, truth, absence, counterevidence, or a general doctrine.
```
